Besides, I am confident that after ruling for one hundred years, Rama will gladly hand over the kingdom to Bharata. I have no doubts about Lord Rama's motives, because He serves me more carefully than He does His own mother. I consider Rama to be more glorious than my own son. What is the difference, whether Rama sits on the throne or Bharata? Rama treats all His brothers as His very self! "

Manthara's anguish became even more aggravated by hearing Queen Kausalya talk like this. She shouted, "Stupid! Rama will never hand over the kingdom to Bharata! He will give it to His own future son! In fact, after becoming the king, Rama will no doubt exile Bharata, or even kill him. With Bharata out of the picture, Rama will be able to rule without a rival."

"O Queen Kaikeyi, I am trying to bring you to your senses, but you take your co-wife's victory to be a cause of happiness! Can't you understand the simple fact that Queen Kausalya is your rival, and because of this, Bharata's future is at stake? Rama's mother hates you because you are young and beautiful, making the king more attached to you than to her. My advice is that you act quickly to somehow have Rama exiled to the forest so that Bharata can be installed upon the throne. "

It so happened that Queen Kaikeyi's heart gradually became poisoned by Manthara's hateful words. She had always cherished the idea of her son becoming the king, and deep down she resented her co-wife's seniority.

Her enviousness of Queen Kausalya aroused, her face flushing with anger, Queen Kaikeyi at last declared, "Manthara, you are right! I must somehow have Rama banished to the forest so that my own son can sit upon the throne. How can I do this? "

Manthara replied, "The means has already been given. If you have forgotten, I shall now remind you, provided you promise to act on it. "

Queen Kaikeyi solemnly promised to do whatever Manthara would recommend, and so the hunchback maidservant said, "O Queen, do you not remember the story you had once related to me? Long ago, Maharaja Dasaratha had fought on behalf of Indra against the asuras. When the demigods suffered defeat, your husband led an attack on the asuras' capital city, Vaijayanta. Although he fought valiantly, King Dasaratha was critically wounded and fell unconscious on the battlefield. You had accompanied the king on that occasion, and it was you who removed him from the battlefield and nursed him back to health. Out of a deep sense of gratitude, Maharaja Dasaratha offered you two benedictions, but you told him that you would ask for the favors later. "

"O Queen Kaikeyi, now is the time to utilize these two boons. With one benediction ask that your son become the king. With the other, demand that Rama be banished to the forest for fourteen years. Rama's absence will give Bharata a good opportunity to establish His popularity among the citizens. After doing this, his position as the rightful ruler of Ayodhya will remain secure. "

After revealing the means whereby Bharata could be installed upon the throne and Lord Rama banished to the forest, Manthara gave Kaikeyi some practical advice about the tactics that would help her get what she wanted.

Manthara said, "Co now to the sulking chamber. Throw off your ornaments and royal dress and put on old, dirty clothes. Then lie down on the bare floor, and when your husband comes to see you, make sure that you are crying like someone bitterly aggrieved. When the king speaks to you, remain silent at first, and do not worry. Maharaja Dasaratha is so attached to you that he cannot bear to see you unhappy. You must be confident that he will do anything to pacify you. "

"My dear Queen, the most important thing is this—You must never forget that the only thing you want is for your son to become the king. Don't give up your anger in exchange for gold or jewelry, or anything else. Simply remind the king of his promise to give you two benedictions. Then, when he promises to give you whatever you want, demand that Bharata be immediately installed upon the throne and Rama banished to the forest. "

Because her heart had come under the sway of malice and greed, Queen Kaikeyi was delighted with Manthara's plan. She assured her maidservant that she would follow her advice to the letter. Having become obsessed with the idea of making Bharata the emperor of the world, Queen Kaikeyi considered Manthara to be the cause of all good fortune.

Like a madwoman, she glorified the maidservant, saying, "My dear Manthara, although hunchbacks are generally sinful, you, on the other hand, are wise and honorable. To me, your so-called bodily deformity appears very beautiful. You stoop over like a lotus-flower, bending in the breeze, and thus your appearance is very charming. Your breasts are large and lovely, concealing your delicate navel as they bend low. Your hips are graceful and your thighs are smooth and shapely. Dressed in bright silk, you appear to sparkle, while the anklets that encircle your soft feet tinkle melodiously. The entire science of diplomacy must be residing within your hump, which is as large as the hub of a chariot wheel. "

"My dear Manthara, when Bharata is installed as the emperor of the world, I will garland that hump with a chain of pure gold. When Rama is exiled to the forest, I will smear your hump with sandalwood paste. O Manthara, I will reward you with exquisite dresses and jewelry, and I will make all the other hunchback women your maidservants so that they will massage your feet. "

Although flattered, Manthara interrupted Queen Kaikeyi, saying, "Remember, all this talk is useless unless we act quickly to stop the king from installing Rama. "

Manthara led Queen Kaikeyi to the sulking chamber. As they walked, she repeatedly fanned the queen's anger by saying, "Never forget what will be in store for Bharata if you do not succeed. You must be determined to either achieve your glorious end or else fast to death. "

Meanwhile, having completed all necessary preparations for Lord Rama's installation, Maharaja Dasaratha returned to his palace. Filled with longing for his youngest Queen, the king entered her bedroom and was surprised to find her absent. Thinking that Queen Kaikeyi must be nearby, he repeatedly called out her name. When there was no response, he became apprehensive.

At last, by inquiring from a servant, the king learned that Queen Kaikeyi had entered the sulking chamber. With an agitated heart, Maharaja Dasaratha hastened through the palace corridors. When he entered the room, he was shocked to see Queen Kaikeyi lying on the bare floor like an Apsara fallen from heaven, her jewelry and garlands scattered here and there.

The young and beautiful Queen Kaikeyi was dearer to Maharaja Dasaratha than his very life. When he saw her writhing on the floor, sobbing, the king felt highly alarmed and pained at heart.

Falling to his knees, Maharaja Dasaratha spoke as follows, while affectionately stroking Kaikeyi's face, "What is the matter, my dear one? Tell me the cause of your grief and rest assured that I will do anything to rectify it. Is it that I have been too busy, and have failed to pay proper attention to you? Did you have an argument with one of your co-wives? Has someone insulted you? "

"My dearest one, I am fully under your control. I would never dare do anything displeasing to you. Speak freely and tell me what is on your mind. O most precious one, you know that I love you more than life itself. Let me dispel your sorrow, as the rising sun evaporates the morning mist. "

Cupid's arrows had pierced Maharaja Dasaratha's heart, and thus he was a slave to the urges of passion. After hearing her husband's words, Queen Kaikeyi felt confident that her goal would be achieved. There was a brief silence, and then she replied, "No one has insulted me or shown me any disrespect. My lord, I will tell you what is on my mind, but first you must give me the complete assurance that you will accomplish whatever it is I desire.”

Maharaja Dasaratha placed Queen Kaikeyi's head upon his lap. While tenderly smoothing her disheveled hair, he replied, "My darling, you know that I love no one more than you, except Rama. I swear that I will do whatever it is you may order. Feel free to tell me your desire, and rest assured that I will fulfill it without fail. My dear one, you know that I will always give you everything you want. Please tell me what is troubling you. "

After hearing this triple assurance, Kaikeyi felt blissfully sure that her husband was completely under her thumb. Urged on by an inflamed desire, born of partiality toward her son, the young queen made the following terrible proclamation, which was as grim as death itself, "May the twelve Adityas, the eleven Rudras, the eight Vasus and twin Asvins bear witness! May the sun and the moon, day and night, and the four directions also bear witness, as well as the Gandharvas, Raksasas, pitrs, Bhutas, Pretas and all other living creatures. My illustrious lord, the follower of dharma and adherent to truth has promised to fulfill my desire. "

"My dear husband, remember how once you were critically wounded and left for dead while fighting with the asuras? I nursed you back to life and so you gratefully promised to reward me with two benedictions. O noble king, you must grant me these two boons today without fail—otherwise, I will give up my life out of grief. Let Bharata be installed as the ruler of Kosala, utilizing the very preparations you have made for Rama. This is the first benediction I desire, and the second is this, "May Rama depart for the Dandaka forest this very day and live in exile there for fourteen years, wearing only tree-bark and deerskin."

When Maharaja Dasaratha heard Queen Kaikeyi's ultimatums, he became practically stunned with bewilderment. He wondered, "What is happening to me? Am I dreaming of vivid mental pictures from a past life? Maybe I have gone completely mad!

Becoming overwhelmed, the king suddenly fainted to the floor. After a brief moment, he regained consciousness, but finding the situation too painful, he swooned once again. It was only after a long time that he again came to his senses, and as he summoned his courage, a burning anger swelled within his heart.

With great agitation, Maharaja Dasaratha rebuked Queen Kaikeyi, saying, "You vicious woman! What have I done to deserve this? What offense did Rama commit against you? Rama loves you as much as He loves His own mother. Why are you bent upon harming Him? Everyone loves Rama as much as life itself, and I could never bear to live without Him. I could abandon my other two wives if need be, but I could never forsake Rama! "

After venting his rage, Dasaratha's mood suddenly changed. Touching his head to Kaikeyi's feet, the king pleaded, "My dear beautiful queen, please give up your terrible determination. Rama is the embodiment of all virtues—He is kind, forgiving, truthful, gentle, fixed in His duty, and everyone’s well-wisher. Rama has always served you just as lovingly as your own son, Bharata, if not more. If you insist, I will install Bharata upon the throne, but I could never think of banishing Rama to the forest. How can you even consider harming Rama? He is faultless and has never so much as uttered an unkind word to you. "

"My Dearly beloved, have pity upon me. Ask for any other benediction. I am ready to give you the entire earth, but not this. If I were to banish Rama, it would certainly be the death of me. Please spare me from this most cruel and irreligious act. "

As he spoke, Maharaja Dasaratha was writhing in agony and wailing piteously. Nonetheless, Queen Kaikeyi was unmoved. Remaining fixed in her determination, she replied, "My dear King, How can you dare lecture me about dharma, when you are trying to avoid giving the benedictions you had solemnly promised? Think about King Sibi, who gave his own flesh to a hawk so that he could fulfill his promise of protection to a pigeon. If you fail to keep your word, you will stain the spotless reputation of the glorious kings who appeared in your dynasty. My dear husband, whatever the circumstances may be, you gave me your promise and now you must keep it! "

As she spoke, Queen Kaikeyi became more and more determined to get her way. Being overwhelmed by anger and greed, she had given up all sense of shame and decency. After a brief pause, Queen Kaikeyi continued, “O King, I know your real motive! After installing Rama upon the throne, you will gradually push Bharata and me out of the picture so that you can enjoy life with Queen Kausalya without impediment. However, know this for certain—if you do not keep your promise, I will drink poison and die this very day! "

Queen Kaikeyi remained silent while King Dasaratha continued to moan in agony, staring at his queen with unblinking eyes. Finally, the king lost his equilibrium and fell unconscious to the floor, while uttering, "Rama, Rama."

Then after some time, Maharaja Dasaratha regained a little composure and said, "You misguided woman! From whom did you learn this shameless wickedness? Don’t you realize that even if Bharata is installed upon the throne, He will never accept the kingdom without Rama? What are the people going to think about me if you force me to perform this ignoble act? Everyone will ridicule me, saying, 'There goes the king who lusted after his wife so much that he exiled his beloved son to the forest.' Kaikeyi, do you really believe that you will find any happiness under such circumstances? "

Pausing, Maharaja Dasaratha began to contemplate how Lord Rama, who had always lived in royal comfort, would be able to endure a harsh forest life. Becoming more and more pained and angry at the thought, the king suddenly mocked, “O righteous Queen, I wish you all success in ruling the kingdom after ridding yourself of all your relatives. Do not expect me to play any part in your sinister plan! I will never grant your wishes. Never! "

In the ensuing silence, a feeling of hopelessness began to swell within Maharaja Dasaratha's heart. He then reached out to submissively touch Kaikeyi's feet. Wailing in agony, the King pleaded, "O Queen, please give up your wicked intentions,” and then fainted once again. Queen Kaikeyi responded by chastising her husband with sharp words, again and again, and in this way the king remained unconscious for about an hour.

After coming to his senses, Maharaja Dasaratha once again rebuked Queen Kaikeyi, saying, "How condemned women are, for they are perverse, cruel and selfish by nature! Maybe not all women, but at least you, O mother of Bharata. Your demands are evil and malicious and so I will never grant them! You can scream and shout, you can take poison or threaten to do whatever you like, but I will never banish Rama to the forest! "

The sun went down, and Maharaja Dasaratha spent the entire night wailing with grief. Sometimes the king would join his hands in supplication and beg, "O lovely-limbed lady, please be kind to me. I am growing old and do not expect to live much longer. Have pity upon me and be merciful to Rama, to our preceptors, to the citizens, and Bharata as well. O lovely-eyed one, please be kind to me."

Maharaja Dasaratha's eyes were red from weeping, and his voice was very pitiful to hear. Still, Queen Kaikeyi simply turned her head away and remained silent. Sometimes the king would chastise his queen in a threatening voice, and sometimes he tried to coax her with argument and reason. All the while, he prayed that the night would never end, so that he would not have to face the ordeal of banishing Lord Rama to the forest the next day.

At last, when he realized that Queen Kaikeyi was unwavering in her determination, Maharaja Dasaratha once again fainted out of hopelessness. He awoke the next morning when the bards came to his door, singing his praises. Feeling disgust, the king ordered the singers and musicians to go away.

When she saw her husband awaken, Queen Kaikeyi resumed her relentless talk, "What is the matter, my lord? Is it wrong for you to grant me the two benedictions you had previously promised? Those who know dharma (religious principles) have declared that truth is the highest virtue. In fact, truth is the very root of dharma, and truth is its ultimate goal. O King, abide by truth, if you actually seek the fruit of righteousness. Grant me my wishes! "

Maharaja Dasaratha angrily replied, "Wicked woman, I hereby disown you! You are no longer my wife! "

Queen Kaikeyi retorted, "My lord, why are you so agitated? Summon Rama quietly and then do your duty by telling him that you are installing Bharata as the inheritor of the throne. "

Again and again Queen Kaikeyi pressed her demands upon Maharaja Dasaratha, as if she were repeatedly whipping a horse. At last the king called for Lord Rama, just at the time when Vasistha Muni and his disciples were entering Ayodhya, after having collected the necessary paraphernalia for the installation ceremony.

When Vasistha Muni arrived at Maharaja Dasaratha's palace, he met Sumantra and told him, "Please inform the king of my arrival. Also, tell him that everyone is eagerly waiting for Rama's coronation to begin, and that the necessary preparations are completed. "

Sumantra went to Maharaja Dasaratha's room, and while standing at the door, he first prope rly glorified the king. Then he related Vasistha’s message and implored the king to come out and greet his family priest. However, Sumantra's words only inflamed Maharaja Dasaratha's grief. The King sharply replied, "Co away! Your words of praise are not welcome! "

Finding the king to be in such an irritable mood, Sumantra retreated a few steps. Diplomatic Queen Kaikeyi then said, "The king was so excited about Rama's installation ceremony that he hardly slept at all last night. Being thoroughly exhausted, he had just managed to get a little rest and so he became annoyed when you disturbed him. Bring Rama here, and then Maharaja Dasaratha will get up and the ceremonies can begin. "

Sumantra said, "Unless I hear the king's voice, I cannot execute any order."

Maharaja Dasaratha called out, "I am not asleep. I have already summoned Rama. Why has my order not been executed? Bring Rama here at once!”

Sumantra quickly departed, thinking the king to be very eager to perform the coronation ceremony, but the brahmanas were becoming restless, because the sacrificial arena was ready and time was passing, making Maharaja Dasaratha conspicuous by his absence.

When Sumantra reached the palace gates, the guards escorted him into the presence of Sitadevi and Lord Rama. Sumantra informed Lord Rama that his father had summoned Him. At this time Lord Rama remarked to Sitadevi, "My father and mother Kaikeyi must be calling Me, being impatient to begin the installation ceremony."

When Lord Rama departed, He saw Laksmana standing at the gate with folded hands. Lord Rama took His brother with Him, mounted the waiting chariot and proceeded through the crowded streets of Ayodhya. The natural characteristic of Lord Rama is that whoever happens to see Him cannot bear to turn his eyes away from that vision. After coming to the inner gates of his father's palace, Lord Rama got down from the chariot and dismissed the citizens that had followed Him.

Lord Rama entered His father's room alone. There He saw Maharaja Dasaratha seated upon the couch, along with mother Kaikeyi, his face displaying prominent signs of dejection and anxiety.

As Lord Rama bowed down to Maharaja Dasaratha, and then mother Kaikeyi, the king could only utter, "Rama, Rama, " as his eyes filled with tears.

Actually, Maharaja Dasaratha could not bear to look directly at his son and so Lord Rama became very apprehensive upon seeing him like this. He wondered, "What has made my father so dispirited, just like a saintly person who has performed an abominable activity? Formerly, even when irritable, My father would become very pleased as soon as I came before him. Why is he not happy to see Me today? "

Lord Rama asked mother Kaikeyi, "What is the cause of My father's distress? Have I unwittingly done something to offend him? "

Queen Kaikeyi had become thoroughly callous because of her sinful behavior. She impudently replied, "The king in not angry, nor is he offended or aggrieved. He simply has something to tell You, but is afraid to do so, thinking that it will hurt You. Formerly, your father had offered me two benedictions after I had saved him from a great danger on the battlefield. Now, due to unmanly weakness, he hesitates to fulfill his promise. "

"My dear Rama, You should protect Your father's virtue by convincing him to not fall prey to falsity. If You promise that You are prepared to carry out the king's order, I will tell You the two benedictions I desire. "

Feeling deeply hurt, Lord Rama replied, "My dear mother, I am very surprised that there is any doubt in your mind that I would unflinchingly carry out My father's wishes, even if it meant entering fire. Please speak freely about what My father wants, and rest assured that I will act accordingly. This is my solemn promise. "

Being thus assured by Lord Rama, cruel Queen Kaikeyi said, "Long ago, when Your father was severely wounded while fighting with the demon Sambara, I carefully nursed him back to life. Out of a sense of deep gratitude, the king begged me to accept two benedictions. At that time I told my husband that I would utilize these two boons later on, when I needed them. Now, I wish to accept these two benedictions and here is what I desire. "

"First I asked Your father to install Bharata as the next king of Kosala. Second, I asked the king to banish You to the forest for fourteen years, where You will have to live as an ascetic, having matted hair and a garment of tree-bark and deerskin. Rama, it is Your duty to insure that Your father does not go back on his word and thus tread the path of unrighteousness. "

Although mother Kaikeyi had spoken so maliciously, Lord Rama was not the least agitated or aggrieved. Without displaying even the slightest displeasure, He cheerfully replied, "My dear mother, I shall immediately go to live in the forest as you desire. Let messengers be sent at once to summon Bharata. I have no regrets. My only wish is that My father may always look upon Me favorably. Please do not harbor any ill feeling toward Me."

Hearing this, Queen Kaikeyi exclaimed with delight, "It is settled! Send messengers to my brother's house so that my son may come as quickly as possible. As for You, Rama, I urge You to leave without delay. Do not worry about Your father. For the moment he is bewildered, but after Your departure, he will regain his normal composure. "

Lord Rama said, "My dear mother, it grieves Me that My father himself has not ordered Me to go into exile. Of course, I am quite ready to leave for the forest at once in obedience to your command alone. "

Queen Kaikeyi said, "Your father is too mortified to speak, because You are his pet son. In fact, until You depart, I do not think that he will even be able to bathe or eat his breakfast. "

While listening to all this, Maharaja Dasaratha could only moan, "Oh, how painful, " and "What a shame. " When Queen Kaikeyi finished speaking, the king lost consciousness and fell to the floor.

As Lord Rama picked up His father He told mother Kaikeyi, "I do not want to live in this world as a slave to greed and passion. Like the rishis, I am devoted to righteousness. I would never give up the path of virtue merely for the sake of ruling a kingdom. My dear mother, there was no need for you to trouble My father. If you had asked Me directly, I would have unhesitatingly given you the kingdom and retired to the forest. "

After saying this, Lord Rama bowed to Maharaja Dasaratha and mother Kaikeyi and then left to break the news to His mother. Laksmana had been listening outside the door, and it was with great effort that He controlled his rage as He followed His elder brother. As the two left the palace, sorrowful wailing could be heard from those who had learned about Lord Rama's impending exile. When Maharaja Dasaratha heard the crying, he hid himself under the bed sheets, out of shame.

As Lord Rama entered mother Kausalya's apartment, the palace ladies joyfully greeted Him and then hurried to inform His mother of His arrival. When Lord Rama entered mother Kausalya's room, she was worshiping Lord Visnu, but she quickly got up and embraced Him. Kausalyadevi said, "My dear Rama, I am so happy that You are going to succeed my husband as the king of Kosala this very day. "

With gentleness and humility, Lord Rama said, "My dear mother, please do not become too upset at what I must tell you. As the result of two benedictions My father had formerly promised mother Kaikeyi, Bharata will be installed upon the royal throne, and today I will leave Ayodhya to go live in the forest for fourteen years. "

As soon as she heard this, Kausalyadevi fainted and fell to the floor. Lord Rama gently lifted His mother to her feet. Then, as she came to her senses, Kausalyadevi bitterly lamented, "What greater misery could happen to me than this? It would have been better for me to remain childless! Why did death not come instantly to take me, as soon as I heard of this calamity? Now, my younger co-wives will surely despise me, and by hearing their taunts, my life will become doubly miserable. "

Laksmana had been controlling His rage, but after hearing this, He could remain silent no longer. Laksmana vented His anger, saying, "Our father has become senile and perverse, being overpowered by lust. He has become a plaything in the hands of wicked mother Kaikeyi! Why should We stand by passively and accept all this? A father, a king, or a spiritual master who has lost sight of what should be done and what should not be done must be rejected.”

"O Lord Rama, You should take control of the kingdom immediately, before everyone hears about the king's decision to install Bharata. Even if all the ministers and people already know of this and are against You, I am prepared to depopulate Ayodhya if necessary. "

Turning to mother Kausalyadevi, Laksmana said, "My dear mother, you should know that I am loyal to Lord Rama alone. I am ready to dethrone Dasaratha if it is necessary to install Lord Rama upon the throne! "

Kausalyadevi did not care for the kingdom, however. She only feared separation from her beloved son. She said, "Rama, please do not go into exile. It would be more virtuous for You to remain here to please Your poor mother than go to the forest at the command of Your father. If You go, I will fast to death, because life would be unbearable without You. "

Lord Rama said, "My dear mother, it is not possible for Me to disobey My father. Therefore, you must give Me permission so that I can leave at once. The order of one's father can never be avoided. Just consider how Parasurama beheaded his own mother, Renuka, at the command of his father, Jamadagni. "

Turning to Laksmana, Lord Rama said, "My dear brother, do not lose all good sense by becoming a victim of anger. Give up the idea of seizing the throne by force and accept the order of Our father. "

"Dear mother, do not torture yourself and speak about ending your life. After My term of exile is over, I will come back to you. Please give Me your blessings so that I can depart without remorse. "

Kausalyadevi was sobbing piteously, for she could not bear to grant her son permission to leave. This was very painful for Lord Rama and so He became sterner and told Laksmana, "Both of you are simply causing Me grief by opposing My desire to go to the forest in accordance with My father's command. To obey My father is the only righteous path for Me, and I will never be deterred from following it. "

After a short pause, Lord Rama spoke again, to try and pacify Laksmana, "My dear brother, don't you see that the sooner Bharata is installed upon the throne and all of this is forgotten, the sooner You will be able to give up Your grief? Do not blame mother Kaikeyi, for she is simply an instrument in the hands of the Supreme Controller, who is the real shaper of our destinies. "

Laksmana was not to be easily pacified, however. He angrily replied, "O Lord Rama, it appears that You have become impotent! You have given up Your ksatriya spirit of reliance upon personal prowess and have surrendered to Destiny! I am not such a eunuch! I will subdue Fate by My own power and then install You upon the throne this very day! "

Lord Rama knew that this harsh criticism was actually a manifestation of Laksmana's love for Him. While wiping the tears from His brother's eyes, He spoke in an attempt to comfort Him. Meanwhile, mother Kausalyadevi finally understood that nothing would deter Lord Rama, and so she pleaded, "My dear son, if You are determined to follow Your father's order, then let me accompany You to the forest.

Lord Rama replied, "My dear mother, after being deceived by mother Kaikeyi and abandoned by you, Maharaja Dasaratha would certainly give up his life. It is the foremost duty of a chaste and virtuous wife not to leave her husband under any circumstance. A woman should never even think of leaving her husband, because he is her lord for as long as she lives. No matter how noble or religious a woman may be, if she fails to serve her husband, she commits sin. This is the verdict of all sruti and smrti—the revealed and remembered scriptures. "

"My dear mother, remain with My father and wait for Me, for I shall return after fourteen years without fail."

Hearing this, Kausalyadevi gave up all hope of accompanying Lord Rama, and so at last she gave Him her blessings, mother Kausalya said, “May You soon return, so that my happiness will once again be restored! May righteousness, which You so highly cherish, be Your protector! May the gods in heaven protect You, and may the weapons that You received from Visvamitra defend You! May heaven and earth, the air, the land and the water, as well as all moving and non-moving beings, protect You! May daytime and night, as well as the sun and the moon, give You protection! May You be happy, Rama, for my blessings go with You! "

Kausalyadevi smelled Lord Rama's head with great affection and embraced Him. In turn, Lord Rama touched His mother's feet, again and again. Then, without hesitation, Lord Rama swiftly departed for His palace to see Sitadevi. Sitadevi knew nothing of this intrigue, and she had been waiting for Lord Rama in happy expectation for many hours. However, when Lord Rama finally entered her room, Sitadevi saw that He was pale, perspiring, greatly troubled, and not accompanied by His usual entourage. Sitadevi hurriedly got up and asked, "My dear Lord, what is the matter?

Without hesitation, Lord Rama replied, "My father has ordered Me to leave Ayodhya and go live in the forest. Instead of Me, Bharata will be installed upon the royal throne. Long ago, My father had promised to fulfill mother Kaikeyi's two wishes after she had nursed him back to health as he lay wounded upon the battlefield. At that time Kaikeyi told her husband that she would utilize the two benedictions at a later date. Now she is asking that her son become the next ruler, and that I must be banished to the forest. "

"My dear Sita, I have no choice but to abide by My father's order, but I want you to remain here. Co every day and offer your respects at My father's feet, and follow Bharata's commands implicitly. My dearest one, I have come here just to see you before going to the forest. "

Sitadevi became very agitated while listening to Lord Rama. With anger born of love, she said, "O Lord, my husband, most excellent of men, what advice are You giving me? It is the duty of a wife to share her husband's destiny, and so I will go with You to the forest. A father, mother, brother, son and daughter-in-law all have separate identities, but a wife has no choice but to follow her husband. "

"O Lord, I cannot find any refuge in my father, mother, friends or relatives, or even myself. My husband is my only shelter and so I am determined to follow You. I will walk behind You, and I will eat only after You have eaten. I will never do anything to displease You, and I shall remain happy by gazing at the hills, lakes and rivers. I will never become tired, because I will feel secure in Your presence. I would not want to live in heaven without You. O Lord, it is You alone I love. Without You, I would surely die. "

Because of Sitadevi's frailty, Lord Rama was reluctant to take her with Him. Attempting to discourage her, He said, "Delicate lady, one has to endure great discomfort while living in the forest. There are no soft beds to lie upon, and one has to bear the onslaught of excessive heat, cold, and torrential rain. In the forest, one can only hope to eat fruit and roots. There is always danger because of the ferocious animals that wander about in search of prey. Considering all this, I could never allow you to accompany Me to the forest. "

Lord Rama's words of refusal made Sitadevi tremble with fear. As pearl-like tears rolled down her cheeks like drops of water falling from a lotus leaf, Sitadevi said, "As long as I remain with You, all these inconveniences and dangers would be of no consequence."

"My dear Lord, without her husband, a woman cannot live. If You do not allow me to go with You, I will give up my life by taking poison, entering a fire, or drowning myself. Before my marriage, an astrologer predicted that I would have to spend part of my life in the forest. Because of this, I know that it is my destiny to accompany You. To be with You is heaven and to live without You is hell. Please—You must take me with You! "

Even after hearing this heartfelt appeal, Lord Rama was unwilling to allow Sitadevi to share His forced exile. Because of this, Sitadevi felt as if she were falling into a great abyss of calamity. Overwhelmed with a terrible fear of being separated from her husband, she became almost mad.

Unable to tolerate Lord Rama's stubbornness any longer, Sitadevi chastised Him for His callousness, saying, "My father made a big mistake by choosing You for his son-in-law! Are you going to desert Your chaste wife, who was married to You before her puberty? Are You going to leave her unprotected and exposed to the stares of others? The citizens of Ayodhya are fools for declaring, 'Lord Rama is as splendorous as the sun, for His glories are without equal! ' O Lord, I refuse to remain here alone, while You go off into the forest! Either You will take me with You or I will die by drinking poison! "

Crying bitterly, her heart tormented by fear, Sitadevi was on the verge of fainting. Seeing his darling wife like this was more than Lord Rama could bear and so His heart finally softened. Taking Sitadevi in His arms, Lord Rama assured her that she could accompany Him.

He said, "My darling, I only tried to dissuade you because I did not know the real situation of your mind. My dear Sita, I could no more abandon you than a wise man could give up his sense of compassion. How could I ever rest peacefully, knowing that you were unhappy? Co now and give the brahmanas all your valuable clothes and jewels, and whatever else you possess."

Sitadevi joyfully departed, and then Laksmana, who had been standing nearby, came and caught hold of Lord Rama's lotus feet. With tears in his eyes, Laksmana pleaded, "O Lord Rama, let me also go with You to the forest.

Lord Rama replied, "My dear Laksmana, You must remain here to serve Our two mothers, for without Your protection, mother Kaikeyi will surely mistreat them. "

Laksmana said, "Bharata will take care of Our mothers, so there is no need to worry about them. O Lord Rama, as You walk through the forest, I will stay in front with My bow in hand so that You and Sitadevi can enjoy the scenery without distraction. I will gather fruit and vegetables, and when You sleep, I will stand nearby to guard You. "

At last Lord Rama cheerfully said, "Alright, You can come with Me, but first go and say goodbye to Your mother. Then bring the two mighty bows that Varuna had given to Maharaja Janaka, as well as the impenetrable armor, two inexhaustible quivers, and a pair of the best swords. After offering respect to Vasistha Muni, return here with the weapons and We shall depart. "

Thereafter, when Laksmana returned, Lord Rama summoned the foremost of brahmanas so that He could give away all His possessions in charity. Before long, Suyajna, the son of Vasistha; the sons of Agastya and Visvamitra; and many other brahmanas arrived there.

There was a brahmana named Trijata who lived in a forest near Ayodhya and was very weak and frail because of poverty. One day, Trijata's wife asked him to go to Ayodhya and beg for some charity from Lord Rama. It so happened that Trijata arrived just at the time when Lord Rama was distributing His entire wealth. When Trijata approached Him and described his impoverished condition, Lord Rama jokingly said, "Take your staff and throw it as far as you can. As many cows as that stick passes over, I will give you. "

Trijata tightened his belt and threw his staff into the air with great enthusiasm. Much to everyone's surprise, it soared all the way across the Sarayu and landed amongst a herd of thousands of cows grazing by the riverside. Lord Rama gave Trijata all the cows that were situated from the bank of the river up to the point where his staff had landed. He then embraced the brahmana and said, "Please do not be offended by My jest. I simply wanted to see you exhibit your exceptional brahminical prowess. "

Thereafter, Lord Rama gave away everything in His possession, so that no brahmana, relative, dependent, or even beggar failed to receive charity that day. Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana then proceeded on foot through the streets of Ayodhya to the palace of Maharaja Dasaratha. The people who lined the streets, as well as those who gazed from their balconies, were very pained to see this.

Addressing one another, the citizens exclaimed, "What a great misfortune! Even common men are now able to see Sitadevi, who previously had never appeared in public. We will take our families and accompany Lord Rama, so that Ayodhya will become desolate, like an uninhabited forest, and the forest will become populous, like a great city.”

When Lord Rama entered mother Kaikeyi's quarters, He saw Sumantra and asked him to announce His arrival to the king. Then, when Lord Rama entered the room, followed by Sitadevi and Laksmana, Maharaja Dasaratha quickly got up and ran to embrace Him. Becoming too saddened, though, the king fainted onto the floor before he could reach his beloved son. Lord Rama hurriedly picked up His father, and all around could be heard cries of anguish. After being brought back to his couch, Maharaja Dasaratha regained consciousness.

Lord Rama said, "My dear father, I have come to bid you farewell. Please give Me permission to leave for the forest, along with Sita and Laksmana. I tried to dissuade them from following Me, but they could not consider living without Me."

Maharaja Dasaratha said, "My dear Rama, take my advice. Imprison me at once and sit on the royal throne by force. Who will stop You? "

Lord Rama said, "My dear father, I have no ambition to rule the earth or attain any kind of royal opulence."

The King said, "Alright, my son. You have my permission to depart for the forest, but just stay here one more day so that I can have this last opportunity to see You."

Lord Rama replied, "No, My dear father. It is My duty to fulfill your promise and so I will do as mother Kaikeyi orders by leaving for the forest at once. "

Maharaja Dasaratha had embraced Lord Rama, but when he heard this, he once again lost consciousness. Sumantra had been weeping, and he also fell to the floor while everyone, except Queen Kaikeyi, cried out loudly due to grief. Sumantra was the most intimate associate of the royal family, and so he suffered more than anyone.

Getting up, Sumantra approached Queen Kaikeyi and chastised her, saying, "Wicked woman! By sending away Lord Rama, you will become the murderer of your husband. Your insistence that Bharata be installed upon the throne violates the religious principle that a kingdom should be handed over to one's eldest son. Rest assured that you will receive the bitter fruit of your evil deeds, for when Lord Rama leaves Ayodhya, all the brahmanas will follow Him. As a result, the kingdom will become devoid of all auspiciousness. "

Sumantra then told Queen Kaikeyi a story, "Once, long ago, your father, Maharaja Kekaya, received a sage's benediction that enabled him to understand all languages, even those of animals. The sage made one condition though. If King Kekaya ever disclosed the meaning of an animal's speech, he would die. "

Once, later on, the king heard the shrill cry of a bird. Understanding its meaning, he began to laugh. Your mother, the queen, suspected that her husband was laughing at her and so she asked him to disclose what he found so amusing. The king told her about the sage's benediction and also warned her that he would die if he disclosed the bird's meaning. The foolish queen was stubborn and jealous, however, and so she replied, 'You can live or die, I don't care, but I insist that you tell me why you were laughing. '"

"The mortified king went to the sage who had given him the benediction and explained his dilemma. The sage said, 'Do not disclose the bird's meaning to your queen. Rather, rebuke her for her impudence. 'Taking the sage's advice, Maharaja Kekaya gathered his courage, and after chastising his wife severely, he lived happily thereafter.”

Sumantra said, "In the same way, O Queen, you are acting very impudently toward your husband. Admit your fault and give up your wicked plan, so that Maharaja Dasaratha can go ahead and install Lord Rama upon the throne."

Still, Queen Kaikeyi was unmoved. Considering Lord Rama's exile to be inevitable, Maharaja Dasaratha ordered Sumantra, "Get ready one aksauhini of soldiers, and organize many merchants, hunters, servants, and the royal treasurer, so that they can accompany Lord Rama into exile. Although He will reside in the forest, my beloved son does not have to be deprived of the objects of comfort and enjoyment.

When she heard this, Queen Kaikeyi became fearful. With a choked voice, she exclaimed, "Bharata will not accept the kingdom if you strip it of all its resources before his installation! "

Maharaja Dasaratha sharply replied, "I have already agreed to install Bharata upon the throne and exile Rama to the forest. Why are you trying to punish me further? If you did not want Lord Rama to take anything with Him into exile, why did you not mention this at the time of accepting benedictions?"

Queen Kaikeyi snapped, "In our dynasty there is the example of how King Sagara exiled his eldest son, Asamanjasa. You should cast off Rama in the same way, without providing Him any comfort."

An elderly minister named Siddhartha could not tolerate this, and so he said with indignation, "Asamanjasa took delight in drowning his playmates in the River Sarayu. When the citizens complained to King Sagara, he rejected his cruel son. What sin has Lord Rama committed? How can you dare make this comparison between Lord Rama and Asamanjasa?"

Although Maharaja Dasaratha was becoming very weak, he managed to raise himself up. He vowed, "O Kaikeyi, if you will not let Rama go to the forest in comfort, I will take all the citizens with me and accompany Rama myself!"

Lord Rama said, "My dear father, please do not become so upset. I have no use for the royal comforts you desire for Me. Let mother Kaikeyi's maidservants bring tree-bark at once, so that I can wear suitable clothes for My forest life."

Upon hearing this, shameless Queen Kaikeyi personally went and brought clothing made from tree-bark, and asked Lord Rama to put it on. Without hesitation, Lord Rama and Laksmana obediently took off Their royal garments and put on the clothes mother Kaikeyi gave Them. However, Sitadevi felt very unhappy when she took from mother Kaikeyi her dress made of kusa grass and tree-bark, and her eyes filled with tears. Then, as she tried to put on the forest dress, it kept slipping from its proper position, because she was not at all used to it. Lord Rama took that tree-bark dress and began draping it over Sitadevi's silk sari This was unbearable for Vasistha Muni.

With tears in his eyes, he restrained Lord Rama while chastising Queen Kaikeyi with strong words, "You wretched woman! Don't you see that everyone is prepared to go with Lord Rama? I am also quite sure that Bharata and Satrughna will follow Him, so that you will be left alone to rule the kingdom. At least Sitadevi should be allowed to go to the forest with a comfortable dress, while being carried upon some kind of conveyance."

Sitadevi wanted to follow her husband's example, and so she kept the forest clothes on. When they saw Sitadevi dressed in tree- bark, the palace residents condemned Maharaja Dasaratha with strong words, and while hearing this talk the king lost all interest in maintaining his life. For about an hour he remained delirious, while mumbling words of self-reproach.

When Maharaja Dasaratha at last regained some composure, he ordered Sumantra to bring a chariot to carry Lord Rama to the forest. At this time mother Kausalya embraced Sitadevi and gave her the following womanly advice, "It is well-known that during times of misfortune some women desert even a righteous and loving husband. These women forget all past favors, for there is nothing that can bind them. Because of ingratitude, such women cannot be controlled by wisdom, by gifts, or even by marriage. On the other hand, there are women who are gentle, truthful and obedient by nature, and they honor their husbands above all."

"O Sita, please do not despise my son because of His exile. Let Him always remain your worshipful Lord in all circumstances."

Sitadevi accepted this advice with folded hands and replied, "O mother I know very well my duty toward my husband, and so please do not speak to me about faithless women. My attachment to my Lord is like the moonlight to the moon. Just as there in no vina without strings or chariot without wheels, so there is no future for a wife without a husband, even though she may have one thousand sons. My husband is my Lord and He shall always be so. How could I despise Him?"

Kausalyadevi began to weep while thinking of the impending separation from her son, and so Lord Rama assured her that the time would quickly pass. Lord Rama went and clasped His father's feet, and then circumambulated him, along with Sitadevi and Laksmana. After this, Lord Rama bowed one last time to His mother, while Laksmana did the same to His mother, Sumitradevi, who affectionately smelled His head in return.

Just then Sumantra came there, and at his behest Lord Rama mounted the chariot, followed by Sitadevi and Laksmana. Without delay, they departed for the forest.

As Lord Rama passed through the streets of Ayodhya, all the citizens came out to see Him. While clinging to the chariot, they begged Sumantra, "Please drive slowly so that we can have one long last look at Lord Rama."

While glancing backward, Lord Rama saw His father running through the crowd, desperately calling for Sumantra to stop the chariot. Being duty bound, however, Lord Rama commanded the driver to go more quickly. These conflicting orders put Sumantra in a dilemma, so that he became confused as to what he should do.

Seeing this, Lord Rama shouted, "Do not prolong the agony of seeing My aged father like this!" and so Sumantra finally urged the horses on with determination. One of the ministers then restrained Maharaja Dasaratha, saying, "O King, if a person wants someone to return, he should not accompany him very far at the time of his departure."

As long as the dust raised by Lord Rama's chariot could be seen, Maharaja Dasaratha kept his gaze fixed upon it. Finally, after losing sight of the departing chariot, the king fainted onto the ground. When Kausalyadevi and even Kaikeyi ran to pick him up, Maharaja Dasaratha forbade the younger queen, saying, "Don't touch me! I have disowned you, and so I no longer want to see you!"

After lifting up her husband, as Kausalyadevi escorted him back to the palace, she noticed that the shops were closed, and gloom pervaded the faces of all the citizens. When he entered his palace, Maharaja Dasaratha told the servants, "Please take me to mother Kausalya's rooms. From now on, I will only remain there."

The king passed the night with great difficulty, and around midnight he said to mother Kausalyadevi, "Please touch me, for my sight has not returned after going with Rama."

Kausalyadevi was heartbroken, seeing the piteous condition of her husband and worrying about Lord Rama. Trying to pacify her, Sumitradevi said, "You should not worry about your son. Because Sitadevi and Laksmana are with Him, Lord Rama will not experience even the slightest difficulty. Dear Kausalya, Lord Rama is not an ordinary human being. He is the Cod of gods, the illuminator of the sun, and the controller of eternal time. While Lord Rama resides in the forest, the earth herself and all natural phenomena will engage in His devotional service. Rest assured that you will one day see the return of your beloved son. Lamenting will serve no purpose. Just see how the citizens have practically become inactive due to grief. O Queen, you will have to pacify them and so you should not allow yourself to become too disturbed."

Meanwhile, the people of Ayodhya had followed Lord Rama's chariot into the forest, and they were begging Him to turn back. Lord Rama told them, "I cannot break My vow, and so you should give Bharata the love you have for Me."

There were some elderly brahmanas who could not walk fast enough to keep up with the others. In great distress, they called out to the horses, begging them not to proceed further. Hearing these anxious pleas, Lord Rama felt compassion and got down from the chariot. Along with Sitadevi and Laksmana, He then proceeded on foot, so that these elderly brahmanas could catch up with Him. Still, Lord Rama would not turn His face backwards, for He was determined to perfectly keep His promise.

In this way they finally reached the River Tamasa. The sun had already begun to set and so Sumantra and Laksmana made a bed of leaves for Lord Rama to sleep on. After lying down with Sitadevi, Lord Rama quickly fell asleep. Laksmana stayed awake all night, sitting nearby with Sumantra, discussing the glories of His elder brother. When Lord Rama awoke early the next morning, He saw all the citizens of Ayodhya sleeping nearby.

After devising a plan, Lord Rama told Laksmana, "It seems that the people will not give up their determination to try and convince Me to return home. Let us depart at once. Sumantra should drive the chariot in such a way that the people will not be able to understand which path we took."

After crossing the river, Lord Rama instructed Sumantra to take the chariot on a clearly visible path, just to mislead the citizens, and then return. Sumantra thus drove off alone, and when he came back some time later, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana got onto the chariot and proceed in another direction into the forest.

When the people awoke and found that Lord Rama had already left, they reproached themselves, saying, "How can we return to Ayodhya without Lord Rama? What will the women and children say if we fail to bring Him back with us?"

The people set out, following the tracks left by the chariot, but because of Lord Rama's trick, they soon became confused and lost the trail. At last, having no alternative, the people returned home feeling quite depressed. When the women saw the men coming back without Lord Rama, they reproached them with cruel and harsh words. Everyone greatly condemned Queen Kaikeyi, saying that they could never bear to live under her rule.

In this way, life in Ayodhya practically came to a standstill, making it look like a great ocean that had dried up. The brahmanas neglected to perform their sacrifices and the mercantile men gave up conducting business. Householders no longer bothered to prepare meals, and the domestic animals let the grass fall from their mouths, as if they were stunned. Cows no longer gave milk to their calves, and mothers felt no pleasure greeting their sons. Even the stars in the sky appeared to have a stern look about them. Feeling completely depressed, no one bothered to eat or engage in any kind of entertainment. Everyone appeared to be almost lifeless, like statues, because they were absorbed in thinking of Lord Rama in separation.

Wherever Lord Rama went, while proceeding through various territories, villages and forests, He heard the people condemning Maharaja Dasaratha and Queen Kaikeyi. Finally, after crossing the Vedasruti and Gomati Rivers, Lord Rama arrived at the River Syandika, which marked the southern boundary of the Kosala province.

There, Lord Rama got down from the chariot. While facing Ayodhya with folded hands, He took leave of His birthplace. Many of the local inhabitants had followed Lord Rama, and when He asked them to return home, they cried loudly, being unable to depart. Still, Lord Rama drove off swiftly, and while going along, He passed His time by having discussions with Sumantra. Finally, they reached the mighty Ganga, and there they stopped under a large inguda tree.

The king of this territory was named Guha, a Nisada by birth and a close friend of Lord Rama's. When he heard of Lord Rama's arrival, Guha jubilantly came to greet Him. As the sons of King Dasaratha rose from Their seats, the Nisada king came and embraced Lord Rama, feeling pained at heart to see how He was dressed in forest clothing.

Guha said, "My dear Lord Rama, my kingdom, Srngaverapura, is as much Yours as it is mine. Please make use of everything, as if You were back in Ayodhya. I am greatly honored that You have come here as my guest."

Guha had brought sumptuous food, comfortable beds, and articles of worship so that he could give Lord Rama a royal reception. Lord Rama was very pleased by all this, and while embracing Guha, He replied, "At heart, I accept all that you have offered Me, but since I have taken a vow to wear only kusa grass, tree bark and deerskin, and eat only fruit and roots, I cannot actually utilize these gifts. Please take away all that you have brought, except some of the food, which I can give to the horses, for they are My father's favorites."

While Guha went and fed the horses, Lord Rama accepted a little water from Laksmana, because He was fasting that day in honor of the Ganga. That night, Sitadevi and Lord Rama slept beneath the inguda tree while Laksmana and Guha stayed awake to guard them. Although Guha requested Laksmana to also rest, He refused to do so as long as Sitadevi and Lord Rama were sleeping. Indeed, Laksmana spent the entire night lamenting for His father and mothers, while Guha listened with tears in his eyes.

The next morning, Lord Rama wanted to cross the Ganga and so Guha gave Him the use of a beautiful boat. Before departing, Lord Rama told Sumantra, "Please return to Ayodhya and engage in My father's service. Remain calm so that you can pacify the others. You have been a great help to Me, but from now on We shall proceed by foot."

Sumantra pleaded with Lord Rama for permission to accompany Him. Lord Rama was adamant, though, and replied, "Do not be so sad, for We shall return after fourteen years. Assure My parents of My well being, and urge the king to install Bharata without delay. Also, request Bharata on My behalf to treat all His mothers equally."

Sumantra could not bear the thought of leaving Lord Rama, and so he argued, "When I return to Ayodhya alone, the people will be terribly unhappy. My Lord Rama, please let me accompany You. If You leave me aside, life will be unbearable. I will drown myself or enter fire."

Lord Rama said, "I am sending you back to Ayodhya so that Kaikeyi will be convinced that I have entered the forest. If you do not return with this chariot, she will always be afraid that I may come back at any time."

Turning to Guha, Lord Rama said, "I do not want to live in the forest surrounded by friends and relatives. I will go to an uninhabited place and live like a sage. Please bring Me the milk-like extract of the banyan tree so that I can mat My hair."

When Guha brought this substance, Lord Rama and Laksmana used it to mat Their hair so that They looked just like forest-dwelling rishis. While bidding Guha farewell, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana boarded the boat. After sipping some Ganga water, They departed.

When the boat reached mid-stream, Sitadevi offered prayers to mother Ganga, asking for Their safe return to Ayodhya. After disembarking on the far side, as the three continued Their journey, Lord Rama instructed Laksmana to carefully protect Sitadevi by walking in front, while He stayed behind Her.

When night fell, the three took shelter of a large tree, and as Sitadevi slept, Lord Rama and Laksmana stayed awake all night. As They talked, Lord Rama once again requested Laksmana to return home so that He could protect Their mothers from Kaikeyi's persecution. Lord Rama became very emotional while thinking about His mother, so that His face became covered with tears. While comforting Him, Laksmana was able to convince Lord Rama that He could not live in His absence, and so, at last, Lord Rama gave Him permission to stay.

The next morning, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana set out, and after walking and walking, They reached the confluence of the Ganga and Yamuna Rivers. Desiring to meet the great sage Bharadvaja, who lived nearby, Lord Rama approached the asrama and waited at the entrance. Soon, a disciple of the sage came to greet Him.

When Lord Rama went inside, He saw Bharadvaja sitting in meditation, surrounded by many students. Lord Rama introduced Himself and briefly related the incidents leading up to His arrival. Bharadvaja offered Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana a very nice reception, and fed them many varieties of foods prepared from fruit and roots.

At last, as he led them to their sleeping accommodations, Bharadvaja said, "My dear Rama, I have been expecting Your arrival ever since I heard the news of Your exile. I would like You to spend Your fourteen years of forest life here. With my students at Your disposal, I am sure that You, Your wife and Your brother will not experience even the slightest inconvenience."

Lord Rama replied, "O best of rishis, your offer is very kind, but since your asrama is so close to Ayodhya, many people would come to see Me if I stayed here. Please tell Me about some isolated spot where we can happily pass our period of exile."

Bharadvaja suggested, "You can go to Citrakuta Mountain, which is about one hundred kilometers from here. After crossing the River Yamuna, when You see the gigantic banyan tree named Syama, You will know that You are proceeding in the right direction. The entire area of Citrakuta is very beautiful, with cascading waterfalls and meandering streams. The cries of peacocks and cuckoos will enchant Your ears, and everywhere there is plenty of fruit and honey. Like the heavenly Gandhamadana, it is a very suitable place for leading a life in the mode of goodness."

Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana spent the night at Bharadvaja's asrama. The next morning, they took the rishi's permission and departed. After some time They came to the Yamuna, and so Lord Rama and Laksmana prepared a large raft to carry Them across the river.

Later, when they reached mid-stream, Sitadevi offered prayers to goddess Kalindi. After disembarking on the far side, They soon came to the banyan tree called Syama. Sitadevi offered her obeisances to the sacred tree, praying for Their safe return to Ayodhya. Finally, after walking for a few hours, the three travelers halted to pass the night by the side of the river.

Early the next morning, They resumed Their journey, and while walking through the forest, Sitadevi questioned Lord Rama again and again about the plants and trees she saw there. As Laksmana walked in front, bow in hand, Lord Rama took great pleasure in describing to Sitadevi the wonders of nature They encountered on the way.

When, at last, They arrived at the vicinity of Citrakuta, They were very pleased to find an abundance of fruit, roots, sweet water, and honey. There, They found Valmiki's asrama. Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana were very warmly received by the sage, for he had been expecting Their arrival for some time.

During the course of their conversation, Valmiki invited Lord Rama to reside at that place. At Lord Rama's request, Laksmana brought the necessary materials, and within a short time, He built a nice little house, having walls made of wooden stakes and a thatched roof. The customary rituals for consecrating their new dwelling were performed, and when Lord Rama entered His cottage, He felt very happy, as if He had left aside the grief brought about by mother Kaikeyi's intrigue.

Meanwhile, after Lord Rama's departure, Guha dispatched some spies to follow Him unnoticed. When news came back that Lord Rama had reached Citrakuta, Sumantra mounted his chariot and began the painful journey back to Ayodhya. Sumantra arrived in the evening of the second day, and he immediately noticed that the city had become practically desolate.

When the news of Sumantra's arrival spread, thousands of people came and gathered around him. In reply to their inquiries, Sumantra simply explained how he had returned after leaving Lord Rama by the shore of the Ganga.

Sumantra then went to see Maharaja Dasaratha, but as he began to relay Lord Rama's messages, the king fainted due to strong feelings of separation. Sumantra and mother Kausalyadevi quickly went to pick him up, but then she also lost consciousness while crying loudly.

Finally, after regaining his composure, Maharaja Dasaratha said, "My dear Sumantra, please describe to me all that Rama said before departing."

Knowing that the king's life could be sustained only by hearing about Lord Rama, Sumantra replied, "At the time of His departure, Lord Rama instructed me to return here, to engage in your service. It is only for your welfare that Lord Rama is determined to fulfill your promise to Kaikeyi. For the same reason, Lord Rama wants you to install Prince Bharata right away."

"After Lord Rama's departure, when I picked up the reins, my horses simply stood with tears in their eyes, refusing to move, and so I stayed with Guha for some time, hoping that Lord Rama would change His mind and call for me. Finally, after losing all hope, I returned here. All along the way, I saw that every moving and non- moving creature in the Kosala kingdom is in a miserable condition due to separation from Lord Rama."

While listening, Maharaja Dasaratha felt terribly distressed. In a fit of despair, he declared, "You must take me to Rama at once, or else bring Him back here, otherwise I shall die! I am merged in the ocean of grief! The two benedictions I granted Kaikeyi are the shores of that ocean, and the term of Rama's exile is its extent. My inward and outward breaths are the waves, and the rivers of tears shed by mother Kausalya and others further agitate that ocean. The flaying of arms in agony is the leaping of the fish, the disheveled hair is the seaweed, and the loud crying is its roar. Kaikeyi is the subterranean fire that causes showers of welled-up tears, and Manthara's words are the alligators that infest its waters."

After saying this, Maharaja Dasaratha fainted, mother Kausalya began writhing in agony on the floor, wailing, "O Sumantra, if you do not take me to Rama at once, I will surely die!"

Although Kausalyadevi was the foremost of devoted wives, she addressed her husband harshly, saying, "It is your fault that Rama has been exiled! How could you be so cruel to your son and His delicate wife? How will They be able to tolerate lying upon the bare ground? How will They survive, eating only fruit and roots? How will They be able to protect Themselves from the ferocious jungle animals, and how will They survive the onslaught of freezing cold, fierce winds and torrential rain?"

"Even if Rama lives and returns after fourteen years, Bharata will never give Him back the throne. And, even if Bharata offered it, Rama would never accept something already enjoyed by a younger brother. Oh, how unfortunate I am for having a husband like you! The primary support of a woman is her husband, but because you are under the control of Queen Kaikeyi, I am now bereft of that support. The secondary support of a woman is her son, but because Rama has gone away, I am now completely doomed!"

Maharaja Dasaratha was lying down, practically unconscious, and when mother Kausalyadevi thought about how much he was also suffering, her heart softened. When the king regained his senses, the two became reconciled, and soon thereafter, he fell asleep. However, after a short while, the king suddenly awoke, having remembered a terrible sin he had committed in the distant past.

Considering this to be the cause of his present suffering, Maharaja Dasaratha narrated the incident as follows, "When I was a young man, I learned the art of piercing an unseen target by hearing its sound. Once, as I was hunting at night on the shore of the River Sarayu, I heard what sounded like an elephant. I released my arrow, but then I heard the loud scream of a human voice, calling out, "Who has cruelly pierced me with his arrow, although I am a sage and without any fault?'"

"I quickly went there and saw a young hermit boy lying on the ground with my arrow stuck in his body. Seeing me, the boy said, 'My old and invalid parents are waiting for me to bring water. You have not only killed me, but you have killed them as well, for without me they will surely die. Go and confess that you have killed me, but first pull out the arrow, because it is giving me great pain.'"

Maharaja Dasaratha continued, "I hesitated to pull out the arrow, being afraid that the boy would immediately die. He then convinced me by saying, 'Do not think that you are killing a brahmana. I was born of a vaisya father and sudra mother.'"

So, I pulled out the arrow and the boy instantly gave up his life. I filled up his pitcher with water and carried it to his asrama with a grief-stricken heart. There, I saw the feeble, old, blind couple. Hearing me approach, they thought that their son had returned, and so they addressed me with affectionate words. This made me feel even worse, but I summoned my courage and explained to the couple how I had accidentally killed their son.

The old man then said, 'Because you have voluntarily confessed your sin, I will not curse you. If I were to do so, you would instantly die. Please take me to where my son's body is lying.'"

"After I led them there, the old, blind couple began to caress the dead body of their son and cry piteously. Then, when they offered libations of water for the benefit of their son's departed soul, King Indra descended to that spot, riding on a celestial chariot. After assuring the parents that they would achieve the same exalted destination as their son, Indra took the boy in his celestial form to the heavenly planets. The old sage then cursed me with the following words, 'Just as we are experiencing great anguish upon the death of our son, you will have to feel similar grief due to separation from yours.'"

"After saying this, the old couple threw themselves upon their son's funeral pyre, thus giving up their lives and ascending to heaven. My dear mother Kausalya, today I am reaping the fruit of my horrible crime. I am dying of a broken heart. I can feel the power of my senses ebbing, like the flame of a lamp without oil. I know that death will come to me very soon. O Queen, I have already become blind because of my grief. Please touch me so that I can be assured of your presence."

Maharaja Dasaratha continued to lament in separation from Lord Rama, while remembering Him with a fully absorbed mind. Then, when half of the night had passed, the king gave up his mortal body.

Early the next morning, the bards came as usual to awaken Maharaja Dasaratha, while the servants stood with folded hands, awaiting the king's orders. Then, when some of the king's consorts entered the room and saw that his body was without any sign of life, they cried out in horror. This awakened mother Kausalya and Sumitra with a start, and when the two queens realized that their husband had passed away, they fell to the floor, wailing in anguish. Upon hearing the uproar, Queen Kaikeyi quickly came there, along with some others, and when they understood that Maharaja Dasaratha had left his body, they also began to cry very piteously.

While caressing her dead husband's head, mother Kausalya told Kaikeyi, "I will enter the king's funeral pyre so that I can accompany him to his destination. Only a sinful wretch like you can bear to go on living in the absence of her husband."

The ministers then came and led Kausalyadevi away while trying their best to pacify her. They preserved the king's body in oil, because the funeral ceremonies could only be performed in the presence of a son. Being bereft of its ruler, the city of Ayodhya became shrouded in gloom. Since no one was able to sleep the following night, people gathered in the streets and reproached Queen Kaikeyi, to vent their grief.

The next morning, all the leading brahmanas assembled at the royal court, urging the priests and ministers to install one of the king's sons upon the throne that very day. Addressing Vasistha, Vamadeva, Markandeya, Kasyapa, Gautama and other foremost rishis, the brahmanas said, "A country without a king is doomed, because all sorts of rogues and thieves take advantage of the situation, creating lawlessness. In a kingdom without a ruler, ownership disappears, sons disobey fathers, the giving of charity is neglected, and religious practices decline as the citizens simply exploit one another."

The rishis replied, "What you say is perfectly correct, for it is the king who makes the demarcation between good and evil in this world. Bereft of a good king, the subjects are just like cattle without a herdsman."

Vasistha suggested, "Since Bharata has already been selected as the successor to the royal throne, He should be installed as soon as possible. Let messengers go swiftly to Girivraja, the capital city of King Kekaya, to bring Bharata and Satrughna back to Ayodhya."

All the brahmanas and ministers agreed. Messengers were dispatched at once, being instructed simply to say, "O Bharata, Your presence is urgently required at Ayodhya."

The night the messengers arrived, Bharata had a nightmare that caused Him to wake up in a very disturbed state of mind. When Satrughna inquired about His despondency, Bharata explained, "Last night, in My dreams, I saw My father, disheveled and dejected, fall from a mountaintop into a pool of cow dung. As he floundered in the mire, the king drank oil from his cupped palms and laughed hysterically like a madman. After the oil became smeared all over his body, he began eating rice that had been cooked in it."

"After that, the scene changed, and I saw that the ocean had dried up and the moon had fallen onto the earth. As the earth remained enveloped in darkness, Raksasas wandered over its surface while fire and lava erupted from volcanoes. Then, this scene evaporated and I next saw that the tusks of my father's elephants had been broken. Young dark-skinned women began harassing the king, who was completely dressed in black, smeared over with red sandalwood paste, adorned with a garland of red flowers, and seated upon an iron throne. Finally I saw a chariot drawn by donkeys swiftly carrying My father toward the south."

"After witnessing this ghastly dream, I have concluded that either My father, Me, or one of My brothers will surely die. Even now, although for no apparent reason, I feel a constant fear in My heart. My voice is hoarse and My complexion has lost its luster. My mind is reeling, and I feel shame and hatred—all for no apparent reason. The more I think about it, the more I become convinced that this nightmare forebodes a great evil."

While Bharata was speaking, the messengers from Ayodhya entered the room. After offering their respects to Maharaja Asvapati and Bharata, they relayed this message, "O Prince, You are being summoned to Ayodhya by the ministers and Vasistha Muni, for there is an urgent matter that requires Your presence."

When Bharata inquired about the welfare of His parents and brothers, the messengers assured Him that they were all fine, for that had been their instruction. Bharata departed at once, and even though Maharaja Asvapati gave Him many valuable gifts, He hardly noticed them because of the urgency of Vasistha's message and the premonition that had been caused by His dreams.

On the seventh day of their journey, Bharata and Satrughna arrived at the outskirts of Ayodhya. Even from a distance, the city did not seem to have its usual cheerful appearance. When Bharata entered Ayodhya, He saw that everyone was in a state of deep depression. The entire city appeared neglected and dirty, because all the citizens had stopped performing their occupational duties.

Being greatly alarmed, Bharata entered His father's palace, and when He failed to find the king in his room, He hurried to His mother's apartment. When Bharata entered her room, Kaikeyi jumped up from her seat, for she was overjoyed to see her long- absent son. Bharata bent down to clasp His mother's feet, and then she picked Him up and lovingly placed Him upon her lap. In answer to His mother's inquiries, Bharata assured her that her father was well.

Bharata said, "I have hurried back to Ayodhya at the urging of messengers. How is My father? I did not find him in his room."

Queen Kaikeyi calmly replied, "My son, Your father, the king, has passed away."

Upon hearing this, Bharata fell to the floor while crying aloud. Queen Kaikeyi quickly went and lifted Him up, saying, "My dear Bharata, do not become so sad. It is the duty of an enlightened person to remain equipoised."

Bharata tearfully asked, "How did My father die? Did he leave any message for Me? Mother, please send someone immediately to inform Rama of my arrival."

As if relating some pleasant news, Queen Kaikeyi frankly replied, "Being exiled from the Kosala kingdom, Rama went to the forest dressed in tree-bark, and Sita and Laksmana have followed Him. Your father could not bear to live without his pet son, and so he gave up his life while crying out, 'O Lord Rama! O Sitadevi! O Laksmana!'

Bharata asked, "Why was Lord Rama sent into exile? What horrible crime did He commit? Did He unwittingly kill a brahmana or take his possessions?"

To reply, Queen Kaikeyi narrated to Bharata all that had happened, considering herself to be very clever for having manipulated events as she did. Queen Kaikeyi said, "In your absence, the king suddenly announced that Rama would be installed upon the royal throne the very next day. To save us from being dominated by Queen Kausalya and her son, I took advantage of two benedictions Your father had formerly promised when I had saved his life."

"With the first boon I demanded that You become the king's successor, and with the second, I banished Rama to the forest for fourteen years. Rama was always Dasaratha's pet son, and so in separation, the king died of a broken heart."

"My dear son, you must accept the royal throne this very day, after performing the funeral ceremonies for Your departed father. Please take heart and give up Your grief."

When Bharata thus learned the truth of the matter, He angrily rebuked His mother, "O mother, you must be the goddess Kali herself, appearing for the destruction of the universe! You have killed My father and exiled Lord Rama, who is dearer to Me than My own life. Are you really so perverse that you believe I will accept the throne from you? You are not My mother—you are My worst enemy! I hope that you go to hell for all that you have done!"

"Dear mother, I will never let you accomplish your selfish ambition. I will go to the forest at once and bring back Lord Rama, so that He can rule the kingdom. After Lord Rama is installed upon the throne, I will engage myself as His eternal servant, for that is My natural position and highest aspiration. Hardhearted woman, think of how mother Kausalya must feel in the absence of her only son. Not only will I bring Lord Rama back to Ayodhya, but I will myself spend fourteen years in exile, just to keep My father's promise intact and save Lord Rama from abandoning His vow."

His anger having become highly inflamed, Bharata was hissing like a serpent. But then suddenly, due to excessive grief, He fell to the floor unconscious.

When He regained His senses after a long time, Bharata spoke as follows to the ministers who had gathered there, "I have no desire to become emperor of the world, for I consider Myself to be a servant of My elder brother, Lord Rama. Being away in a distant place, I had no idea of My mother's evil schemes. Please believe Me when I say that I had nothing to do with Lord Rama's exile, and that my mother's hopes are not shared by Me."

Queen Kaikeyi was shocked by Bharata's response to all that she had done. Her illusion having been destroyed, like a dream upon awakening, she felt pained at the turn of events and tears fell from her eyes.

Mother Kausalya learned of Bharata's arrival and so she went to meet Him, just as Bharata and Satrughna were on Their way to see her. While going, Kausalyadevi fainted, so that Bharata and Satrughna came upon her lying on the floor. They picked mother Kausalya up and embraced her, but when she saw Bharata, she sarcastically said, "Oh, You have come back to enjoy the kingdom You always coveted. You must be overjoyed that everything has worked out perfectly, according to plan."

Bharata felt very pained to hear these harsh words, and so He remained speechless for some time. Then, regaining His composure, Bharata fell at mother Kausalya's feet. After getting up, He said, "My dear mother, I had nothing to do with Lord Rama's exile. I had no idea of what my mother was planning. You know how much I love Lord Rama! Can't you see how aggrieved I am because of My father's death and Lord Rama's exile?"

"Just to convince you of My innocence, I hereby make this solemn declaration: 'May whoever helped my mother in her nefarious plan to exile Lord Rama reap the consequences of killing a king, a cow, a woman, a child, or an elderly person. May he become addicted to wine, women, meat and gambling. May godly men painstakingly avoid him, and may he remain childless throughout his life. May he incur the sinful reaction that afflicts the arsonist, the traitorous friend, and the unfair arbitrator. May he suffer the sinful reaction of refusing water to a thirsty man and shunning his wife when she approaches him at her time for conception.'"

"My dear mother, Lord Rama is My very life and soul, and My existence has no other purpose than dedication to His service."

Hearing this, mother Kausalya gave up her doubts and became convinced of Bharata's innocence. She lifted Him up and placed His head upon her lap, and she cried loudly while fondly caressing Him. Bharata also felt very aggrieved while seeing His stepmother. They passed the night together in commiseration.
