The next morning, Vasistha came and encouraged Bharata to cremate the body of His deceased father. The body was taken out of the vat of oil and placed upon a decorated carrier. However, as He looked at the dead body, Bharata lost all composure and began speaking to His father while sobbing convulsively. Once again, Vasistha urged Bharata to calm Himself, so that the funeral rites could be completed.

At last the procession wended its way as flowers were scattered on the road in front of the carrier. The funeral pyre had been constructed from logs of sandalwood and aguru. After the body was placed upon it, the fire was ignited while the brahmanas chanted mantras from the Sama-veda.

All the king's consorts had come there, riding upon chariots and palanquins, according to their status, and thus the air was filled with the sound of hundreds of wailing women.

At the conclusion of the cremation, everyone went to the River Sarayu to offer water for the benefit of the king's departed soul. After that, a ten-day contamination period was observed, and then Bharata performed some required rituals for two days. At the end of the twelfth day, Bharata gave profuse charity to the brahmanas.

At the dawn of the thirteenth day, Bharata and Satrughna went to the funeral pyre to collect the remains, which were to be thrown into the River Sarayu. Upon arriving there, however, Bharata and Satrughna once again became overwhelmed with grief. Weeping profusely, They fell to the ground. While writhing in agony, They cried out, again and again, "O dear father, where have you gone, leaving Us aside?"

Vasistha came and picked up Bharata, saying, "My dear prince, You must control Yourself so that You can perform Your duties. First collect the bones and ashes of Your father's cremated body. After cleaning the grounds, You must throw these remains into the river. The dualities of life and death, joy and sorrow, and gain and loss, are experienced by all embodied beings, and are unavoidable. The indulgence in lamentation that You are now displaying is not befitting a descendent of the Iksvaku dynasty."

Vasistha similarly picked up and pacified Satrughna. Thereafter, all the funeral rites were dutifully completed. Finally, after returning to the palace, Bharata began to think about going to the forest and bringing back Lord Rama and Laksmana.

Satrughna said, "Lord Rama is the best well-wishing friend of everyone, and yet He has been exiled to the forest at the insistence of a cruel and selfish woman. My brother Laksmana must be even more condemned than mother Kaikeyi! Was there nothing He could do to avert this disaster?"

Just at that moment, the hunch-backed maidservant, Manthara, appeared on the scene. Although she looked no better than a monkey, her body was smeared with sandalwood paste, and she was wearing a valuable dress and jeweled ornaments.

Knowing her to be the real cause of Lord Rama's banishment, the doorman suddenly grabbed Manthara by the arm and dragged her before Satrughna, exclaiming, "Here is the wicked witch that incited Mother Kaikeyi to banish Lord Rama to the forest! O Prince, she fanned the flames of jealousy within the queen's heart, and thus became the murderer of Your father. Punish her as You see fit!"

Overcome with a strong desire for revenge, Satrughna violently grabbed Manthara and began dragging her along the floor. As Manthara's ornaments broke, so that the jewels scattered here and there, her hunchbacked companions ran away in fear.

Satrughna then vowed, "I shall now release My long-held wrath upon both Manthara and Mother Kaikeyi, so they can feel as much pain as they have caused others."

As Queen Kaikeyi ran to Him for shelter, Bharata restrained His younger brother, saying, "O Satrughna, women never deserve to be killed! I would have gladly killed Manthara and my mother, except that I knew that Lord Rama would have never forgiven Me. Please restrain Yourself!"

Satrughna relented, letting go of Manthara, and she went sobbing to Kaikeyi's shelter.

The next morning, the bards came to eulogize Bharata in expectation of His becoming the next king. Their praises only served to aggravate His grief, however, and so He ordered them to stop by saying; "I am not the ruler of Ayodhya."

Meanwhile, in the royal assembly, Vasistha Muni ordered that Bharata be summoned, along with the ministers, military commanders and other important citizens. When Bharata arrived at the assembly hall, He was received with the same enthusiasm that had formerly been shown Maharaja Dasaratha.

Vasistha said, "O Prince, because Rama and Laksmana have been banished to the forest and Your father has passed away, life within the kingdom is now disrupted. We know that You had nothing to do with Queen Kaikeyi's intrigues, and that Maharaja Dasaratha selected You as his successor. All the preparations have been made and the citizens are in great anticipation. Please give Your consent so that we can install You upon the throne today."

Bharata felt very pained at heart to hear this proposal. While restraining His tears, He replied, "O foremost of rishis, you are well aware of our family tradition that the eldest son must be the inheritor of the royal throne. I could never think of usurping the kingdom from Lord Rama, for I consider Myself to be His menial servant. I want to go to the forest as soon as possible, to persuade Lord Rama to return to Ayodhya. If I am unsuccessful, then I shall also remain in the forest, like Laksmana."

"Please make ready a formidable army, and have the engineers construct roads where there are none, so that we can proceed without impediment."

Tears came to the eyes of all who heard Bharata's impassioned statements, and the ministers were very pleased by His determination. Upon receiving everyone's approval, Bharata felt relieved and began shedding tears of joy.

Thereafter, the ministers summoned all kinds of engineers and laborers. Soon, a huge work crew left Ayodhya, to construct a broad highway into the forest.

First the workers cut down many trees, dug up their roots, and leveled the ground by filling in the holes. They built bridges across the streams, and after paving the road, they lined it with rows of flowering trees. Dams were constructed so that artificial lakes could be built.

When everything was completed, the entire highway, which extended from Ayodhya to the shore of the Ganga, was sprinkled with sandalwood-scented water. Large tents were put up at appropriate places, to provide rest camps for the travelers, and fences and moats surrounded them. The entire highway, which appeared to be fit for the demigods, was completed in an amazingly short time.

The next morning, Bharata ordered Sumantra to bring His chariot, and before long, They departed, along with a huge army. mother Kausalya, Sumitradevi and Queen Kaikeyi rode in separate chariots, and they were all hopeful of bringing Lord Rama back from the forest. Many other citizens also accompanied Bharata, being delighted at the prospect of seeing Lord Rama.

Arriving at the shore of the Ganga, the party rested for the night. When Guha saw Bharata's army, he suspected that He might be going with the intention of attacking Lord Rama. He therefore told his associates, "We are followers of Lord Rama. As a precaution, let one hundred soldiers guard each of our five hundred boats so that if necessary, we can prevent Bharata from crossing the Ganga."

Then, taking many gifts with him, Guha went to meet Bharata so that he could ascertain His actual mentality. When Sumantra saw Guha approaching, he informed Bharata of his friendship with Lord Rama. The king of the Nisadas came and extended all hospitality, and in turn Bharata asked about the route to Bharadvaja's asrama.

Guha said, "You can rest assured that I will guide You there. Now, please tell me frankly what Your intentions are in going to see Lord Rama. Since You have brought such a large army, it appears that You want to fight with Him."

Bharata replied, "My dear Guha, your words give Me great pain. Please believe Me when I say that I have come here to persuade Lord Rama to return to Ayodhya so that He can be installed as the ruler of the Kosalas."

Guha was convinced by Bharata's sincerity, and because darkness had already fallen, everyone prepared to rest for the night. Later, Guha approached Bharata as He lay upon His bed, and said, "When Lord Rama came here, along with Sitadevi and Laksmana, He lay down on a bed that we made of dry leaves. As Sitadevi and Lord Rama slept, Laksmana and I stayed awake all night talking. Laksmana could not bear to accept any comfort as long as Lord Rama lay upon the bare ground. The next morning, Lord Rama and Laksmana matted Their hair with the sticky extract of the banyan tree and then crossed the Ganga."

When Bharata heard how Lord Rama had matted His hair, He became disheartened, thinking that He might not be willing to return to Ayodhya. Kausalyadevi was seated nearby, and she came to comfort Bharata. Guha continued to speak about everything that Lord Rama had done while going to the forest. Then he led Bharata, Satrughna and Their mothers to the place where Sitadevi and Lord Rama had slept, showing them the kusa grass that remained crushed by the weight of Their bodies. Everyone felt sad to see this, for they remembered how Lord Rama used to sleep in royal comfort.

Bharata was especially pained, because He considered Himself to be the cause of Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana's sufferings. While condemning Himself, Bharata made up His mind to mat His hair, and also lie upon the bare ground, to take Lord Rama's place in exile.

The next morning, Bharata, who now had matted hair and clothes made of tree-bark, requested Guha to help Him cross the Ganga. All the men and horses then crossed the river in boats, while the elephants swam. After disembarking, Guha showed Bharata the way to Bharadvaja's asrama.

When He came close to the sage's hermitage, Bharata left his army at a distance of three kilometers and then proceeded on foot with Satrughna and Vasistha Muni. When he saw Vasistha, Bharadvaja quickly got up to greet him. After the usual formalities, Bharadvaja inquired, "My dear Bharata, please tell me why you have come to the forest. I certainly hope that you have not come with the intention of harming Rama."

It was painful for Bharata to hear the sage's words. With tears in His eyes, He replied, "It is very disturbing for Me to see how people doubt My motives. Please be assured that I have come here simply to convince Lord Rama to return to Ayodhya and rule the kingdom. O best of rishis, kindly point out where My brother is staying."

Bharadvaja said, "My dear Prince, I already knew of your intentions by my yogic powers. Still, I felt it was my duty to test You. Lord Rama is staying at Citrakuta Mountain. Remain here tonight and then continue Your journey tomorrow morning."

Bharata accepted the invitation. Bharadvaja then asked Him to call for His soldiers, for he wished to entertain them. After the army's arrival, Bharadvaja invoked Visvakarma, along with other leading demigods, headed by Indra, Yama, Varuna and Kuvera. The sage requested them to supply all necessary objects of enjoyment so that he could provide a first-class reception for his guests.

Immediately after the departure of the demigods, fragrant breezes began to blow. Then, by the influence of Visvakarma, the entire area, to the extent of sixty kilometers, became carpeted, except for some gardens placed at intervals, full of fruit trees and flowers. Miraculously, four-room houses and stables manifested themselves, as well as a royal palace furnished with every conceivable article of enjoyment.

Bharata entered this magnificent building, followed by His priests and ministers, and as He approached the royal throne, He meditated upon Lord Rama, as if He were seated there. Bharata circumambulated His elder brother and then bowed His head to offer obeisances. He picked up a camara and sat down in the chair meant for the chief minister, as all others took their respective seats.

Meanwhile, by utilizing his mystic power, Bharadvaja created a river of sweet-rice that flowed through his asrama. Lord Brahma and Kuvera each sent 20,000 women whose embrace would cause a man to appear as if seized by a fit of madness.

The chief Gandharvas—Narada, Tumburu and Gopa—came to sing of Lord Rama's glories to Bharata, and Bharadvaja summoned celestial trees to accompany them with musical instruments. Thus, a bael tree played tablas while pipal trees danced. Other trees, such as the tamala, appeared as hunchbacks and dwarves to render service to Bharata, and jambu trees took the form of young women.

While Bharata was being entertained in this manner, the soldiers were hardly neglected. Celestial girls sang to them in very sweet voices while urging them to drink wine and eat sweet-rice. Seven or eight girls were engaged in massaging oil over each soldier's body, and then bathing him with water. Other girls massaged the soldiers' feet, and had them drink wine in a secluded place. Even the horses were fed sumptuously with grains soaked in honey.

When the men became intoxicated, they exclaimed, "This is heaven! We no longer want to continue our journey to the forest or return to Ayodhya!"

In this way the soldiers became satiated with all kinds of enjoyment in the association of heavenly damsels. In fact, thousands of soldiers were seen running here and there while singing, dancing and laughing. Everyone was so amazed by the entertainment that they felt as if they were enjoying a delightful dream. The night seemed to pass quickly, and with the appearance of dawn, all the Gandharvas, Apsaras and celestial trees took permission from Bharadvaja and returned to their respective abodes.

Bharata then took His mothers and went to Bharadvaja to ask for directions to Citrakuta. As the sage inquired whether Bharata had enjoyed the reception, he noticed that a woman appeared to be emaciated because of grief, another was clasping his feet to offer respect, and a third stood with her head hung in shame.

When Bharadvaja inquired about their identities, Bharata replied, "This is mother Kausalya, the seniormost widow of Dasaratha, and this is mother Sumitra, the mother of Laksmana and Satrughna. The other woman, who is cruel, vulgar, conceited and sinful, is my mother. Being the cause of Lord Rama's exile, she is the root of all our present miseries."

Bharadvaja said, "My dear Bharata, You should not consider Your mother to be guilty, for there is a higher purpose behind Rama's banishment to the forest. Your mother's actions are just a part of a great plan, meant for the welfare and happiness of all beings."

Thereafter, Bharata circumambulated Bharadvaja and then commanded His army to depart. As the regiments of infantry, cavalry, elephants and chariots proceeded, all the forest animals fled n fear to distant places. At last, when Citrakuta Mountain came into view, Bharata asked the soldiers to be on the lookout for Lord Rama's cottage. Someone then saw a column of smoke rising in the distance, and so he excitedly went and told Bharata. After ordering His army to set up camp, Bharata went on foot, along with Vasistha, Satrughna and a few others.

Meanwhile, Lord Rama had been sitting with Sitadevi on a big rock at the foot of the hill. Pleasantly passing their time, Lord Rama was describing to Sitadevi the varieties of roots that the rishis eat. He also pointed out all kinds of trees, plants and wild animals that were found in the region. They had been residing at Citrakuta for about three months, and Lord Rama always tried His best to divert Sitadevi's mind from the grief of living in exile. Lord Rama pointed toward the River Mandakini which was adorned with beautiful flowers, and which served as a sporting ground for numerous swans, cranes and other exotic birds.

Lord Rama said, "Dear one, there is no reason for Us to be aggrieved, for We are living together in such a lovely place."

It was at this time that Lord Rama spotted the dust being raised by Bharata's army in the far distance. Soon, Lord Rama could hear the sounds of the wild animals fleeing in fear. Turning to Laksmana, He said, "Just see the dust being raised over there by something frightening the forest animals. Please find out at once what is causing this disturbance."

Laksmana climbed a tall tree, and from there he could see the vast army approaching. With great alarm, he called down to Lord Rama, "Put out the fire immediately and hide mother Sitadevi within a cave. Then bring Our weapons, for there is an army coming toward Us."

Lord Rama called back, "Look at the flags on the chariots and see if you can tell whose army it is."

When Laksmana saw that the flags had the design of a white kovidara tree, he shouted, "It is Bharata's army! Our brother must be coming here to kill Us so that He can be assured of enjoying unrivaled sovereignty. The soldiers are approaching us with great enthusiasm, so We had better take our bows and prepare to fight from the top of the hill. O Lord Rama, it is Our good fortune that Bharata has come here, so that I can fight with the one who has caused Our suffering. We should not hesitate to kill Bharata. After doing so, I will take great pleasure in killing mother Kaikeyi and all her relatives, just to purge the earth of the sins she committed."

To pacify Laksmana, Lord Rama replied, "When Bharata has come here in great eagerness to see Me, what is the necessity of bows and arrows? In My opinion, Bharata has come here to offer Me the kingdom. However, if You want to kill Bharata so that You can enjoy the kingdom for Yourself, just say so and I will order Bharata to hand it over to You peacefully."

After being thus admonished, Laksmana hung His head in shame and said, "I was hoping that Our father had come to see Us. I can see his elephant, but it is without the usual white umbrella and so I think that he has not come."

Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana returned to their cottage while Bharata was searching in the forest nearby. Climbing a tree, Bharata spotted a column of smoke and so He hastened in that direction, followed by Guha, Satrughna and Sumantra, while Vasistha went to get the three mothers.

At last Bharata came to a clearing in the forest, and there He saw the small cottage with Lord Rama and Laksmana's gold-plated bows, and a sacrificial altar in front. Upon seeing Lord Rama, Bharata rushed toward Him with tears in His eyes, crying out, "Oh! How painful it is to see You with matted hair and tree-bark clothing! You were accustomed to living in royal comfort, but due to My fault, You are now suffering in the forest."

As Bharata ran to touch Lord Rama's lotus feet, He fainted and fell to the ground. Lord Rama quickly came and lifted Him up, and He was also very aggrieved to see His younger brother so emaciated and dressed in tree-bark. Lord Rama embraced Satrughna, who had fallen at His feet, and then Sumantra and Guha.

After taking Bharata upon His lap, Lord Rama inquired, "My dear brother, why have You come here, leaving Our father alone and unattended to? Tell Me about Our mothers, and about the residents of Ayodhya. Are they well and happy? I hope that You have not come here after having already lost Your sovereignty!"

Lord Rama continued to inquire about Bharata's welfare, in a manner that would give instruction to all political heads of state, "My dear brother, a righteous kingdom will surely prosper. Are the brahmanas, demigods and elders being properly respected? Are the women within the kingdom being properly honored and protected? Are disputes being impartially judged? I hope that You avoid the fourteen weaknesses—atheism, hypocrisy, anger, procrastination, laziness, slavery to the senses, contempt for good advice, lack of vigilance, fondness for bad council, impractical planning, inability to keep a secret, attachment to foolish friends, negligence of religious observances, and failure to counteract enemies."

"Bharata, are you aware of the three kinds of prowess—physical strength, power of authority, and intelligence? Do You avoid the following eight kinds of friends—those who are deceitful, reckless, violent, envious, gossip-mongers, foul-mouthed, usurpers of property, and unjust?"

"Do You avoid making alliances with these twenty kinds of kings—those who are child-monarchs, senile, frail and ill, over- aggressive, cowardly, greedy, sensualists, and frivolous, and those who were defeated in battle, and who were overthrown, and those who frequently travel abroad, have many enemies, are poverty- stricken, are opposed to religious principles, are despised by their ministers, and are mentally disturbed?"

"I hope that You prefer to keep the company of one learned man rather than thousands of ignorant fools. A single talented minister can give one immense benefit, whereas in times of necessity, thousands of fools are of no use."

"My dear Bharata, if a king does not get rid of a physician who is simply adept at aggravating a disease, a servant who in intent upon bringing disgrace upon his master, or a warrior who wants to become the ruler himself, then he will surely be killed by these three."

"Are the women protected and honored by You, and are they pacified with regards to their material demands? I hope that You do not place too much faith in women, or disclose Your secrets to them."

While observing Bharata's matted hair and forest dress, Lord Rama could understand that He had accepted these austerities out of love for Him. After once again embracing Bharata, Lord Rama inquired, "My dear brother, why have You relinquished the throne and come here dressed as a mendicant?"

Bharata replied, "My dear Lord Rama, my mother's intrigues were carried out without My knowledge. Please believe Me. You are the rightful inheritor of the royal throne, and so I implore You to return at once and rule the kingdom. Practically the whole of Ayodhya has come to a standstill, for everyone is morose because of Your absence."

As He made this impassioned appeal, Bharata clasped Sri Lord Rama's lotus feet and placed them upon His head. Lord Rama picked up Bharata and embraced Him while saying, "I know that You had no part in banishing Me to the forest. Still, I don't want to regain the kingdom by sinful means."

"My dear Bharata, You should not condemn Your mother or the king, for they were acting within the bounds of propriety. In fact, elders are always free to treat their dependents as they like. Just accept the royal throne and rule the kingdom, for that was our dear father's desire. For My part, I am determined to spend fourteen years in the forest. I consider father's order to be the supreme religious principle."

Bharata objected, saying, "In the presence of an elder prince, no one can become the king. You must come back to Ayodhya and sit upon the throne after offering water for the benefit of Our dear father's departed soul."

Upon hearing of His father's death, Lord Rama fainted onto the ground. Sitadevi, Bharata and Laksmana hastily sprinkled water over His body so that Lord Rama soon regained consciousness. After tearfully lamenting at great length the loss of His father, Lord Rama declared, "I shall never return to Ayodhya!"

Afterwards, Lord Rama went to the Mandakini-ganga River, along with His brothers and Sumantra. There, He offered water for the welfare of His departed father. Upon returning to His cottage, Lord Rama clasped His brothers' hands, and all four began wailing with grief. This sound reverberated throughout the valley, and when the soldiers of Bharata's army heard it, they became saddened, knowing it to be the grief of the four brothers. They hurried toward the direction from which the sound was coming, and soon after, they greeted Lord Rama with tears in their eyes.

Meanwhile, keeping the three widows of Maharaja Dasaratha in front, Vasistha also hastened to see Lord Rama. Arriving at the Ganga, he saw the remnants of articles that Lord Rama had used for making offerings to His departed father. Mother Kausalya was pained to observe that the only food Lord Rama had at His disposal was some pulp of inguda fruit.

Considering this to be unworthy of being offered to her husband, with a tinge of amusement she thought, "Now I am practically experiencing the truth of the old adage that says, "The deities that a man worships have to partake of the same food upon which he subsists."

When Vasistha and the three women came to the cottage, they were very pained to see how Lord Rama looked like a demigod that had fallen from heaven. Lord Rama quickly stood up and went to touch His mothers' feet. As Lord Rama bowed down, the mothers wiped the dust from His back, while Sitadevi and Laksmana also came to greet them.

After the exchange of welcomes, Lord Rama sat down with His preceptor, Vasistha, while the others took their seats around them. Bharata told Lord Rama, "My mother is now repentant, and so, with her permission, I wish to return the kingdom of Kosala to You."

Everyone present applauded the proposal, but Lord Rama replied, "My dear Bharata, in this world no one can act independently. Under the control of eternal Time, everything ultimately meets with defeat. Therefore, no wise man should lament for life's reverses. Union always ends in separation. Pieces of driftwood float together on the surface of the ocean, and later on disperse. Similarly, members of family and society meet briefly and then depart for their separate destinations. As solid pillars gradually decay, causing a house to collapse, so a man totters into old age, and at last meets death. As a river cannot return to its source, so everyone must follow the path of his forefathers."

"Why should one feel sorry for others, when he himself is going to die? A man's skin becomes wrinkled and his hair turns grey. What can he do? He is happy to see the sun rise, and then he is happy to see the sun set, without realizing that he has died a little. Therefore, a sober and learned person should use his energy for executing religious principles, so that he can attain a higher destination after death."

"My dear Bharata, there is no need to lament for Our father, because he has given up an old body to attain heavenly happiness. You must return to Ayodhya at once and execute Our father's order, just as I will remain here to carry out his will."

Still, Bharata argued, "O Lord Rama, Our noble father had become too infatuated with women in his old age. He seemed to confirm the popular notion that at the time of death, one invariably becomes obsessed with something. You should return to Ayodhya and thus undo the harm Our father caused by his weakness. You are a ksatriya, and so it Your duty to act like one, and not like a sage. You are a grhastha, which is the noblest of the four asramas. Why are You abandoning the duties of married life? If You refuse to return to Ayodhya, then I shall also not go back. I will remain here in the forest, like You and Laksmana."

Lord Rama said, "My dear Bharata, You are wrongly criticizing Our father by saying that he was impelled by lusty attachment for mother Kaikeyi. At the time of the king's marriage to Queen Kaikeyi, her father had made him promise that it would be her son who succeeded him as emperor. Please go back to Ayodhya so that Our father's promise can be kept intact. You must take responsibility for ruling the kingdom, even if it is only for My sake."

A great sage named Abaci was accompanying Vasistha, and he was the next to speak. In the hopes of arousing within Lord Rama the desire to enjoy royal opulence and sense gratification, thus facilitating His return to Ayodhya, Abaci expounded an atheistic philosophy.

The sage said, "O Prince, a living being is nothing more than the resultant combination of a sperm and ovum. The father is called the efficient cause of the living entity and the mother is called the material cause, but in reality, the only cause of creation is the chance interaction of atomic particles. When a person dies, he once again becomes dust. Thus, everything we see is just a combination of atoms, and nothing more. What we call mother, father, son or daughter, have no real relationship with us."

"O Rama, why should You suffer, just for the sake of Your so- called father? What is the use in performing the sraddha ceremony? Can a dead man eat Your offerings? Whatever we perceive is the all-in-all. Therefore, You should enjoy what is pleasing to the senses by accepting the kingdom from Bharata."

After hearing this, Lord Rama heatedly replied, "Your views are actually impiety in the garb of piety, and because of this, they are most dangerous. In the guise of wisdom, you are preaching the grossest ignorance."

"One who lives only for the pleasure of the senses becomes a slave to uncontrolled lust and greed. Truth is the highest principle of virtue, and so I will adhere to the order of My father without being swayed by the aspiration for personal sense enjoyment. This earth is the field of activities, and when one attains the rarely achieved human birth, only pious acts should be performed."

"My father made a mistake by keeping you as an advisor, because your views are staunchly atheistic, like those of Carvaka and the Buddhists. People like you are so condemned that the Vedas prohibit one from even seeing the face of an atheist."

Jabali said, "My dear Rama, I am actually a follower of the Vedas, and I have firm faith in the existence of God. I was only speaking from the atheistic point of view to try and persuade You to return to Ayodhya."

Lord Rama had become terribly angry while listening to Jabali's atheistic discourse and so Vasistha also spoke to pacify Him. The sage said, "My dear Rama, every king in the Iksvaku dynasty had selected his eldest son to inherit the kingdom. The unbroken rule is that as long as an elder brother is alive, no younger brother can sit on the throne. Therefore, I implore You to return to Ayodhya. To deny my order would be irreligious, because the guru's instructions take precedence over those given by a mother or father."

Lord Rama remained adamant, however, insisting that one's duty to his parents is inviolable. When Bharata finally understood that His attempt to persuade Lord Rama had failed, He became so depressed that he ordered Sumantra, "Spread kusa grass in front of the door to Lord Rama's cottage. I am going to sit there blindfolded, fasting from all food and drink, until Lord Rama relents and accepts the royal throne."

Sumantra would not act without Lord Rama's order, however, and so Bharata spread the kusa grass with His own hands and then sat down at the entrance to Lord Rama's cottage. Lord Rama said, "My dear brother, I have not done You any harm. Why are You trying to force Me like this?"

In response, Bharata appealed to all those present, "Why are you standing like statues? Why don't you try and dissuade Lord Rama from His determination to remain here in the forest?"

The people replied, "Since Lord Rama is fully determined to fulfill His father's promise, it is very difficult for us to even think of arguing with Him.

Lord Rama then said, "My dear brother, why don't You also accept this attitude, instead of stubbornly trying to oppose Me?"

At last, Bharata gave up the idea of fasting until death. Still, He pleaded, "My dear Lord Rama, let me act as Your proxy by staying here in the forest for fourteen years, while You return to Ayodhya to rule the kingdom.

Lord Rama replied, "This is not a proper proposal, for the use of a proxy is only sanctioned when one is incapable of performing some act."

Just then, a host of celestial rishis appeared invisibly in the sky and praised the wonderful conversation between the two divine brothers. Desiring to accelerate the death of Ravana, the rishis said, "O Bharata, you should follow Your elder brother's advice."

Lord Rama was very pleased to hear this, but Bharata still pleaded, "I am not powerful enough to rule the kingdom, and so You must return to Ayodhya and take charge of the administration."

Saying this, Bharata fell at Lord Rama's lotus feet. Lord Rama took Bharata upon His lap and said, "My dear brother, You are quite capable of ruling the kingdom in My absence. My mind cannot be deterred from its decision, and so You must give up all reluctance and accept the responsibility that has come to You."

At last surrendering to the inevitable, Bharata brought forth a pair of wooden sandals that were inlaid with gold, and requested Lord Rama to place His lotus feet upon them. Lord Rama put on the shoes and immediately gave them back to Bharata.

Bharata bowed down to Lord Rama and said, "After relegating the burden of ruling the kingdom to these shoes, I will remain outside Ayodhya, keeping matted hair and wearing clothes of tree- bark. O Lord Rama, I will eat only fruit and roots while awaiting Your return, but if after fourteen years You do not come back, I will give up my life by entering fire."

Lord Rama accepted this statement and embraced Bharata and Satrughna. He assured Them that after the expiry of His term of exile, He would return home to sit on the throne. With tears in His eyes, Lord Rama bid farewell to His brothers while urging them not to neglect mother Kaikeyi. Lord Rama spoke some very sweet words to His mothers, but they were too upset to say anything in reply. Lord Rama then implored everyone to return to Ayodhya, and after doing so, He turned His back and entered His cottage.

Thereafter, placing Lord Rama's shoes upon his head, Bharata got onto his chariot, along with Satrughna, and set out, keeping the brahmanas in front.

After arriving at Bharadvaja's asrama, Bharata went and offered his obeisances to the sage. In answer to Bharadvaja's inquiries, Bharata described Lord Rama's determination to remain in exile. Vasistha then brought Lord Rama's shoes and requested Bharadvaja to empower them with the potency to fulfill all the needs of Ayodhya's citizens. This was done ceremonially by the sage, after which Bharata took permission to depart.

Returning by the same route by which he came to the forest, Bharata eventually arrived at the outskirts of Ayodhya. Seeing the city's desolate appearance, he remarked to Sumantra, "Without Lord Rama, Ayodhya has no life, just as the material body becomes inert after the passing away of the soul."

After entering his father's palace, Bharata broke down and cried, because of its bleak and desolate appearance. At this time he made up His mind to retire to Nandigrama and live there like an ascetic. The ministers praised this noble determination. Bharata then called for His chariot and departed, along with Satrughna. Out of spontaneous affection, all the citizens followed Bharata as He went to Nandigrama. Upon His arrival, Bharata first ordered His ministers to place Lord Rama's shoes upon the throne.

Thereafter, as He remained at Nandigrama, along with His army, Bharata submitted all the state affairs to Lord Rama's shoes, as well as all the gifts that were presented to Him. Bharata would often be seen holding the royal umbrella over Lord Rama's shoes, or else fanning them with a camara. In this way Bharata ruled the kingdom for fourteen years, always feeling Himself subordinate to Lord Rama, who remained there in the form of His shoes.

Meanwhile, Lord Rama began to notice anxiety in the minds of the rishis living in the vicinity of Citrakuta. Feeling that perhaps He was the cause, Lord Rama humbly approached the leader of the rishis and said, "I can see that there is something disturbing the minds of the rishis living here. If you can, please disclose the reason for this, especially if there is some fault on My part."

The elderly sage replied, "My dear Lord Rama, there could never be any fault on Your part. Our fear is caused by the Raksasas, headed by Khara, the younger brother of Ravana. Being envious by nature, Khara and the other Raksasas come before us in hideous forms, and while disrupting our sacrifices, they pollute our bodies with foul substances. We plan to move to another area before the Raksasas resort to violence. I will take my disciples to Asva Muni's asrama, which is not very far from here. If You like, You can accompany us and take up residence there."

Lord Rama informed the sage that He preferred to remain at Citrakuta, but after some time, He made up His mind to go to Atri Muni's asrama, feeling disturbed by memories of Bharata and His mothers' visit there. Thereafter, Atri Muni received Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana very warmly, as if They were his own children. Sitadevi then took the opportunity of meeting the sage's elderly wife, Anasuya. While Sitadevi listened with rapt attention, Anasuya spoke about the duties of a chaste wife.

Finally, being very pleased with Sitadevi, Anasuya said, "My dear child, I wish to offer you a benediction by utilizing my ample stock of ascetic merit. Just tell me what it is that you would like."

Sitadevi replied, "O saintly lady, your presence is, in itself, sufficient benediction."

Nevertheless, Anasuya gave Sitadevi an unfading celestial garland, celestial garments and ornaments, and cosmetics that were inexhaustible. When evening approached, Anasuya asked Sitadevi to go and attend to her husband, after putting on her newly acquired dress and jewelry. Lord Rama was very pleased to see Sitadevi in this way, and that night They slept at Atri's asrama. The next morning, Lord Rama inquired from Atri Muni about the path to the Dandaka forest.

At this time the sage warned, "This forest is infested with fierce and malicious Raksasas. They take pleasure in devouring anyone who neglects to wash his mouth after eating, or who is inattentive in following other such scriptural injunctions."

Lord Rama bid farewell to Atri Muni and Anasuya, and then, along with Sitadevi and Laksmana, entered the dense forest, like the moon entering a mass of dark clouds.

## Aranya-kanda

Within the Dandaka forest, Sitadevi, Lord Rama, and Laksmana saw a delightful cluster of cottages inhabited by rishis. Surrounded by trees laden with fruit and flowers, and resounding with the singing of birds and the chanting of Vedic mantras, the asrama sanctified the hearts of all who beheld it. In the surrounding area, the deer and other animals roamed without fear. Lord Rama and Laksmana removed the strings from Their bows and entered the asrama.

They were very warmly received by the rishis and were given a grass hut to stay in. After spending the night, Lord Rama bid farewell to the rishis and entered deeper into the forest, with Sitadevi in the middle and Laksmana behind her.

Suddenly, a gigantic, hideous Raksasa appeared upon the scene. Dressed in blood-stained tiger-skin and having sunken eyes, long sharp teeth, a jagged jaw, and a round protruding belly, the Raksasa carried a spear from which dangled the heads of three lions, four tigers, two wolves, ten spotted deer, and an elephant, all dripping with blood. Appearing like death personified, the horrible monster roared ferociously and lunged forward, snatching Sitadevi.

Quickly retreating to some distance, the Raksasa roared once again and declared, "I will take this woman for my wife, and drink the blood of You other two."

Lord Rama exclaimed, "To see Sitadevi being touched by someone else is even more painful for Me than the death of My father!"

Laksmana said reproachfully, "How can You just stand there and lament? Watch now as I kill this Raksasa by venting the anger against Bharata that I have been obliged to suppress for so long!"

At this point, the Raksasa inquired, "Who are You, that have intruded into my forest?"

Lord Rama replied, "We are two ksatriyas. Why have you dared to obstruct Us and carry off My wife?"

The Raksasa said, "My name is Viradha, the son of Java by his wife, Satahrada. As a reward for my severe austerities, I received a benediction from Brahma that no weapon can kill me. For this reason I advise You to run away at once. If You do so, leaving this lovely woman for me, I will spare You."

Upon hearing this, Lord Rama angrily strung His bow and quickly released seven golden-feathered arrows that made Viradha fall to the ground, bleeding profusely. After letting Sitadevi go, Viradha picked up his lance and rushed madly toward Lord Rama and Laksmana. Although the two brothers showered innumerable arrows upon the Raksasa, he simply laughed hideously in response. In fact, as Viradha swallowed some of the arrows, the rest simply fell down from his body as a result of Brahma's benediction. While laughing, Viradha threw his lance, but Lord Rama cut it to pieces with His arrows.

Taking their swords, Lord Rama and Laksmana rushed at Viradha. And yet, even as they hacked away at his body, the Raksasa picked Lord Rama and Laksmana up in his arms and began carrying Them away on his shoulders into the forest. Seeing this, Sitadevi cried out piteously, "O Raksasa, please leave these two aside and take me instead."

When They heard this, Lord Rama and Laksmana decided to kill Viradha at once. Exhibiting superhuman prowess, Lord Rama broke off the Raksasa's right arm while Laksmana wrenched off his left. This caused Viradha to fall to the ground unconscious, while Lord Rama and Laksmana continued to beat him with Their fists. Still, despite being repeatedly smashed and kicked, the Raksasa did not die.

Lord Rama said, "My dear Laksmana, because this demon cannot be killed in battle, let Us bury him alive. Quickly dig a big hole, while I keep My eye on him."

As Laksmana was digging, Viradha regained consciousness and said to Lord Rama, "Now I recognize You. In truth, I am the Gandharva, Tumburu. Because of lusting after the Apsara, Rambha, I was cursed by Kuvera to become a Raksasa until that time when You would deliver me. Please bury me in this hole, for in this way I will become free from that curse. Afterwards, You should go and meet the great sage, Sarabhanga, who lives about twenty kilometers from here, for he will give You some very beneficial advice."

As Laksmana continued digging, Lord Rama kept His foot on the Raksasa's neck. Finally, Lord Rama threw the screaming demon into the deep pit, and covered him over with big stones. In this way Tumburu gave up his Raksasa body and ascended to heaven, being relieved from Kuvera's curse.

As advised, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana set out to visit the great sage Sarabhanga. When They approached the sage's asrama, They were amazed to see King Indra sitting on his celestial chariot, the wheels of which did not touch the ground. Surrounded by numerous demigods, Indra was conversing with Sarabhanga, and at that time another celestial chariot came and waited in the sky. When he saw Lord Rama approach, Indra quickly ascended to the sky in his chariot drawn by 1000 horses, because he only wanted to meet the Lord after Ravana's death.

Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana hurriedly went and touched Sarabhanga's feet. After being welcomed by the sage, Lord Rama asked, "O great sage, if you are able to disclose the matter, please tell us the purpose of Indra's visit."

Sarabhanga replied, "Indra came here to take me to Brahmaloka, which is the destination I have earned by my severe penance, but I told the king of heaven that before departing from this world, I wanted to see You, knowing that You were nearby."

Later, when Lord Rama requested Sarabhanga to designate a place of residence for His period of exile, the sage advised Him to approach the great sage Sutiksna. Sarabhanga then said, "My dear Rama, I have a request that I beg You to kindly fulfill. The time has come for me to give up my material body and so I wish to do so in Your presence."

Sarabhanga built a fire and made it blaze brightly by pouring ghee. Then, while chanting mantras, the sage entered the fire and burned his body to ashes. Immediately, Sarabhanga was seen rising out of the fire. In a youthful and dazzlingly effulgent celestial body, he ascended to Brahmaloka, where he was welcomed by its inhabitants.

Thereafter, some rishis came to Lord Rama and said, "Many of us living here are being mercilessly killed by the Raksasas. You may come and see the dead bodies that are still lying about, here and there. O Lord Rama, we humbly beg You to give us protection."

Lord Rama assured the rishis, saying, "As a ksatriya, I am your servant, and so, for your sake, I will kill the Raksasas."

Lord Rama then proceeded to Sutiksna's asrama, in the company of those rishis.

Welcoming Them, Sutiksna said, "I have been awaiting Your visit, O descendent of Raghu. Before ascending to Brahmaloka, Indra came here to inform me of Your immanent arrival."

Later, when Lord Rama asked Sutiksna to designate a place for His residence, the sage offered Him the use of his own cottage. Lord Rama refused, however, saying, "If we were to remain here, our killing the deer would certainly be the cause of your unhappiness."

Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana spent the night at Sutiksna's asrama. The next morning, when Lord Rama took permission to depart, Sutiksna embraced Him and invited Him to return after visiting all the hermitages in the Dandaka forest.

Later in the day, as they were walking along the forest path, Sitadevi said, "My dear husband, due to the powerful influence of material nature, it is possible for even a great and noble man to gradually become degraded. Because of this, one should always be very careful to control his mind and senses so as to avoid the addictions that result from material desires. There are three sinful activities that should especially be avoided—false speech, sexual relations with another's wife, and cruelty toward those who are not inimical."

"In Your character there is not even a pinch of the first two faults, for You have never uttered a lie, and You could not even think of lusting after another's wife. But, my dear Lord, I see that You take the lives of so many innocent animals. Because of this, I can understand that there is still the contamination of enmity in You. I feel very unhappy when I see the poor animals being needlessly killed."

"My dear Lord, simply by carrying a bow in Your hands You increase the killing propensity, just as putting fuel too near a fire makes it blaze higher."

"Please listen as I tell You a story in this connection. Once there was a sage who performed such wonderful austerities that Indra became afraid of being overthrown. Desiring to obstruct the sage's advancement, Indra came before him in the guise of a warrior. While handing him a sword, Indra requested, 'Please keep this for me very carefully, until I return later on to take it back.'"

"The sage consented, and to keep his promise, he always kept the sword with him, even when he went to the forest to collect fruit and flowers. But, as a result, the sage's mind gradually became tinged with the desire for cruelty. At last, he gave up his execution of austerities to live a life of violence. In this way, the sage became so degraded that after death he went to hell for punishment."

"My dear husband, since that brahmana became degraded simply by the association of Indra's sword, I request You to keep Your bow in hand only for the purpose of killing the Raksasas harassing the rishis. Please do not kill even the innocent Raksasas, and so what to speak of other poor creatures. Of course, I am a foolish woman and so it is not my position to instruct You about religious principles. Still, if You think it fit, please give my words some thought and then do what You think is best."

Lord Rama replied, "Dear Sita, I very much appreciate your words, which are certainly full of wisdom. I know that it is only because you love Me that you have advised Me for My welfare. Unless one is dear, he is never given such frank council. O Janaki, the rishis could easily kill the Raksasas, but they do not do so, because it would diminish their stock of acquired ascetic merit. It is for this reas Dandaka forest. I might be able to renounce you, or even Laksmana, but I could never give up a promise made to the brahmanas."

Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana came to a large, heavenly lake. Mysteriously, the sound of singing could be heard coming from within the water, although no one was seen.

A sage named Dharmabhrt was present, and at Lord Rama's request, he explained, "This lake, known as Pancapsara, was originally created by the sage, Mandakarni, by utilizing his mystic power. Thereafter, he performed austerities on the shore of this lake for ten thousand years, while subsisting only upon air. Because of this, the demigods became very fearful, thinking that the sage might come to occupy one of their posts."

"To divert Mandakarni, the demigods deputed five Apsaras, and it so happened that the sage became captivated by their feminine attractions. After marrying them all, Mandakarni began residing with them in a secret house that he created within the water. The sound that you hear is the music of those Apsaras. Even to this very day they constantly serve the great sage, who has regained his youth by utilizing his ascetic prowess."

Thereafter, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana wandered from asrama to asrama within the Dandaka forest. Sometimes they would stay at an asrama for two weeks, sometimes for a month, and sometimes even longer, up to a year. In this way, ten years of Lord Rama's exile passed quite comfortably and contentedly. After visiting the asramas within the Dandaka forest, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana returned to Sutiksna's hermitage and continued living there.

One day, while conversing with Sutiksna, Lord Rama said, "I have heard that the great sage, Agastya, lives somewhere in Dandakaranya, but I could not find him within the vast forest. I would like to go and offer My respects to the sage, and receive his blessings in return. Kindly give Me directions."

Sutiksna replied, "I think that it is very good for You to go and see Agastya. I suggest that You set out this very day."

After taking directions from Sutiksna, Lord Rama departed, along with Sitadevi and Laksmana. When They came to the vicinity of Agastya's asrama, Lord Rama told the story of how the sage had formerly killed the Raksasa brothers, Vatapi and Ilvala.

As evening approached, They arrived at the asrama belonging to Agastya's brother, and so They decided to spend the night there. The next day, They arrived at Agastya's asrama, which was free from all disturbances because the Raksasas were afraid of him. Lord Rama sent Laksmana to announce His arrival. When a disciple informed him, Agastya commanded, "Invite Rama to come here at once, for I have been waiting in expectation for a long time."

When Lord Rama entered the asrama, He saw that sitting places were kept ready for receiving the demigods. Agastya came to greet Lord Rama, and with great reverence the Lord bowed down and touched the sage's feet. Agastya had Lord Rama seated. After the customary inquiries, he provided his guests with very nice food. When the meal was finished, Agastya took great pleasure in giving Lord Rama a bow of Lord Visnu that had been made by Visvakarma, as well as two inexhaustible quivers presented by lndra, an infallible arrow given by Brahma, and a gold-inlaid sword.

Turning to Sitadevi, Agastya said, "You have earned eternal glory by voluntarily undergoing great hardship for the sake of your husband. What you have done is especially meritorious because it has been the nature of women since the very dawn of creation to love a man only so long as he is prosperous."

When Lord Rama asked Agastya to suggest a place for His residence, the sage thought for awhile and then recommended Pancavati, about twenty kilometers away, near the Godavari River. Before Lord Rama's departure, Agastya confided, "I already know about Your entire life by my yogic perception, which has been enhanced by my affection for You."

While on the way to Pancavati, Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana came upon Jatayu, the gigantic king of the vultures. Thinking him to be a Raksasa, Lord Rama inquired about his identity.

With mild words, Jatayu replied, "My dear child, I was a good friend of Your father's. Long ago, Kasyapa married eight daughters of Prajapati Daksa. From Vinata, Aruna was born, and from Tamasa, Syeni was born. I am the son of Aruna and his wife, Syeni. My name is Jatayu."

"My dear Lord, I would like to offer myself as Your sincere servant. There are many fierce Raksasas living in this forest and so whenever You and Prince Laksmana leave Your cottage, I will watch over Sitadevi."

Due to Jatayu's relationship with His father, Lord Rama respectfully bowed to him. Jatayu then accompanied Lord Rama, Sitadevi and Laksmana to Pancavati, keeping a watchful eye all the while.

Upon their arrival, Lord Rama told Laksmana, "My dear brother, please select a site for building Our cottage. It should be near a lake or river, for water and greenery together create ideal scenic beauty."

Laksmana said, "My dear Lord, please select a place that is to Your liking, and then I will build a cottage."

Lord Rama picked out a spot near the shore of the Godavari, where there was a lake that was adorned with pink and blue lotus flowers. The land was flat and so it gave an unobstructed view of the surrounding area. The cries of swans and cakravakas could be heard, as well as those of peacocks, echoing from the caves of nearby hills. Laksmana built a nice cottage and after it was consecrated with flower offerings, Lord Rama went inside, feeling very pleased.

Gradually, winter set in, and so the morning sunshine became very pleasing to the sense of touch. One morning, when Lord Rama and Laksmana returned home after bathing in the river, They began conversing with one another as They prepared to perform Their morning religious duties.

At that time, a female Raksasa came there, and when she saw Lord Rama, her heart became infatuated with love for Him. With His dark, glowing complexion and lotus-petal eyes, Lord Rama appeared to be Kandarpa, the god of love himself, in human form. By contrast, the Raksasi had an extremely ugly face, protruding belly, deformed eyes, and copper-colored hair. Lord Rama's youthful body was well-proportioned, muscular, and bore all the signs of royalty, whereas the Raksasi was very odd-looking and on the brink of middle age.

Still, being pierced by Cupid's arrows, she approached Lord Rama and said, "My dear handsome one, please tell me who You are, and what Your purpose is in coming to the forest, dressed like a hermit."

In reply, Lord Rama briefly narrated the events leading up to His exile and then asked the Raksasi to identify herself. She said, "My name is Surpanakha, and I am the sister of Ravana, Kumbhakarna, Vibhisana, Khara and Dusana. I live in this forest and strike fear into the hearts of all creatures."

"To tell You quite frankly, Rama, I have become overpowered by love for You, and so I have made up my mind to have You as my husband. Give up Your ugly and deformed wife! She is not worthy of such a great hero as You! I am powerful and can travel at will, and so l am a suitable partner for You. First I will devour flat-bellied Sitadevi, as well as your brother Laksmana. Then we will be free to revel together in the hills and valleys of the beautiful Dandaka forest."

As Surpanakha looked at Him with love-intoxicated eyes, Lord Rama heartily laughed and jokingly replied, "I am a married man, and I am sure that a young, beautiful girl like you could not tolerate living with a co-wife. On the other hand, My brother is without a wife and is an even greater hero than I, and so I suggest that you marry Him."

Surpanakha took Lord Rama's words seriously. Leaving Him aside, she turned to Laksmana. Being saturated with lusty desires, she said, "My dear handsome hero, You appear to be more powerful and attractive than Your brother. Because of this, You are the most suitable husband for me. Come, make me Your wife, and we shall roam together through the forest, enjoying our loving affairs."

Laksmana smilingly replied, "O woman with soft skin and lovely limbs, you should know that I am simply a servant of My elder brother. If you marry Me, you will become no better than His maidservant. In My opinion, you would be much better off becoming Lord Rama's second wife. After some time, He will surely give up His aging and misshapen Sitadevi, and accept you alone. You have such a lovely face, which is decorated with sweet smiles, and your ample breasts, slender waist and wide hips make a man forget all other women. Who could resist you? Marry Lord Rama, and very soon He will cast aside His ugly wife."

Surpanakha was too simple-minded to understand that Lord Rama and Laksmana were joking. Once again she turned to Lord Rama and said, "You are too attached to ugly Sita and so I shall eat her at once and then take You by force as my husband!"

After saying this, Surpanakha rushed toward Sitadevi. Lord Rama restrained her while telling Laksmana, "You should not have joked with this Raksasi is such a way as to put Sita in danger. Now, disfigure her so that she will be taught a lesson!"

Receiving Lord Rama's order, Laksmana drew his sword and quickly sliced off Surpanakha's nose and ears. The Raksasi screamed horribly in pain. While bleeding profusely, she ran to the adjoining forest, called Janasthana, where her brother, Khara, was staying along with many other Raksasas. Smeared with blood and terribly frightened, Surpanakha threw herself at her brother's feet and bitterly wept. As Khara looked on, she incoherently tried to explain what had happened, and his heart became filled with horror and rage because of her disfigured appearance.

While breathing like a snake that had been trampled, Khara finally said, "My dear sister, get up and calm yourself! Tell me clearly what has happened! Who has dared to do this? Does he not realize that he drank poison by doing so? There is not a demigod or asura, Gandharva or sage, who can harm you and escape with his life! Just point out the culprit and I will drink his blood dry with my sharp arrows!"

While bitterly sobbing, Surpanakha managed to reply, "There are two brothers named Lord Rama and Laksmana staying nearby in the Dandaka forest. I do not know whether They are demigods or asuras, but because They have mutilated my face, I will become pacified only after drinking Their blood!"

Khara called for fourteen of the most powerful Raksasas and sent them, along with Surpanakha, to kill Lord Rama, Laksmana and Sitadevi. Lord Rama was sitting with Sitadevi in Their thatched cottage, while Laksmana guarded the door, when Surpanakha pointed Them out to the other Raksasas. Upon seeing the hideous man-eaters, Lord Rama ordered Laksmana, "Stay here with Sitadevi while I fight."

Coming outside, Lord Rama called out to the Raksasas, "We have come to this forest at the request of the rishis, who have sought Our protection from your atrocities. We are living a life of tapasya, and are eating only fruit and roots. Why have you come here to disturb Us? You had better turn back now, if you at all value your lives."

Surprised at being challenged so courageously, the Raksasas stared at Lord Rama with blood-red eyes and replied, "Our master, Khara, is angry with You, and so he has ordered us to kill You. How can You dare fight with so many of us? In a moment we will smash You with our weapons and You will be dead!"

After saying this, the Raksasas rushed at Lord Rama while brandishing their weapons, but with fourteen arrows, Lord Rama cut their weapons to pieces and then with fourteen more pierced their hearts. Like trees whose trunks had been severed, the Raksasas fell to the ground dead, bathed in their blood.

Shaking with fear, Surpanakha ran back to her brother and informed him of the slaughter. She said, "You tried to comfort me by sending others to kill Lord Rama and Laksmana, but it was the Raksasas who were killed and not Them. Now you must go and kill Lord Rama and Laksmana yourself. If you do not do so, then I will give up my life out of shame!"

After saying this, Surpanakha fell to the ground, overwhelmed with grief. While beating her belly, she burst into tears. Khara became enraged while listening to his sister's harsh words, and so he vowed, "Give up your sorrow! I promise to kill Rama and Laksmana this very day, so that you will be able to drink Their blood!"

At this, Surpanakha became somewhat pacified. Khara ordered his brother, Dusana, to bring his chariot and weapons and assemble all the 14,000 Raksasas.

As Khara proceeded toward the Dandaka forest, along with the army of Raksasas, dark clouds began to shower rain mixed with blood. Khara's horses accidentally stumbled and fell down on the road. A vulture came and perched on Khara's flagpole, jackals began howling frightfully, and darkness set in because of a solar eclipse, although it was not the proper time for such an occurrence. All the while, Khara's left arm trembled and his voice became hoarse and faint.

And yet, in spite of witnessing these ominous omens, Khara simply laughed and said, "Because of my great prowess, I do not care for any of these so-called inauspicious signs!"

Meanwhile, all the demigods and celestial rishis assembled in the sky to witness the impending battle. As the Raksasas approached, Lord Rama described to Laksmana the auspicious omens that He could see, saying, "My arrows are feeling joyful, My bow is stirring with delight, and My right arm is throbbing. My dear brother, take Sita at once to a mountain cave and watch over her carefully as I massacre these Raksasas."

After the departure of Sitadevi and Laksmana, Lord Rama put on His blazing golden armor. As the Raksasas advanced, roaring ferociously, Lord Rama summoned His all-devouring anger and assumed a form that was very dreadful to see. While his army surrounded Lord Rama, showering their clubs, spears, swords and axes, Khara released 1000 arrows. Lord Rama cut all these weapons to pieces, and although He was deeply pierced and bleeding, He did not appear to feel any pain.

Lord Rama proceeded to release thousands of arrows at the Raksasas, cutting to pieces their bows, flags, shields, armor and bodily limbs. Innumerable Raksasas fell down dead, along with their horses and elephants, and many of their chariots were smashed to pieces. Becoming more enraged, the remaining Raksasas countered by showering their weapons with greater enthusiasm. Lord Rama easily cut these weapons to pieces, however, while at the same time piercing many Raksasas' hearts. As numerous grotesque heads became severed from their gigantic trunks, the remaining Raksasas began fleeing to the shelter of Khara.

Dusana suddenly rushed at Lord Rama in great anger, and others followed, being encouraged by his exhibition of valor. Lord Rama invoked the best of Gandharva weapons, causing many thousands of arrows to stream forth from His bow, covering the sun and thus creating darkness. Thousands of Raksasas were cut to pieces so that their dismembered corpses lay in heaps.

Dusana then rallied his army, urging the remaining 5000 Raksasas to attack Lord Rama. In the fighting that followed, Dusana fought heroically, and when Lord Rama saw that His arrows were being successfully counteracted, He became furious. Picking up a blazing arrow, Lord Rama cut Dusana's bow in half. Then, with four more arrows, He killed the Raksasa's horses. With another arrow, Lord Rama killed Dusana's driver, and with three more He pierced him in the chest.

Although severely pained, Dusana grabbed a spiked club, and after jumping from his chariot, he rushed impetuously at Lord Rama. However, with two arrows, Lord Rama severed Dusana's arms, and then with one more He took away the Raksasa's life.

The remainder of Dusana's army rushed at Lord Rama, hoping for revenge. Working at the speed of mind, Lord Rama released 5000 arrows in a steady stream, like so many blazing meteors, so that 5000 slain Raksasa warriors soon lay upon the ground. Upon seeing this, Khara ordered his few remaining soldiers to attack, but Lord Rama made short work of them as well. Thus, the Dandaka forest became a sea of reddish mud, due to the blood of the 14,000 slain Raksasas.

Only Khara, Trisira, and a few others remained alive. As Khara prepared to attack, his general, Trisira, begged for permission to fight first. Khara gave his consent and so the three-headed Raksasa approached Lord Rama while showering his arrows. When some arrows pierced His forehead, Lord Rama became angered and retaliated by killing Trisira's horses and driver, and knocking down his flag. Then, as Trisira attempted to jump from his disabled chariot, Lord Rama pierced him in the heart with a fiery arrow, and cut off his heads with three more.

Khara rallied the surviving Raksasas and fearfully attacked Lord Rama. During the fierce battle that followed, the sun became invisible as their streams of arrows completely covered the sky. Bringing his chariot close by, Khara dexterously broke Lord Rama's bow in half. Khara then shot 1000 arrows that shattered Lord Rama's armor, making the pieces fall to the ground.

Although His limbs were deeply pierced, Lord Rama calmly strung the bow of Lord Visnu. Lord Rama then cut down Khara's emblem, but the Raksasa retaliated by wounding Him severely. Becoming enraged, Lord Rama quickly released six arrows that pierced Khara's head, arms and chest. Then, with thirteen more, Lord Rama killed his horses, severed his driver's head, broke his bow, and smashed his chariot. The last of these arrows, which was as dazzling as lightning, pierced Khara in the chest, making him fall backwards, unconscious.

Khara quickly recovered, jumped from his broken chariot, and stood before Lord Rama, club in hand. Lord Rama chastised Khara, saying, "You cruel and sinful Raksasa, because you have dedicated your life to giving pain to others, you are thoroughly condemned. To punish you, I will certainly cut off your head this very hour!"

The enraged Khara replied, "You vile wretch! Those who are actually heroes do not boast of their prowess!"

Having said this, Khara hurled his gigantic mace at Lord Rama. That blazing club burned to ashes all the trees and bushes that stood in its path, and yet Lord Rama's arrows broke it to pieces as it soared through the air. Once again, Lord Rama and Khara exchanged harsh words, and then the Raksasa uprooted a tall sala tree. After whirling it around, Khara hurled it at Lord Rama, while shouting, "You are killed!" However, Lord Rama cut that huge tree to pieces.

Then, desiring to put an end to his adversary, Lord Rama released 1000 arrows. Although blood poured profusely from every part of his body, Khara made one last attempt by impetuously rushing at Lord Rama. Lord Rama selected a blazing arrow, and when it pierced Khara's chest, making him burst into flames, the Raksasa dropped dead onto the ground.

The demigods were overjoyed to witness Lord Rama's victory, and while beating their drums and showering flowers they glorified the Lord for His astonishing feat of killing all the Raksasas in just one and a half hours.

Great rishis, headed by Agastya, then appeared before Lord Rama and disclosed, "It was just to arrange for You to come here and kill the Raksasas that Indra had visited Sarabhanga. It is for this reason that the sage had advised You to come and reside here."

Sitadevi and Laksmana came out from Their cave. When she saw that Lord Rama was safe and sound, Sitadevi ran and embraced Him with great joy. Meanwhile, a Raksasa named Akampana, who had escaped the massacre, went to Lanka and told Ravana about the death of his two brothers and their followers.

Ravana became red with rage upon receiving this news. With great agitation he shouted, "Who has dared to offend me in this way, bringing about his own death? No one can save him now, not even Indra, Yama or Visnu! Tell me at once—who has dared make me angry?"

Akampana was very afraid of Ravana's wrath and so he begged, "My lord, I will gladly answer your questions, but please promise that you will not become angry with me for my truthful speech."

When Ravana gave him assurances, Akampana said, "The 14,000 Raksasas, including your two brothers, were killed by a human being named Rama, the son of King Dasaratha."

Ravana inquired, "Was this Rama accompanied by all the demigods?"

Akampana replied, "Oh no, my lord! Rama killed all the Raksasas single-handedly, without even taking help from His younger brother, Laksmana. Rama's prowess is so formidable that He accomplished the task in a very short time. No one could stand before Him, and when He became enraged, it appeared as if the entire universe would be destroyed!"

Ravana angrily declared, "I shall go to Janasthana at once and kill this Rama and Laksmana. Then we will see what kind of great heroes They are!"

For the benefit of his master, Akampana warned, "Please do not act hastily due to underestimating Rama's prowess. He is capable of annihilating the entire cosmic manifestation and then recreating it. Even if all the demigods and asuras combined together, I do not think that Rama could be killed by them. Therefore, do not consider attacking Him with force. I have thought of another means whereby you can insure Rama's death. His wife, Sitadevi, is incomparably beautiful, and her face is lovelier than thousands of moons. She is the very emblem of feminine attractiveness and besides this, she is the perfection of womanly chastity and behavior. I think that without Sita, Rama could no longer bear to live and so I suggest that you go and kidnap her."

Ravana immediately liked this idea. After thinking it over for some time, he said, "Your plan is brilliant! I shall go on my chariot to Pancavati tomorrow and carry away Sita by force with great pleasure."

After dismissing Akampana, Ravana went to the hermitage of Tataka's son, Marica. After receiving Ravana with great respect and washing his feet, Marica said, "O lord of the Raksasas, your surprise visit here fills my heart with misgivings. I know that you would not have personally come here unless the matter was very serious."

Ravana replied, "What you say is correct. A human being named Rama has completely annihilated my army posted at Janasthana. His brother, Laksmana, disfigured Surpanakha, and so Khara and Dusana tried to retaliate. I had considered my two brothers to be invincible, but they have been killed, along with all their soldiers. My dear Marica, I need your help, because I want to kidnap Rama's wife, Sita."

With great shock and horror, Marica said, "Whoever has given you this idea is actually your worst enemy in the guise of a friend. O Ravana, if you try to carry out this foolish plan, it will only result in your destruction, for Lord Rama has inconceivable prowess, on the level of Lord Visnu. Lord Rama is like a fathomless ocean. Crocodiles are His bow and whirlpools are His mighty arms. The releasing of His arrows is the constant flowing of the waves, and the span from shore to shore is the battlefield wherein His enemies drown."

"O King of the Raksasas, you had better control your anger and return to Lanka. Enjoy yourself there, along with your wives, and let Lord Rama enjoy His wife in the Dandaka forest. Otherwise, you will unnecessarily bring disaster down upon your head!"

Ravana took Marica's advice and returned to his magnificent palace at Lanka.

Meanwhile, after witnessing the great slaughter of Raksasas, Surpanakha went to see her brother. Ravana had ten heads and twenty arms, and his body bore many scars from former, conflicts, including those that had been inflicted by Lord Visnu's Sudarsana cakra.

Once, Ravana attacked Bhogavati, and after defeating Vasuki and Taksaka, he took away the latter's wife by force. After conquering Kuvera, Ravana took possession of the Puspaka chariot. Out of spite, he destroyed some of the heavenly gardens, such as Nandana Forest.

Long ago, Ravana had performed severe austerities, and at the completion of 10,000 years, he began offering his ten heads in sacrifice to Lord Brahma. Being pleased with Ravana, Lord Brahma offered him the benediction of being immune to death at the hands of all kinds of living entities, except human beings. After receiving this benediction, puffed-up Ravana began killing brahmanas and disrupting their sacrifices. Thus he became a thorn in the side of the demigods and so they fervently prayed to Lord Visnu for his destruction.

Surpanakha came before Ravana and showed him her disfigured face. He was seated upon a golden throne within his seven-story palace.

With great agitation, Surpanakha said, "My dear brother, have you become so absorbed in sense gratification that you fail to recognize the grave danger at hand? When a king is interested only in his own vulgar enjoyment, and does not attend to state affairs, everyone comes to despise him. Haven't your spies told you how Rama single-handedly killed 14,000 Raksasas at Janasthana, including your two brothers? You are a useless king, and so I predict that you will not remain on the throne for long!"

Ravana became mad with rage while listening to his sister goad him in front of his ministers. Still, he controlled his anger, and after thinking a moment, he inquired, "Who is this Rama, and what is His strength? Was it He that deformed you like this? Speak, for I want to know everything!"

Surpanakha replied, "With long, powerful arms, and large eyes like lotus petals, Rama appears to be the god of love himself. Although He is the most heroic ksatriya, He has dressed in deerskin and tree bark, and keeps matted hair. Rama's younger brother is named Laksmana, and it is He who cut off my nose and ears, under His brother's instruction."

" Rama's wife is named Sita, and she exactly resembles the goddess of fortune. She has large, dark eyes, and the beauty of her face surpasses that of many, many moons. Her smooth skin has the color of molten gold, and her slim waist, graceful hips and full breasts make her incomparably beautiful."

"O Ravana, there is no other woman like Sita. I know that if you were to see her, you would fall madly in love. She would make a perfect wife for you. I wanted to capture Sita and bring her to you, but when I tried to do so, Laksmana disfigured me. My dear brother, you should go and kidnap Sita, if you are really the powerful hero you make yourself out to be!"

After hearing this, Ravana became firmly determined to possess Sita. Once again he mounted his chariot, and after crossing the sea, he came to Marica's asrama. Marica, who was dressed like a sage and engaged in performing austerities, received Ravana very respectfully and inquired, "O King, what is the reason for your early return to my humble residence?

Ravana replied, "After hearing about Lord Rama's slaughter of the Raksasas at Janasthana, I have not found a moment's peace. I have made up my mind to kidnap Sita, and I want you to help me."

"For this purpose I have devised a very clever plan. I want you to take the form of a golden deer with silver spots and then play in front of Sita. She will surely become captivated by such a cute and wonderful creature and want to have it as her pet. As you proceed to lead Rama astray, I will go and kidnap the unprotected Sita and take her back to Lanka. Thereafter, when Rama becomes aggrieved and emaciated due to separation from His beloved wife, I will easily be able to kill Him."

Upon hearing the name, Rama, Marica became very fearful, and as he thought about Ravana's plan, his mouth became dry. Staring with unblinking eyes, Marica addressed Ravana with folded hands, "O lord, if you go ahead with your scheme, it will result in the destruction of Lanka and all the Raksasas. Because of your lusty nature, and your ignorance of Lord Rama's prowess, you are heedlessly rushing toward your doom. Please hear from me attentively about Lord Rama's supreme potency before you blindly bring about your own destruction."

"Previously, I wandered over the earth with club in hand, being very proud of my superhuman strength. I subsisted upon the flesh of the rishis in the Dandaka forest. Out of fear, Visvamitra approached Maharaja Dasaratha, to solicit Lord Rama's help for protecting his sacrificial performance."

"Thereafter, when I came to disrupt the sacrifice, Lord Rama hurled me into the ocean with just one arrow. Having been spared by Lord Rama, I returned to Lanka, but still I was not cured of my pride. Taking the form of a large, carnivorous stag with a flaming tongue, I returned to the Dandaka forest to roam about, drinking the rishis' blood."

"Then, once again, I came upon Lord Rama, who was wandering in the forest, along with Sitadevi and Laksmana. Considering how He had adopted a life of renunciation, I became determined to gain revenge. However, when I rushed at Him, Lord Rama released three arrows, instantly killing my two companions. I fled with my life, and I think that I was spared only because Lord Rama's arrows do not kill those who run away from the battlefield."

"Ever since that time, I have been obsessed with fear of Lord Rama, and as a result, my inclination for violence and fighting has vanished. Having given up all malice toward others, I have taken to an ascetic life of yoga practice. Still, I have failed to obtain peace of mind, for whenever my glance happens to fall upon a tree, I think that it is Lord Rama, dressed in tree bark. Thus, the entire forest appears to have become transformed into innumerable Lord Ramas, and because of this, wherever I look I feel terrified. Sometimes I see Lord Rama in my dreams, and so I immediately wake up, my heart thumping with terror. I have become so afraid of Lord Rama that whenever I hear a word beginning with the letter 'R,' such as ratha or ratna, my heart trembles."

"O King, for your welfare, as well as mine, I advise you to forget about kidnapping Sitadevi. Aside from fearing Lord Rama, why should you perform such a sinful act? There is no offense more punishable than the abduction of another's wife. Remain satisfied with your thousands of wives and thus save your dignity, fortune, kingdom, and life itself."

After listening very patiently, Ravana replied, "O Marica, I did not ask for your advice, but only for your help in kidnapping Sita. As my minister, it is your duty to give advice only when asked, and to obey my orders without question. I have made up my mind and nothing can deter me. I want you to take the form of a deer and charm Sita."

"Then, when she begs Lord Rama to capture you, lead Him deep into the forest and call out, "O Sita! O Laksmana!" When He thinks that His brother is in difficulty, Laksmana will leave Sita alone to go help Him. Just perform this little service, and in return I will reward you with half my kingdom. On the other hand, if you refuse, I will kill you this very day! This is your choice, Marica, either certain death at my hands, or possible death at the hands of Lord Rama. Make up your mind!"

Marica boldly said, "Flatterers are easy to come by, but rare is the person who speaks unpleasant words that are meant for one's benefit. A minister should always give good advice, even if uncalled for, especially when the king becomes misguided. Unfortunately, it is inevitably seen that one who is about to die is never willing to take good advice."

"O King, if we carry out your plan, then I am certain that both of us will end up dead. Still, I would rather die at the hands of an exalted enemy on the battlefield than be killed here by you, so let us depart at once."
