Being very pleased with Marica for his cooperation, Ravana embraced him. Thereafter, the two mounted upon Ravana's aerial chariot and departed. After passing over numerous forests, rivers, towns and fields, they landed in the Dandaka forest, near to where Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana were staying.

After getting down from the chariot, Marica transformed himself into a wonderful deer and began playfully prancing back and forth in front of Lord Rama's cottage. The golden deer had numerous jewel-like silver spots, and the tips of its horns were like sapphires. Its mouth appeared like a pinkish lotus flower, its tail was like a rainbow, and its ears were bright blue. All in all, the magical deer seemed to have been constructed entirely of valuable jewels. Having a dazzling luster, the deer illuminated the entire area surrounding the cottage. Sometimes it nibbled at the grass, and sometimes it frolicked among the trees.

Sitadevi was gathering flowers in the groves of mango, asoka and karnikara trees as Marica in the form of a deer leapt, ran, and crouched, being eager to attract her attention. Catching the scent of the Raksasa in disguise, the other deer quickly fled into the forest, helter-skelter. When the magical deer suddenly came close by, bounding in front of her eyes, Sitadevi looked at it with wonder and enchantment.

Sitadevi had never before seen such a wonderful deer, and with wide-open eyes she called out, "My dear Lord, Laksmana, come here at once!"

When Laksmana came and saw the deer, He said, "It's a trick! This must be Marica the Raksasa disguised as a deer, seeking revenge. He is known to take such a form, just to waylay kings who come to the forest to hunt."

Sitadevi interrupted Laksmana, because her intelligence had been deluded by the deer's beauty. She said to Lord Rama, "O dear husband, please go quickly and catch this deer, for it has captivated my fancy. Such a lovely pet would make me happy by providing a diversion from our dreary forest life. If you can catch this deer alive, I will take it back to Ayodhya at the end of our exile, for it will delight Bharata and our mothers. Please bring the deer for me to play with. It is so lovely, and its smooth skin shines like the moon."

"My dear husband, if You cannot capture the deer alive, then kill it, so that I can make a rug out of its jewel-like skin. You must think that I am a foolish woman, but I must have this deer, for I am enchanted by its beauty."

Lord Rama was delighted to receive the chance to fulfill Sitadevi's wish, and He was also enchanted by the deer's mysterious beauty. He told Laksmana, "Sita is enthralled, and so I must bring her this deer. Look at its tongue, darting about like a flame, or like lightning in a cloud. Nowhere can such a deer be found, even in the heavenly Nandana or Caitraratha gardens. For sure, this deer's splendor will cost it its life. I will make a carpet from its spotted golden skin so that Sita and I can enjoy sitting together on it. And, if, as you say, the deer is actually Marica, then killing it will be beneficial for the rishis residing in the forest. O Laksmana, until My return, stay here with Your bow in hand and guard Sita carefully."

Having said this, Lord Rama picked up a sword, bow and two quivers, whereupon Marica suddenly vanished from sight. As Lord Rama entered the forest, the deer once again came into view, running away swiftly and looking backward. Then again, the deer came very close to Lord Rama, as if tempting Him to capture it. However, as Lord Rama ran toward the deer, it once again disappeared from sight. In this way, by repeatedly becoming visible and then again disappearing from view, Marica lured Lord Rama far away from His cottage. Confused by the deer's puzzling movements, Lord Rama felt helpless and frustrated.

Finally, having become exhausted by the chase, Lord Rama rested awhile beneath a tree. Then, once again, the deer suddenly appeared close by, but when Lord Rama got up and tried to catch it, the deer mysteriously vanished. Out of frustration, Lord Rama gave up the idea of capturing the deer alive. When the deer next appeared at some distance, Lord Rama shot a blazing arrow of Lord Brahma, hoping to kill it. Marica tried to jump high to avoid Lord Rama's arrow, but it nonetheless pierced his heart. As Marica fell to the ground, mortally wounded, he assumed his real form as a Raksasa. Then, remembering Ravana's instructions, Marica imitated Lord Rama's voice and called out in great distress, "O devi! O Laksmana!"

Marica gave up his life, and Lord Rama became very dejected upon hearing these words, wondering, "What will Sitadevi and Laksmana think?" In fact, a terrible fear entered Lord Rama's heart, and so He immediately began to rush back to His cottage.

Meanwhile, when Sitadevi heard Marica's voice, she thought that Lord Rama must be calling for help. Becoming panicky with fear, she turned to Laksmana and urged, "Go quickly and find out what has happened! That was My Lord crying out for help! He needs You! O Laksmana, my heart is throbbing and I can hardly breathe! The Raksasas must have overwhelmed Him!"

Still, Laksmana kept Lord Rama's order to guard Sitadevi firmly in mind and so He did not even stir. This apparent indifference made Sitadevi more upset and so she chastised Laksmana with very harsh words, "Why do You just stand there? Do You want My Lord to die? Now I can understand that You are actually the enemy of Your elder brother in the guise of a friend. I think that You would like My Lord to die, so that You can be free to enjoy me as You like! Otherwise, You would quickly rush to His rescue! Co quickly, Laksmana! What is the use of my remaining alive and safe when my husband is in danger?"

Unto Sitadevi, who was sobbing and trembling with fear, Laksmana replied, "Try to control yourself! Lord Rama cannot be harmed by anyone—not by any Raksasa or even Indra and all the demigods. Do not talk like this! The voice you heard was the conjuring trick of Marica, just to frighten us. Lord Rama ordered Me to protect you, and so I will stay here and obey Him."

Sitadevi had become practically deranged due to fright. Laksmana's words of pacification aroused hatred and anger within her heart. Her eyes became red with rage.

Sitadevi raved, "You shameless, wicked man! I think that You are enjoying Lord Rama's misfortune. Otherwise, why would You speak so calmly? I can now understand that all along You were simply pretending to be Your elder brother's humble servant. The real reason You accompanied My Lord to the forest was to look for an opportunity to kill Him and then fulfill Your lusty desires to enjoy me."

"Maybe You are Bharata's agent! In any case, you will never obtain the fulfillment of Your sinful desires! Do You actually think that I would accept You after having been the wife of my lotus-eyed Lord? I would rather die! Without my husband, I could not bear to live for even a moment!"

His heart pierced as if by arrows, horrified Laksmana replied with folded hands, "O Princess of Mithila, you are like a deity to Me, and so I cannot speak harshly in reply. I know that it is the nature of women to create discord between friends. For sure, women are so fickle and hard-hearted that when they become obsessed with a desire, they give up all sense of morality."

"O daughter of King Janaka, you have goaded Me with your sharp words and so I shall go to Lord Rama, as you demand, but because I can see terrible omens, foreboding great evil, I am afraid that when I return with Him, We will no longer find you here."

With great agitation, Sitadevi responded, "If my Lord is killed, I will jump from a cliff, drown myself, or take poison, for I would rather die than be touched by another man!"

Laksmana had become very angry while listening to Sitadevi's harsh and cruel words. Although He dutifully tried to console her, Laksmana was actually very eager to see Lord Rama, and so as Sitadevi continued to malign Him, Laksmana departed at last, giving Ravana his eagerly-awaited opportunity.

Dressed in saffron cloth, his hair tied into a knot on top of his head, wearing wooden sandals, and carrying an umbrella on his right shoulder and a staff and kamandalu (water pot) on his left, Ravana came before Sitadevi in the guise of a wandering mendicant. Sitadevi was sitting inside the cottage, shedding tears of grief, as Ravana appeared in the doorway.

Out of fear, the wind ceased blowing, the leaves on the trees stopped fluttering, and the waters of the Godavari became hushed. While chanting Vedic mantras, Ravana approached Sitadevi, and while staring at her, his heart became pierced by Cupid's arrows.

In the hopes of charming Sitadevi, Ravana said, "Who are you, lovely lady, residing alone within this terrible forest? Are you the goddess of modesty, Hri; the goddess of fame, Kirti; the goddess of mystic powers, Bhuti; or the goddess of love, Rati? Or, are you Laksmidevi herself, bereft of your lotus flower?"

"Your smooth white teeth are like a row of jasmine buds, and your dark eyes resemble two bumblebees that hover over your lotus-like face. Tapering and graceful are your thighs, like the trunks of baby elephants, and your hips are rounded and ample. Your firm and abundant breasts seem to touch one another, and their nipples are pointed and prominent. Beneath them is a waist so slim that a thumb and index finger can encircle it perfectly."

"No mortal or even celestial woman can equal your beauty, which has ravished my heart like a swelling river that overflows its banks. Why do you remain here when you could prosper somewhere else? You should live in a palace, dressed in the finest clothes and jewelry and attended by countless servants. O sweet- smiling one, choose a worthy husband and leave this jungle full of ferocious animals."

Although spoken to so boldly, Sitadevi offered her guest a nice reception, for she did not want to offend a brahmana. After offering Ravana a seat and water to wash his feet, Sitadevi gave him some food and explained, "My dear sir, my name is Sita, and I am the daughter of the noble-minded King Janaka of Mithila. At the age of nine I was married to my beloved husband. Later on, at the urging of his wife, Queen Kaikeyi, my father-in-law, Maharaja Dasaratha, installed her son Bharata upon the royal throne and banished Lord Rama to the forest for fourteen years."

"Now, please tell me your name and ancestry. O brahmana, why have you come alone to the Dandaka forest?"

The so-called brahmana replied, "I am Ravana, the King of the Raksasas, and even the great demigods tremble at the mere mention of my name. O faultlessly beautiful one, now that I have seen you, I could never again take delight in my other innumerable consorts. O Sita, become my principal queen and you will have 5000 maidservants awaiting your commands. Leave this forest and enjoy with me in the beautiful gardens of Lanka."

Sitadevi became very angry while listening to Ravana. With great contempt she replied, "I am completely devoted to my husband, who is as steady as a rock, as grave as an ocean, and sheltering like a banyan tree. I am devoted to him, who has mighty arms and a broad chest, whose face resembles the full moon, and who is self-controlled and virtuous."

"He is a lion among men, and you, who are like a jackal, want to possess me, a lioness. Would you dare touch the sun or lift Mount Mandara? Would you pierce your eyes with a needle or carry a blazing fire in your clothes? Would you attempt to swim across the ocean with a boulder tied around your neck? Then why do you dare consider running away with Lord His wife?"

"My Lord is like Garuda, and you are but a crow. He is the ocean, and you, a small puddle. He is sandalwood and you are mud. He is like gold and you are like iron. He is like a swan and you are no better than a vulture. You may kidnap me, but as long as my Lord lives, you will no more enjoy me than a fly enjoys the ghee it flounders in."

Although she spoke boldly, Sitadevi was shaking with fear. To further intimidate her, Ravana said, "Listen to me, lovely lady. I am the half-brother of Kuvera, the god of wealth. After vanquishing him, I took away his Puspaka chariot, and now he hides from me somewhere near Mount Kailasa. All the demigods are afraid of me and wherever I go, the sunshine becomes like moonshine, the wind calms itself, and rivers stop flowing. Forget Lord Rama, for He is a mere mortal who is doomed to perish soon. Come with me and enjoy heavenly delights. I passionately yearn for you and so please do not reject me."

Sitadevi replied, "If you are actually Kuvera's brother, then why do you act with such wicked intentions? The ravisher of Indra's wife may survive, but whoever tries to molest me is doomed!"

Finally, Ravana lost all patience. In a fit of rage he revealed his gigantic form as a fierce Raksasa having ten heads, twenty arms and sharp teeth. He then declared, "If you wish to have a husband who is renowned throughout the three worlds, then accept me. Serve me, and I shall never displease you. Why are you so fond of someone who has been banished to the forest?"

After a momentary pause, Ravana quickly reached out with his left hand and grabbed Sitadevi by the hair. As if summoned, Ravana's golden chariot came close by. Placing his right hand on Sitadevi's thigh, Ravana took her in his arms, mounted the chariot and ascended into the sky. Wailing like one injured or mad, Sitadevi called out, "O my dear Lord Why don't you come here and save me? You must punish this wretched Ravana!"

In desperation, Sitadevi next called out to the trees, the river, the birds and the animals, begging them to inform Lord Rama of her abduction. Sitadevi then spotted Jatayu, who was perched upon a tree, sleeping. She cried out, "Jatayu! I know that you cannot defeat Ravana, but please tell my Lord that I have been kidnapped by him."

Jatayu awoke upon hearing Sitadevi's calls. When he saw how Ravana was carrying her away, he challenged the king of the Raksasas, "O Ravana, I am Jatayu, the king of the vultures. How is it that you dare to lay your hands upon the wife of another? Beware! You are carrying a poisonous snake in your arms. I have become old, whereas you are young and armed with weapons. Still, I will not let you carry away Sitadevi. I warn you! Give up your evil intention or else be thrown down from your chariot like an over-ripe fruit falling from a tree."

Being challenged, Ravana angrily rushed at Jatayu. As they began to fight, it appeared as if two mountains were striking one another. Ravana struck Jatayu with many weapons, and the giant vulture wounded the Raksasa king with his sharp talons. Although pierced by numerous arrows, the sight of Sitadevi crying while seated upon Ravana's chariot enabled Jatayu to tolerate the pain.

As he attacked Ravana, Jatayu warded off the onslaught of arrows with his wings and then managed to break the Raksasa's bow with his feet. Having gained this advantage, Jatayu cut off Ravana's armor and then killed the mules yoked to his chariot. Heroic Jatayu proceeded to smash Ravana's chariot to pieces while simultaneously striking off the driver's head with his beak. As a result, Ravana fell to the ground while tightly holding Sitadevi in his arms. All who witnessed this wonderful display of prowess applauded Jatayu with great enthusiasm.

However, due to his age, Jatayu became exhausted. Ravana once again rose up into the sky by the strength of his own prowess, holding Sitadevi in one arm while wielding a sword with the other. Jatayu suddenly swooped down on Ravana from the back and began pulling his hair so forcibly that the Raksasa's lips quivered with indignation. Keeping Sitadevi pressed tightly to his left thigh, Ravana struck back at Jatayu with his palm. Jatayu dodged the blow, however, and proceeded to tear off Ravana's twenty arms with his powerful beak. Ravana's arms were immediately replaced with new ones, and after leaving Sitadevi aside, he began striking Jatayu with his fists and feet.

The fighting continued in this way for about an hour. Finally, in desperation, Ravana took out his sword and swiftly cut off Jatayu's wings, feet and flanks, making him fall to the ground, fatally wounded. Greatly distressed, Sitadevi ran to where Jatayu lay and embraced him as tears came to her eyes. Ravana quickly went and seized her by the hair.

When Sitadevi cried out, "O my dear Lord" the whole universe went out of order, so that all became enveloped in darkness. Due to empathy with Sitadevi's grief, the wind no longer blew and the sun became lusterless. Lord Brahma, however, who could perceive the entire incident through divine eyes, declared, "Our purpose is now accomplished!"

As Ravana rose up to the sky with Sitadevi, her ornaments broke apart, causing jewels to fall to the ground. The pearls from her necklace slipped from her breast like the pure water of the Ganga falling from the sky. Sitadevi was overwhelmed with fear and grief, and while continuously struggling to get free, she reproached Ravana again and again for his vile and cowardly act. As Ravana sped to his destination, all creatures seemed to lament, "There is no dharma, there is no truth, there is no gentleness."

Her loosened hair waving in the wind, her mark of tilaka erased, and her face without cheer, Sitadevi moaned, " O dear Lord, O Laksmana."

Sitadevi then happened to see five monkey chiefs sitting on top of a mountain. Unnoticed by Ravana, she took off her silk upper garment and some jewelry and dropped them in the midst of the monkeys, hoping that they would give these things to Lord Rama. As the monkeys stared at him with unblinking eyes, Ravana continued to carry Sitadevi towards Lanka.

After crossing the ocean, the Raksasa king took Sitadevi into the inner apartments of his palace. There, he ordered the fierce female Raksasa attendants, "I want you to watch over this woman very carefully, and do not let anyone see her without my permission. Provide her with the very best clothing, jewelry and food, and give her gold, pearls—whatever she wants. But, take heed of this warning—anyone who utters so much as one harsh word to Sita will die by my order!"

Ravana called for eight powerful Raksasas and told them, "Ever since Lord Rama killed my brothers and their army at Janasthana, I have felt an intolerable enmity toward Him. In fact, I will not be able to sleep peacefully at night until Lord Rama is dead. Co now and spy on Him, and bring back to me all the information you gather."

Because of his previous experience with women, Ravana foolishly felt happy now that he had Sitadevi. After dispatching these Raksasas, he went to see her, being overwhelmed by desire for her. Ravana saw Sitadevi in a very distressed condition, surrounded by numerous Raksasis. Bathed in her tears, Sitadevi appeared like a wind-buffeted boat at sea, or a doe that had strayed from its herd while being pursued by dogs. Although she was unwilling, Ravana forced Sitadevi to accompany him on a tour of his palace.

As she viewed the spacious and fabulously decorated rooms, containing thousands of women, Ravana explained, "Lanka is inhabited by 320 million Raksasas, excluding the aged and infants, and 1000 of these are my personal servants. If you are wise, lovely Sita, you will fulfill my ardent desire by becoming my principal queen. Become my master and enjoy all this opulence you see before you. I am tormented by love for you and I shall be devoted to you alone. Please grant me your favor and look upon me with affection. After all, youthful beauty is fleeting. You should enjoy life with me and forget insignificant Lord Rama."

While covering her moon-like face, Sitadevi shed silent tears. Ravana continued, "Do not be afraid that our union goes against religious principles. I love you more than life itself. I fall at your feet and beg you to grant me your favor. Let this plea that has arisen from love's anguish not go in vain. Never before has Ravana bowed before a woman."

Ravana thought that by speaking of his love for her, Sitadevi would soon be won over. However, Sitadevi fearlessly replied, "My heart is devoted to my husband without deviation, and to Him alone. Why should I, a swan sporting with her mate within a lotus-filled lake, prefer a duck meandering on the shore? You can do whatever you like to me, but rest assured that because of your vile and sinful lust, you will soon meet with death at the hands of my Lord."

Being harshly rebuked, Ravana became angry and said, "I will give you just twelve months to surrender to me. At the end of that time, if you still resist, I will have my cooks cut you into pieces and serve you as my breakfast."

Turning to his attendants, Ravana ordered, "Take Sita to the asoka grove. Watch her very carefully and try to win her over to my side by whatever means required. Threaten her, flatter her, do whatever it takes, but tame her as one would an elephant."

Sitadevi went to live in the asoka grove, which was a beautiful garden filled with trees bearing fruit and flowers. She was already thoroughly miserable, and as the deformed Raksasa women continued to intimidate her, she fainted due to fright.

Meanwhile, Lord Brahma summoned Indra and said, "Mother Sita has been kidnapped by Ravana and taken to Lanka. This is our good fortune, because it will surely bring about the destruction of the Raksasa king. Still, there is the danger that Sitadevi may die due to separation from Lord Rama, and so I want you to go and give her this heavenly sweet-rice to eat."

Indra went to the asoka grove, along with Nidra-devi, the goddess of sleep. After Nidra had woven her spell, putting all the Raksasi guards to sleep, Indra approached Sitadevi in the dress of a brahmana and said, "I am the king of heaven, Indra, and I have come here to render assistance to Lord Rama. Please take this celestial sweet-rice, for as soon as you eat it, you will become immune to hunger, thirst and other bodily miseries for years to come."

Sitadevi doubted that the brahmana was in fact Indra and so she asked him to reveal his celestial form. Being so requested, Indra assumed his real form. When Sitadevi noticed how his feet did not touch the ground, how his garland was not at all faded, and how his clothing was completely free from dirt, she became convinced and so happily agreed to eat the sweet-rice.

First Sitadevi offered the food to Lord Rama and Laksmana, praying that They would accept it. Then, as soon as she ate the prasadam, Sitadevi became freed from all bodily pangs. Since their mission had been accomplished, Indra and Nidra-devi instantly vanished from Sitadevi's sight.

Meanwhile, after killing Marica, as Lord Rama hurried back to His cottage, He thought, "I know that the Raksasas want to take revenge for My having slaughtered their entire contingent at Janasthana. Marica's tricks must have been part of a plan to lure Me away from Sitadevi so that they could devour her. I hope that Laksmana did not leave Sitadevi alone after hearing Marica cry out, perfectly imitating My voice."

Lord Rama was already very apprehensive, and so when He heard the frightful cry of a jackal at His back, He became even more afraid. Birds and animals began to pass Lord Rama on the right side, while crying out frightfully. His left eye twitched, His left arm throbbed convulsively, and His heart thumped loudly. While observing all these inauspicious signs, Lord Rama met Laksmana, who was on His way from the cottage.

Taking Him by the left hand, Lord Rama reproached Laksmana, saying, "How could You disobey My order and leave Sita unprotected? From all the inauspicious omens I see, I can understand that she has either been killed or kidnapped. Oh, Laksmana, how could you leave Sita alone?"

As they rushed back to the cottage, Lord Rama tripped on the forest path. Overwhelmed by fear for Sitadevi's safety, and angry with Laksmana for deserting her, Lord Rama lamented like a madman, "If Sita is dead, then I will kill Myself! I could not even think of living without her! Oh Laksmana, how could You betray Me like this and leave My dear Sita unprotected?"

Laksmana tried to explain what had happened by saying, "Mother Sitadevi became like a mad woman after hearing Your cries for help, and she spoke to Me very harshly. She goaded Me with sharp words and accused Me of leaving You to die so that I could enjoy her for Myself. Then she accused Me of being Bharata's accomplice and Your enemy in disguise. Just to prove that her accusations were false, I had to come to Your aid."

With great irritation and agitation, Lord Rama snapped, "That is no excuse for leaving Sita alone and unprotected. You know that I am unconquerable and fully able to defend Myself against the Raksasas. You should not have disobeyed My order simply because of being provoked by a woman's anger. O Laksmana, You have made a great mistake by acting in a way quite unworthy of You."

While conversing in this way, Lord Rama and Laksmana returned to Their cottage and found it deserted. Feverishly, Lord Rama began searching everywhere in the vicinity, and when He failed to find Sitadevi, His face became withered and darkened due to grief.

Afflicted with transcendental madness arising from separation, Lord Rama questioned the trees, "O kadamba, O bilva, O arjuna, have you seen My beloved Sita pass this way, dressed in yellow silk and with flowers in her hair?"

After receiving no reply, Lord Rama inquired from the animals and birds, and when they gave no answer, His sorrow increased. Sometimes Lord Rama imagined that He caught a glimpse of Sita and so He cried out, "Dearly beloved, why are you running away from Me and hiding? Why don't you speak to Me?"

Then again, Lord Rama imagined that the Raksasas had devoured Sitadevi, and this caused Him to envision her delicate bodily features. Lord Rama and Laksmana continued to comb the surrounding hills and forests, refusing to give up hope of finding Sitadevi. However, when she was not found after a thorough search, Lord Rama became discouraged and sat down in utter despair. Still, Laksmana encouraged Lord Rama to continue looking, and so They roamed through more forests, hills and plains.

Finally, Lord Rama could continue no longer and so He gave way to intense grief, standing motionless as if bereft of all reason. Breathing hotly and His eyes filled with tears, Lord Rama became indifferent to Laksmana's assurances that They would surely find Sitadevi. Being tormented by love, Lord Rama cried out helplessly, "Sita, Sita," again and again. Then, as if deranged, Lord Rama called out, "My darling, have you hidden yourself just to play a joke on Me? I beg you to please come out now so that My intolerable suffering will be relieved."

When Sitadevi did not come, Lord Rama became even more disappointed, being convinced that the Raksasas had devoured her. While Lord Rama continued to lament in a most pathetic manner, Laksmana also became sick at heart. Lord Rama said, "O Laksmana, please go to the shore of the Godavari. Maybe Sita went there to gather lotus flowers."

After some time, when Laksmana returned without Sitadevi, Lord Rama personally went to the river and began questioning the trees and animals. Due to fear of Ravana, none gave a reply, although Lord Rama did notice that the deer seemed to be looking at Him with a meaningful intention. Lord Rama questioned the deer and tried to read their minds by looking through their eyes. It seemed that the deer replied to Him by turning their heads upward and toward the south, as if to indicate that Sitadevi had been carried away through the sky in that direction.

Laksmana could understand the deer's message, and so He suggested to Lord Rama that They walk toward the south in the hopes of finding some clue. After setting out, They soon came to a trail of scattered flowers that Lord Rama recognized to be those He had given Sitadevi. Addressing the nearby mountain, named Prasravana, Lord Rama asked, "Where has Sita gone, after passing this way?"

When He received no reply, Lord Rama became enraged and challenged, "O mountain, if you do not answer Me I will shatter you to pieces with My arrows!"

Still there was silence as Lord Rama glanced over the mountain with red-hot eyes and prepared to release His arrows for its destruction. Laksmana then pointed out Sitadevi's running footprints, along with those of a giant Raksasa. Following these footprints, they came to where Ravana's broken bow and quiver lay, as well as shattered debris from his chariot and broken pieces of Sitadevi's jewelry. When drops of blood were also seen, Lord Rama concluded that Sitadevi must have been devoured by the Raksasas, for it appeared as if two of the man-eaters had fought over her at this place.

As He gazed at the dead mules, smashed chariot and scattered weapons, enraged Lord Rama vowed, "No Raksasa will escape My vengeance, for today I shall destroy them all in retaliation for Sitadevi's murder. Why didn't the useless demigods do something to save My helpless wife? If the demigods do not deliver Sita to Me at once, then as revenge for their negligence, and the devouring of Sita by the Raksasas, I will destroy the entire universe!"

Lord Rama's eyes were red with anger and his lips, which were pressed together, trembled with rage. After taking His bow from Laksmana's hands, Lord Rama placed a powerful arrow upon the string which was capable of destroying the entire universe.

Terrified, Laksmana joined His hands in supplication and pleaded, "My dear Lord, by nature You are gentle, self-controlled, and the well-wisher of all living entities. Please control Your wrath and act only after careful consideration. It appears to me that there was only one Raksasa in this fight, for there is only the debris of one broken chariot. Let us look some more, and if we cannot find any further clues of mother Sitadevi's whereabouts, You can act as You see fit."

"My dear brother, You must practice forbearance. After all, suffering is inevitable. If You are seen to be unable to tolerate life's miseries, then how will the common people be expected to do so? Please think only about how Your enemy can be killed. Why should You needlessly destroy the universe?"

While speaking, Laksmana lovingly massaged His brother's lotus feet, and fortunately, Lord Rama became pacified. After withdrawing the arrow from His bow, Lord Rama asked what They should do next and so Laksmana suggested that They thoroughly search the entire area of Janasthana.

Thereafter, while roaming through the forest, Lord Rama and Laksmana came upon the fallen Jatayu, lying in a pool of blood. At first Lord Rama thought that he was a Raksasa disguised as a bird, resting a little after having devoured Sitadevi. But as Lord Rama approached him with an arrow fitted to His bow, Jatayu identified himself and explained how he had been fatally wounded while trying to rescue Sitadevi from the clutches of Ravana. After understanding the situation, Lord Rama threw His bow aside. As He embraced Jatayu, Lord Rama became doubly aggrieved, because of the plight of His well-wisher.

Anxiously, Lord Rama inquired, "My dear Jatayu, can you tell Me anything more about Sita and her abductor?"

The dying Jatayu replied, "Ravana kidnapped Sitadevi at a particular time of day known as vinda. If a person loses something at that time, he will surely get it back very soon. My dear Lord, do not be overly distraught about Sitadevi, for You will certainly regain her after killing the king of the Raksasas in battle."

Then, as Jatayu was describing Ravana's exalted lineage, he suddenly gasped his last breath while uttering, "Lord Rama, Lord Rama."

Lord Rama told Laksmana, "I feel more saddened by the death of Jatayu, who gave up his life for My sake, than the kidnapping of Sitadevi. Go and bring some logs so that We can cremate his body. Let it be known that Jatayu will attain the highest destination as a reward for the service he has rendered to Me."

Laksmana prepared the funeral pyre and Lord Rama placed Jatayu's body upon it. Laksmana lit the fire and both brothers made offerings for the benefit of Jatayu's departed soul, while Lord Rama chanted Vedic mantras. Lord Rama and Laksmana then went to the Godavari to offer oblations of water, and after doing so, They bathed. Thus completing the funeral ceremonies, Lord Rama and Laksmana once again fixed Their minds on finding Sitadevi, and so They continued wandering in the forest.

Passing out of the Dandaka forest, Lord Rama and Laksmana traveled toward the southwest. After some time They came to a huge cave, in front of which stood a fierce female Raksasa. The ghastly Raksasi, having sharp teeth, a large protruding belly and tough skin, suddenly caught Laksmana by the hand and said, "Handsome hero, please come revel with me in this delightful forest."

Saying this, the Raksasi embraced Laksmana and continued, "My name is Ayonmukhi, and I am Yours. Take me as Your beloved wife."

Laksmana angrily drew His sword and cut off the Raksasa's ears, nose and breasts. Screaming with pain, she ran away, and so Lord Rama and Laksmana proceeded into the dense forest.

Lord Rama then said, "My left arm is throbbing and My mind feels perturbed. O Laksmana, we should be prepared for some immanent danger."

A moment later, the frightful cry of a vanjulaka bird was heard. Laksmana said, "This indicates that victory will be Ours."

After They had walked some distance, a loud noise was suddenly heard and a storm began brewing. Lord Rama and Laksmana proceeded cautiously with Their swords in hand, and in this way they came upon a huge Raksasa having no head, neck or legs, and a gigantic mouth located in the middle of his belly.

This Raksasa was as big as a mountain, and sharp, bristling hair stood up all over his body. On his chest were two fiery eyes, and his long arms stretched for twelve kilometers, enabling him to easily catch large animals to eat. Although Lord Rama and Laksmana retreated to a distance of three kilometers upon seeing the Raksasa, the monster caught Them and squeezed Them so tightly that They were put into a helpless condition.

Lord Rama did not feel aggrieved, but Laksmana became despondent and said, "My dear brother, You should offer Me as a sacrifice to this Raksasa in exchange for Your own life, so that You can continue looking for mother Sitadevi."

Lord Rama encouraged Laksmana not to be afraid, and at that time the Raksasa said, "My name is Kabandha. It is my great fortune that You have come to this forest, because I have been very hungry for a long time. Since You are the food Destiny has provided me, I think that You will have a very difficult time escaping with Your precious lives."

Laksmana said to Lord Rama, "We should save Ourselves by quickly cutting off this Raksasa's arms with Our swords."

When he heard this, Kabandha became furious and opened his mouth wide with the intention of devouring Lord Rama and Laksmana at once. Before he could do this, Lord Rama cut off his right arm and Laksmana cut off his left, making the Raksasa fall to the ground, bathed in his own blood. In an anguished voice, Kabandha asked, "Who are you?"

Laksmana replied, "This is Lord Rama, a ksatriya in the line of Iksvaku, and I am His brother. We have come here looking for Lord Rama's wife, mother Sitadevi, who was kidnapped by the king of the Raksasas, Ravana."

Kabandha was overjoyed to hear the name of Lord Rama. After warmly welcoming his guests, he said, "I am so fortunate that You have come here and redeemed me! Please listen as I tell you how I happened to acquire this ghastly form. In my last life, I was the son of Danu. By performing austerities, I was able to satisfy Brahma, who then awarded me the benediction of having a long duration of life. After receiving this boon, I became very proud, thinking that my arch enemy, Indra, could no longer harm me."

"Thereafter, when I attacked the king of heaven on the battlefield, he hurled his thunderbolt at me. As a result, my head and legs became thrust into my body. Finding myself in such a wretched condition, I begged Indra to kill me, but he refused to do so, because it would falsify Lord Brahma's words. When I asked Indra how I would survive without a head, he placed my mouth in the middle of my belly and made my arms twelve kilometers long."

Indra then declared, "When Lord Rama and Laksmana come before you and cut off your arms, you will regain your celestial form."

"Ever since that time, I have been catching all kinds of creatures and stuffing them into my mouth, hoping that one day I would capture Lord Rama mentioned by Indra."

"Once, I harassed a great sage named Sthulasira, and so he cursed me to retain this horrible form for eternity. I tearfully begged the sage to prescribe some end to my plight, and so he confirmed that I would regain my original form after being cremated by Lord Rama. At last, I have been delivered by You. In return, after my death, I will direct You to a powerful ally who will help You get back Your wife."

Lord Rama said, "My wife, Sita, has been kidnapped by Ravana, the king of the Raksasas. Unfortunately, I only know his name and nothing about his appearance or place of residence. I will cremate your body so that you can attain your desired destination, but in return you should give Me some information about Ravana."

Kabandha replied, "I am very sorry, but I do not know anything about Ravana. Still, after being cremated, I will tell You about someone who has traveled throughout the three worlds, and so will be able to help you locate the king of the Raksasas.

Lord Rama and Laksmana took Kabandha and placed him on a funeral pyre that Laksmana made within a mountain cave. Because the body was so fat, as it burned, it looked like a big lump of flaming ghee. From out of the fire rose Kabandha in his original celestial form, dressed in fine garments and decorated with ornaments and flower garlands.

After taking his seat upon a celestial chariot pulled by swans, Kabandha said, "O Lord, political misfortune can be overcome by six means—making peace, fighting, assassination, amassing superior arms and fortifications, creating dissention, and seeking the help of allies. You are now plunged into the depths of despair due to the loss of Your wife, but You will one day be able to retrieve her with the help of someone in a similar condition. That person is the monkey king, Sugriva, who has been exiled by his brother, Vali, the son of Indra."

"My dear Lord, You should go and make friends with Sugriva. He is the son of Surya, and he also needs a benefactor. He is now living along with four other vanaras on Rsyamuka Mountain, which is located near Lake Pampa. First go to Lake Pampa and meet the ascetic woman, mother Sabari. A great sage, Matanga, resided there long ago, and after his death, mother Sabari took care of his disciples. These disciples of Matanga have long since ascended to higher worlds, but mother Sabari remains at Matanga's asrama, awaiting Your arrival before departing for Brahmaloka."

"Near Lake Pampa is Rsyamuka Hill, where Sugriva lives in a large cave, being afraid of his brother, Vali. Sugriva is loyal, intelligent, generous, wise, very brave and powerful. Go and make an alliance with him, for he knows all about the Raksasas, and his followers can wander over the earth until they find mother Sita."

After saying this, Kabandha took permission from Lord Rama and ascended to heaven. Following Kabandha's directions, Lord Rama and Laksmana departed, and on the second day, they arrived at Lake Pampa, next to which they saw Matanga Rsi's asrama. When Lord Rama and Laksmana entered the hermitage, mother Sabari quickly got up and greeted Them with folded hands. After touching Lord Rama and Laksmana's lotus feet, mother Sabari gave Them water to wash Their feet and supplied Them with food and drink.

When Lord Rama inquired about her spiritual practices, mother Sabari, a perfected soul, replied, "Just by seeing You, I have achieved the fruition of all my austerities. When You came to live at Citrakuta, the rishis I had been serving ascended to heaven in celestial chariots. Before departing, however, they assured me that You would come here, and that after welcoming You, I would also attain a heavenly destination."

At Lord Rama's request, mother Sabari took Him and Laksmana on a tour of the beautiful area surrounding Lake Pampa. Afterwards, she requested permission from Lord Rama to cast off her mortal body so that she could rejoin the rishis she had previously served so faithfully. Being very pleased with mother Sabari, Lord Rama granted her desire.

After building a fire, the elderly ascetic mother, dressed in deerskin and tree-bark and having matted hair, jumped into the blazing flames. The next moment, mother Sabari was seen rising up to heaven, like a flash of lightning. She was adorned with celestial jewelry and garlands.

Having thus witnessed mother Sabari's spiritual prowess, Lord Rama and Laksmana went to bathe in Lake Pampa, which had been created from the water of the seven seas. Thereafter, feeling enlivened and hopeful of finding Sitadevi, Lord Rama impatiently departed, along with Laksmana, being very eager to meet Sugriva.

## Kiskindha-kanda

The beauty of the spring scenery surrounding Lake Pampa awoke within Lord Rama remembrances of His loving pastimes with Sitadevi, and because of this, His grief became intensified. While constantly thinking about Sitadevi, Lord Rama wondered if she would be able to live in separation from Him, especially during this spring season.

Giving vent to His unbearable sorrow, Lord Rama bitterly lamented, "O Laksmana, now is the month of Caitra, and the lake is so lovely with its deep blue water. This is the season of love, and all the trees are resplendent with fruit and flowers. Look at the karnikara trees with their bright red blossoms, raining their petals so that the carpet of green has become spotted with red."

"Without Sita, life seems meaningless. That which pleased Me when she was present now gives Me pain! When I hear the kokila's call, I think of Sita's sweet voice. When I see the pinkish lotus flowers swaying on the crests of the waves, I think of Sita's eyes. The gentle fragrant breeze only reminds Me of Sita's honey-scented breath. O Laksmana, it is a cruel spring! The pain is unbearable for Me! I can no longer live without Sita. You had better return to Ayodhya and render service to Bharata, while I stay here and give up My life!"

Hoping to instill some hope within Lord Rama, Laksmana replied, "My dear brother, there is no reason to believe that mother Sitadevi is not alive and well. Wherever Ravana may be— in heaven or on earth, within the sea, or in the nether regions — We will find him and vent Our wrath! Give up this useless melancholy and make up Your mind to find mother Sitadevi! It is by earnest endeavor that We shall succeed, not by lamenting!"

After being admonished by Laksmana, Lord Rama gave up His morbid depression. As They continued talking, Lord Rama and Laksmana reached Rsyamuka Hill. From a distance, Sugriva could see Them approaching. Suspecting them to be allies of Vali, Sugriva became very afraid. Along with his associates, he quickly went to take shelter within Matahga's asrama, since it was protected by the sage's magic spell. Still, despite this security, Sugriva was so frightened and restless that he wandered from hilltop to hilltop, being unable to remain still for a moment.

Hanuman said to his distracted master, "You should give up this paranoia of Vali. Because you are a fickle-minded monkey, you hastily come to some conclusion that will later on be rejected after giving the matter careful thought."

Sugriva replied, "Whether they are agents of Vali or not, these persons who have come here certainly seem dangerous. They look like chiefs of the demigods, and They are armed with bows and swords. Remember that Vali is very cunning, and he has many friends."

"My dear Hanuman, I want you to go and find out why these two have come here. Observe Them carefully—Their speech and Their expressions. Find out who They are and what They want. If you purposely glorify me in Their presence, you will be able to find out whether They are friendly or inimical."

Hanuman concealed his real form and appeared before Lord Rama and Laksmana as a mendicant. After falling flat to offer his obeisances, Hanuman said, "O great heroes, please tell me how it is that such exalted personalities as Yourselves have come to this desolate region. Your strongly-built bodies indicate that You are warriors, and yet You are dressed like ascetics. My name is Hanuman, and I am a minister of Sugriva, who has been banished from his kingdom by his elder brother, Vali. I am the son of Vayu, and I can go anywhere at will and assume any form I please. Sugriva has sent me to extend to You his friendship and hospitality."

Lord Rama said to Laksmana, "This Hanuman is a minister of Sugriva, the noble king of the vanaras who We have been searching for. How courteously he behaves, and how poetically he speaks. In his eyes, limbs, attitude and expression, there is nothing the least displeasing or duplicitous. He has depth, power and confidence, and his speech reflects a mature unification of heart, voice and intelligence. Even an enemy would be charmed by his words! My dear Laksmana, please explain to Hanuman the sequence of events that has led Us to come here."

Laksmana said, "It is Our good fortune that We have met you, for We have been searching for Sugriva, being eager to make an alliance of friendship with him. This is Lord Rama, the son of Maharaja Dasaratha, and I am His younger brother, Laksmana. Lord Rama's father wanted to install Him as his successor to the royal throne, but due to political intrigue, He was banished to the forest instead."

"Recently, the Raksasa king, Ravana, kidnapped Lord Rama's beloved wife, mother Sitadevi. Overwhelmed by grief, We have been searching for mother Sitadevi in the forest. While wandering here and there, We came upon a fierce Raksasa named Kabandha. By killing him, We relieved him from a terrible curse, and in return he advised Us to make an alliance of friendship with Sugriva. It is for this purpose that We have come here, and We are ready to do whatever is beneficial for you and your noble king."

Laksmana's tone of voice was pathetic, and tears filled His eyes as He described Their plight. Hanuman said, "Sugriva, like you, is now bereft of his kingdom and wife. I am sure that he and his followers will help You find mother Sitadevi. Please come with me now and meet Sugriva. He has been very anxious to find out the reason for Your coming here."

After saying this, Hanuman assumed his real gigantic monkey- like form. Taking Lord Rama and Laksmana upon his shoulders, Hanuman immediately departed for Prasravana Mountain. First Hanuman went alone to Sugriva and explained about Lord Rama's friendly intentions. Sugriva then took the form of a mendicant and went to see Lord Rama and Laksmana. As he approached, Sugriva extended his hand as an offering of friendship. With great pleasure, Lord Rama extended His hand in return and warmly embraced the monkey chief. Laksmana lit a sacred fire between Lord Rama and Sugriva to formally unite the two friends. After ceremonially circumambulating the fire, Lord Rama and Sugriva looked upon one another without anxiety.

Sugriva said, "My dear Lord Rama, from this day on, Your happiness shall be my happiness, Your sorrow shall be my sorrow, and vice versa."

After saying this, Sugriva snapped off a sala branch full of flowers and placed it on the ground for Lord Rama to sit on. Hanuman broke off a branch of sandalwood blossoms for Laksmana.

After all were seated, Sugriva said, "O my Lord, I pass my life in constant anxiety because of my brother, Vali. After he forcibly took away my wife and kingdom, I took shelter within this forest. Still, I remain haunted by the fear that Vali may come and attack me at any time, and thus I know not a moment's peace."

Lord Rama mildly smiled and said, "Service is the real fruit of friendship, and so it is My duty to dispose of Vali so that you can regain your wife and kingdom."

Sugriva said, "Hanuman has told me how You were exiled to the forest, and how Your wife was kidnapped. As a friend, it is my vow to help You recover her, whether she is in heaven, on earth, or in the nether regions. My dear Lord, I am sure that I saw mother Sitadevi being carried away by a powerful Raksasa, and she was crying out, "O my dear Lord" She saw us seated upon this hill and so she threw down her upper garment and some jewelry. We have kept these."

Lord Rama was very eager to see these things and so Sugriva went and brought them from a dark cave where they had been stored. Immediately recognizing the cloth and jewelry, Lord Rama cried out, "O darling, O dearly beloved!" As tears fell from His lotus eyes, Lord Rama became so bewildered with grief that He suddenly fell to the ground, wailing pitifully. After coming to His senses, Lord Rama said, "O Laksmana, these were worn by Sitadevi. Can You recognize them?"

Laksmana replied, "I do not know about the yellow silk cloth bangles or earrings. Out of respect for mother Sitadevi, I would never gaze above her ankles, but because I went every morning and bowed down at her lotus feet, I recognize the ankle-bells."

Thereafter, when Lord Rama inquired about Ravana, Sugriva said, "Unfortunately, I have not even heard of Ravana, but You can rest assured that I will help You find him. My dear Lord, You should not lament so much for the loss of Your dear wife. A person can never become happy simply by grieving. Such sorrow actually diminishes one's strength, and thus puts his very life in danger. Even though I am a foolish monkey, in a similar situation, I do not lament as much as You."

Lord Rama became a little pacified by Sugriva's words. After embracing once again, the two sat down comfortably. When Sugriva mentioned his fear of Vali once more, Lord Rama said, "Friends and enemies are known by the respective service and disservice they render. My dear Sugriva, please rest assured that I will kill Vali today, but first I would like to hear in detail how such bitter enmity arose between you two brothers."

Sugriva replied, "It is only to a true friend that one can give full vent to his grief. My dear Lord, please listen as I tell You the whole story. When my father died, being the elder prince, Vali became the king of Kiskindha, and I engaged in his service."

"Previous to this, Mayavi, the son of Maya Danava, had become Vali's enemy because of a woman. Then, one night, as everyone was asleep, Mayavi came to Kiskindha and challenged Vali to fight. Although I, along with his wives, tried to dissuade him, the enraged Vali rushed out of the palace to meet the aggressor."

"I followed my brother, and when Mayavi saw both of us, he became afraid and ran away. As we chased him, Mayavi entered a deep and dark cave, the entrance of which was covered by dense weeds. Vali ordered me to guard the entrance while he went inside to fight with the demon."

"One entire year passed as I waited with no sign of my brother. Then, when I saw blood mixed with foam oozing from the cave's entrance, and heard what sounded like the enemy but not my brother's voice, I concluded that Vali must have been killed. After blocking the cave's entrance with a huge boulder, I offered water for the benefit of my brother's departed soul. When I returned to Kiskindha, the ministers installed me on the throne, and I began to rule the kingdom righteously."

"Some time later, Vali returned to Kiskindha after killing Mayavi, much to everyone's surprise. When he saw me sitting on the throne, Vali became enraged and immediately arrested all the ministers, putting them in chains. I could have fought with my brother, but out of respect, I bowed to him and placed the royal crown at his feet, hoping he would become pacified. Vali remained angry, however, and he continued to abuse me severely."

Calling together all the leading citizens, Vali announced, "I had entered the earth to kill the demon, Mayavi, and it was only after searching for one full year that I was able to find him. After I killed Mayavi, along with all his relatives, the flow of blood practically filled up the cave, making it very difficult for me to get out. When I finally reached the cave's entrance, I discovered that a huge boulder blocked the way out. I called for Sugriva, again and again. When there was no reply, I kicked aside the boulder and returned home. Much to my surprise, I saw Sugriva sitting on the throne. I could very well understand that Sugriva had tried to shut me up within the cave so that he could gain control of the kingdom."

Sugriva concluded, "In retaliation, Vali took away all my possessions, including my wife, and banished me from Kiskindha, leaving me with just a single cloth. Ever since that time, I have been residing on this mountain, along with my associates."

Lord Rama said, "My dear Sugriva, rest assured that I will kill Vali so that you can recover your wife and kingdom."

Sugriva doubted whether Lord Rama was powerful enough to kill Vali. To illustrate his brother's prowess, Sugriva narrated the following story, "Once there was a great demon named Dundhubhi, who wandered over the earth in the form of a buffalo. When he came to the beach, he challenged the ocean to fight."

The presiding lord of the ocean appeared before Dundhubhi and said, "O best of asuras, I am not a competent match for you. I suggest you approach Himavan, the presiding deity of the Himalayas and father-in-law of Lord Siva."

Thinking that the Ocean was afraid to fight with him, Dundhubhi went to the Himalayas and began tearing down its peaks. Himavan appeared on a mountaintop and said, "I am the shelter of many great rishis, who are nonviolent and peaceful in mind, and I am not at all adept at warfare. O greatest of the demons, please leave us aside and do not create any more disturbances."

When the angry demon asked with whom he should fight, Himavan described my brother Vali, the son of Indra. Dundhubhi then went to Kiskindha. While bellowing loudly, he began tearing up the ground with his hooves, uprooting big trees and damaging the city gates with his horns. Vali immediately came and challenged the arrogant demon to fight.

After a brief exchange of harsh words, Vali grabbed Dundhubhi by the horns, whirled him around, and dashed him to the ground, making blood flow from his ears. A fierce duel followed as each struck the other forcibly, but soon the demon began to weaken. Noticing this, Vali lifted Dundhubhi into the air and smashed him to the ground with all his strength. As blood poured profusely from every hole in his body, Dundhubhi gave up his life.

Vali lifted Dundhubhi's dead body and hurled it a distance of six kilometers. As the corpse flew through the air, drops of blood fell onto the ground at Matanga's asrama. This angered the great sage and he wondered who had done such a stupid thing.

When Matanga learned that Vali had killed the demon, he pronounced the following curse, "If that monkey ever comes within six kilometers of my asrama, he will instantly die. If his ministers come here, they will remain as stone statues for thousands of years."

Sugriva concluded his narration by saying, "Vali went and tried to pacify the sage, but because he failed to do so, he has been afraid to approach this place ever since that time. Over there You can see the dried up skin and bones of the great demon. You can just imagine how powerful Dundhubhi must have been. How can you hope to defeat Vali?"

Laksmana laughed to think how Sugriva doubted Lord Rama's ability to kill Vali. He asked, "What could Lord Rama do that will satisfy you, regarding His prowess?"

Sugriva replied, "Once, at this place, Vali pierced seven large sala trees with seven arrows. If Lord Rama can split one of these trees with a single arrow and then kick the remains of Dundhubhi a distance of 200 bows' length, I would consider Him a suitable match for Vali."

After hearing this, Lord Rama effortlessly lifted Dundhubhi's skeleton with His big toe and sportingly flung it a distance of many kilometers. Still, Sugriva said, "When Vali had thrown the demon's body, it was very heavy with flesh and blood, and he was tired from fighting. Since the carcass is now comparatively light, it is impossible to judge who is the more powerful—Lord Rama or Vali."

Lord Rama picked up His bow and released a powerful arrow that pierced all seven sala trees and then entered the earth, going all the way down to Patala. Finally, after an hour, the arrow returned to Lord Rama's quiver.

Sugriva was truly astonished and he very reverently bowed down at Lord Rama's lotus feet. In turn, Lord Rama picked up Sugriva and embraced him. He then suggested, "Let us go now to Kiskindha. Sugriva, you go on ahead and challenge Vali, while Laksmana and I hide behind some trees outside the city gate."

Sugriva went to Kiskindha and roared loudly to challenge his brother. When he heard this, Vali came rushing out of the city, excited with rage, just like the sun emerging from behind a hill. Vali and Sugriva proceeded to angrily strike one another with their fists, but as Lord Rama watched the fight with His bow in hand, He could not tell which of the two was Vali, for the brothers looked alike. Because of this, Lord Rama refrained from releasing His arrow.

Sugriva thought that Lord Rama was unwilling to help him, and because he appeared to be losing the fight, he ran away, his body battered and soaked with blood. While chasing Sugriva, Vali taunted, "Coward! Run for your life! This time I will spare you!"

Sugriva took shelter in the forest surrounding Matanga's asrama, and soon after, Lord Rama and Laksmana came there. Reproachfully, Sugriva said, "O Lord, if You did not intend to kill Vali, why did You encourage me to challenge him? It would have been better if You had honestly said, 'I am not willing to kill your elder brother.'"

Lord Rama explained, "As I watched the fight, I became confused, because your voice, bodily features and dress exactly resemble your brother's. It is for this reason alone that I did not release My arrow. Put on some distinguishing mark and then go once again and challenge Vali."

Turning to Laksmana, Lord Rama said, "Uproot a blossoming gajapuspi creeper and fasten it around Sugriva's neck. In this way I will easily be able to distinguish him from his brother."

Sugriva once again departed for Kiskindha, accompanied by Lord Rama, Laksmana, Hanuman, Nala, Nila and Tara. When they reached the outskirts of town, everyone concealed themselves within a grove of trees, while Sugriva approached the gate, challenging his brother with loud roars.

Vali was inside the ladies' apartments when he heard Sugriva summoning him. In a fit of rage, he got up from his seat and began storming out of the room with heavy strides. In great anxiety, Vali's wife, Tara, got up and clung to him lovingly, trying to restrain him.

Tara pleaded, "My lord, please cast aside your anger for now and consider the matter carefully. You have already severely beaten Sugriva and so it is very surprising that he has returned so quickly and is roaring so confidently. I can only conclude that your brother is accompanied by a strong ally, and for this reason he dares to act so boldly."

"Our son, Angada, has received information from his spies that Lord Rama and Laksmana have come here and made an alliance of friendship with Sugriva. Lord Rama is inconceivably powerful, on a level with Lord Visnu, and so you should not risk creating enmity with Him. Why don't you settle this quarrel by installing Sugriva as your successor, and in this way make friendship with Lord Rama as well?"

Being destined to die, Vali could not accept this good advice, and so he replied, "I cannot tolerate such arrogance on the part of my younger brother. As far as Lord Rama is concerned, I have no quarrel with Him. He is a virtuous ksatriya and so I have no fear that He would harm an innocent person with whom He has no enmity."

Vali stormed out of the city, hissing with rage, and a fierce battle followed. As before, Vali soon gained the upper hand and so Sugriva signaled to Lord Rama, indicating that his strength was failing. Lord Rama could see that Sugriva was on the verge of collapse and so He fitted a powerful arrow onto His bowstring. That arrow flashed through the air like lightning and pierced Vali deeply in the chest. And yet, although Vali fell to the ground, bathed in his blood, he did not give up his life, for he was wearing a celestial gold chain that had been given to him by his father, King Indra.

Lord Rama and Laksmana came out from the grove and approached the mortally wounded Vali. Filled with indignation, Vali chastised Lord Rama with harsh and disdainful words, "I had heard that You are a righteous king, devoted to dharma, and that You are heroic and compassionate. Little did I realize that You are not these! Like a well covered with weeds, You make an external show of virtue, but Your sinful action has betrayed Your wicked heart. I fought with Sugriva, because I was convinced that You would not attack me. How could anyone claiming to be a ksatriya and a hero kill someone who is fighting with another, and with whom he has no enmity? I never harmed or insulted You. I never attacked Your kingdom. What merit have You acquired by striking me unfairly? How will You justify this action in front of righteous men? If You had fought honestly, it would be You lying here, and not I! You shot Your arrow at me from a hiding place, like a snake attacking a sleeping man. I challenge You to justify this shameful act!"

Lord Rama replied, "Your accusations show that you are ignorant of true morality. Monkeys are by nature frivolous because of their uncontrolled minds. You are a monkey and your advisors are also monkeys, so you cannot understand dharma."

"The entire earth, with all its mountains, forests and rivers, is ruled by the dynasty of Iksvaku. The kings of the Iksvaku dynasty have complete authority over all men and animals. They have the power to punish or reward whomever they please. It is you that have acted sinfully under the sway of lust and greed. You have taken Ruma, the wife of your younger brother, and enjoyed her as your wife. It is for this sinful act that you deserved to die at My hands."

"Death is the proper punishment for one who has sexual relations with his daughter, daughter-in-law, sister, or the wife of a younger brother. If a king does not punish a sinner, then he himself becomes sinful. It is for this reason that I promised to kill you and thus enable Sugriva to regain his wife and kingdom. Aside from this, it is an accepted practice that while hunting, a ksatriya releases his arrows at inattentive animals from a hidden position. Because you are only a monkey, My act does not bear the slightest fault."

After hearing this, Vali felt shame for his abominable actions. With hands joined in supplication, he replied, "I must admit that what You have said is correct. I had only dared to denounce You because I was overly proud, being ignorant of my own sins."

"My dear Lord, I beg that You to give protection to my only son, Angada, who was begotten by me through my wife, Tara. He will certainly become very distressed when he hears about my death. Please excuse the harsh words by which I foolishly accused You of acting in an unrighteous manner."

Lord Rama assured Vali that He would take care of Angada. When she learned of her husband's defeat, Tara came running out of the city with her son. Surrounding Tara were Vali's ministers, but upon seeing Lord Rama, they began to fearfully run away. Tara tried to stop them, but the ministers advised, "Do not go to see Vali. Protect your son within the fortifications of Kiskindha and prepare to install him upon the royal throne."

Tara replied, "I do not care for sovereignty. The kingdom and its royal opulence are now useless to me without my husband."

While crying piteously and beating her breasts in agony, Tara went to where Vali was lying on the ground. She embraced her dying husband and lamented bitterly while Vali's other wives came and surrounded them. Tara's only desire was to follow her husband. Having made up her mind to fast until death, she sat down by Vali's side.

Hanuman pleaded, "O Queen, please get up. Do not let your grief deviate you from the path of righteousness. You will have to perform the funeral ceremonies for your dead husband, and then protect Angada carefully after installing him upon the throne."

Tara adamantly replied, "As for Angada's installation, that decision lies in the hands of his uncle, Sugriva. My only desire is to follow my husband to his destination."

Vali said, "My dear Sugriva, I beg you to please forgive me for all the wrongs I have done. Accept the kingdom from me now, and take care of my son, for he will always remain faithful to you."

After saying this, Vali handed Sugriva the celestial gold chain he had received from King Indra. Turning to Angada, he said, "My dear son, now that I am departing, you should always remain obedient to Sugriva. Act after due consideration and accept the dualities of pleasure and pain with an equipoised mind. Avoid excessive attachment and hatred, for both extremes lead to degradation."

After saying this, Vali, who was deeply pained by Lord Rama's arrow, gave up his life. As Tara continued to embrace her dead husband while sorrowfully wailing, NiIa came and extracted the arrow from Vali's chest. Tara told Angada, "Bow down to your father's feet," and as the young prince did so, he also became overwhelmed by intense sorrow.

Seeing Tara's anguish, Sugriva also became despondent. With a voice laden with sadness, he said, "O Lord, I am now experiencing that the fulfillment of my desire has produced just the opposite of what I had anticipated. Instead of happiness, I feel repulsion when I think of ruling the kingdom. Life itself seems tasteless, after having killed my elder brother. To atone for my sinful act, I have decided to enter fire and give up my life. My dear Lord, these other heroic monkeys will help You search for Your beloved wife."

Lord Rama was very unhappy to hear Sugriva talk like this, and tears came to His eyes. Tara approached Lord Rama and pleaded, "I know that my husband will be very unhappy without me, even if he's living in heaven. Please kill me so that I can join him. O Lord, if You consider the killing of a woman to be sinful, then think of me as being non-different from Vali."

Lord Rama consoled Tara and then said to Sugriva, "The tears of sorrow you have shed are sufficient bereavement for the departed soul of your brother. Now it is time for you to attend to your responsibilities. This world is moving under the direction of eternal time, and thus all living beings make their appearance and then depart. Under the direction of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, time never oversteps its limit or deviates from its course. Because everything is destined by time, one should not lament for that which is inevitable."

Sugriva gradually became pacified. With Laksmana's help, he began arranging for the cremation of his dead brother's body. Tara had a palanquin brought from Kiskindha. Sugriva and Angada put Vali's body on it, and led the procession to a nearby mountain stream. As the funeral pyre was being built, Tara placed Vali's head upon her lap and continued to bitterly weep, giving pain to all who saw her.

Finally, the other women came and raised up Tara, enabling Sugriva and Angada to place the body on the funeral pyre. After the completion of the cremation, water was brought from the River Tungabhadra, and offerings were made to the departed soul of the heroic monkey king.

Hanuman asked Lord Rama to accompany Sugriva to Kiskindha, to perform his coronation. Lord Rama declined, however, saying, "Due to the nature of My exile, I cannot enter any city. Hanuman, I suggest that you perform Sugriva's coronation. At the same time, Angada can be installed as his successor. The monsoon season is about to begin and that will not be a good time to search for Sitadevi. All of you monkey chiefs can remain at Kiskindha for four months while Laksmana and I pass Our time staying in a mountain cave. When the autumn season arrives, we can begin an all-out search for Ravana's kingdom."

When Sugriva entered Kiskindha, he was heartily welcomed by all the citizens. At the time of his coronation, the bathing ceremony was performed by leading monkeys, such as Mainda, Dvivida and Hanuman, as well as by Jambavan, the king of the bears. Sugriva got back his wife, Ruma, and he installed Angada as his successor.

For four months, Lord Rama and Laksmana lived within a cave of the Prasravana Mountain. Due to feeling intense pangs of separation from Sitadevi, Lord Rama could not find any pleasure in the scenic beauty of the mountainous region. Nor would sleep come to visit Him at night, because of His continuous crying.

One day, Lord Rama told Laksmana, "After evaporating water from the ocean for eight months, the sky sends forth heavy showers of rain. Dark clouds as large as hills range across the sky in clusters, creating a melancholy mood. See the golden lightning streaking inside the black monsoon cloud, like Sita in the arms of Ravana! The lush green grass provides a colorful dress for mother Earth, spotted with various flowers and restless birds. Bees are humming, frogs are croaking, and the rumble of thunder and patter of rainfall provide musical accompaniment. Sugriva must be enjoying the monsoon season, his object having been fulfilled, but without Sita, I am like the riverbank that erodes because of the river's swelling current."

At such times Laksmana would try and encourage Lord Rama by pointing out that the purpose of a grieving man is never fulfilled. However, the dark skies and frequent rain, although nourishing for the tropical jungle, only served to intensify Lord Rama's longing for Sitadevi.

Lord Rama assumed that by enabling Sugriva to get back his kingdom, the monkey king would never forget his obligation to help find Sitadevi. And yet, even as the autumn season came, and the skies became clear and blue, Sugriva did not come to meet Lord Rama. Having achieved his goal, and being without any cause of fear, Sugriva spent his time completely absorbed in enjoying the company of young women, especially his wife Ruma and the newly- acquired Tara. Because of this, he even neglected his responsibility of taking care of the state administration.

When Hanuman saw how Sugriva had become a slave to sensuality, and was completely neglecting his duties, he approached the king and advised, "You had better keep your promise and fulfill your obligation to Lord Rama. Out of respect for his friend, Lord Rama has not yet come here to remind you, but you should remember that it is only by His grace that you have prospered. Now is the time for you to summon all the monkeys under your command and begin an all-out search for mother Sitadevi."

Hanuman's speech brought Sugriva to his senses. The king called for NiIa and ordered, "Let my proclamation be broadcast to all the monkey warriors, that they must report here for duty within fifteen days or else be punished with death!"

After giving this command, Sugriva returned to the inner apartments. Meanwhile, due to separation from Sitadevi, the beauty of the autumn season only served to heighten Lord Rama's anguish. And, because He could understand that Sugriva was simply wallowing in a life of sensuality, Lord Rama became even more depressed. Laksmana also became despondent while observing Lord Rama's grief, and so He tried His best to cheer Him up.

One day, Lord Rama said, "Now that autumn has arrived, the ground has become firm and dry, and the air is crisp and cool. This is the perfect time to initiate a military campaign, and yet Sugriva is not to be seen. It seems that he has forgotten all sense of duty, being absorbed in sense enjoyment in the association of numerous women."

My dear Laksmana, I want You to go to Kiskindha and admonish Sugriva in My name. Tell him, "O king of the monkeys, one who acts like you is the vilest of persons. I am astonished that you are not afraid of avoiding Me, the killer of your elder brother. I hereby warn you that if you neglect your promise to help Me find Sitadevi, I will personally come to Kiskindha and kill you, along with all of your relatives."

When Lord Rama exhibited this transcendental anger, Laksmana also became excited with rage. While picking up His bow and preparing to depart, Laksmana declared, "If that rascal Sugriva does not jump up to execute Your command, I will kill him this very day!"

Saying this, Laksmana hastily started to leave. Lord Rama tried to restrain Laksmana's anger, saying, "First speak to Sugriva in a conciliatory tone, for I am sure that will be sufficient to bring him to his senses."

Thereafter, with an air of great indignation, Laksmana entered Kiskindha, a beautiful city built within a huge cave. As He rushed toward Sugriva's palace, like a maddened elephant, Laksmana impetuously knocked down the trees that stood in His path.

There were many fierce monkeys guarding the palace entrance, and when they saw Laksmana coming, His lips trembling with rage, they began uprooting large trees and picking up boulders. When Laksmana saw the guards arming themselves, He became so ferocious that the panic-stricken monkeys began running away in all directions. The ministers rushed to inform Sugriva of how Laksmana had arrived in an exceedingly angry mood. However, because Sugriva was with Tara and filled with sexual desire, he did not pay much attention to their words.

Meanwhile, as the ministers ordered the monkeys to arm themselves for battle, enraged Laksmana met Angada and told him to inform Sugriva of His arrival. Angada went to Sugriva and while clasping his feet, told him of Laksmana's presence. Sugriva had fallen asleep in a drunken state, however, and so did not even wake up. Only when numerous monkeys came due to fear of Laksmana and noisily clamored around Sugriva did he finally become roused from his slumber.

As Sugriva opened his blood-shot eyes, the ministers said, "Laksmana is waiting for you outside the palace gate, in a very angry mood. We advise you to go and offer your obeisances and submission, just to appease Him."

Finally realizing the gravity of the situation, Sugriva quickly got out of bed, wondering why Laksmana was so angry with him. Hanuman reminded Sugriva, "O King, because you were absorbed in sense enjoyment, you did not notice how time was passing. Autumn arrived long ago and yet you neglected to fulfill your promise to help Lord Rama search for mother Sitadevi. Laksmana has come here just to remind you of your duty, and so you had better approach Him with folded hands, hoping that He becomes pacified."

Sugriva told Angada to escort Laksmana into the palace. All the monkeys who had previously taken up weapons now stood with folded hands to greet Him. When Laksmana came to the inner apartments, He could hear the sound of women singing to the accompaniment of musical instruments, as well as the jingling of their ornaments.

Actually, when He saw all the young and beautiful ladies, Laksmana became somewhat ashamed, because of His vow to shun the company of others' wives. Although He was thus restrained from entering, Laksmana twanged His mighty bow, causing Sugriva to become very afraid.

Sugriva trembled when he heard that awesome sound and he told Tara, "I wonder why Laksmana is so angry. I think it would be better if you go first and try to appease Him."

As Tara came before Him, her dress loosened and her eyes rolling due to intoxication, Laksmana's anger subsided. As He gazed downward at her feet, out of respect, Tara asked, "My dear prince, why are you so angry with Sugriva?"

Laksmana curtly replied, "Your husband has neglected his obligation to Lord Rama, preferring to pass his days in the inner apartments, dallying with women."

Tara pleaded, "You must forgive Sugriva, for he has lost his good intelligence under the influence of lust. We have heard that even great rishis sometimes hanker for sense enjoyment, and so what to speak of a fickle-minded monkey? Please do not think that Sugriva has forgotten his obligation to Lord Rama. He has already called for millions of monkeys to assemble here so that they can be engaged in the search for mother Sitadevi. Why don't You come in now and talk with Sugriva?"

Laksmana entered the inner apartments of the palace. When He saw the fabulous opulence, along with the groups of beautiful women, His anger once again became aroused. Sugriva was sitting on a couch, embracing Ruma, but when he saw Laksmana, he felt embarrassed, and so quickly stood up with folded hands, as did all the ladies.

Laksmana angrily chastised Sugriva, "You simply made empty promises, proving yourself to be a false friend and a most abominable person! One who receives help from a sincere friend and then does not repay that service is most hard-hearted and deserves to be killed!"

"Once, when Lord Brahma saw such an ungrateful person, he exclaimed, 'The means of atonement has been prescribed for the killer of a cow, as well as for a drunkard, a thief, and for one who has broken a sacred vow, but there is no redemption for an ungrateful soul!' I warn you, Sugriva. If you do not start helping Lord Rama immediately, you will meet your brother Vali in the abode of Yamaraja this very day!"

Tara replied for her husband, "O Laksmana, Sugriva is not a liar, nor is he hard-hearted. He has not forgotten what Lord Rama did for him. His only fault is that he lost all sense of time, due to overindulgence in sensuality. We have heard that in Lanka, the abode of Ravana, there are millions of Raksasas. Since it would not be possible to kill Ravana without slaying them first, Sugriva has summoned millions of monkey warriors from all corners of the earth. He has not yet met Lord Rama, nor initiated the search for mother Sitadevi, because he is awaiting their arrival. Sugriva has set a fifteen day time limit, and so thousands of monkeys and bears are expected to start arriving at Kiskindha today."

After hearing this, Laksmana gave up His anger. Sugriva humbly threw aside his garland and said, "I could never repay Lord Rama for all that He has done for me. He is my master and my Lord. I will follow Him wherever He goes and do whatever He orders. I admit that I was at fault, and now I beg for Your forgiveness."

Laksmana replied in a mild voice, "Kindly forgive My angry mood. I think that it would be best for you to go see Lord Rama right now. He will become encouraged by your sincerity."

Sugriva ordered Hanuman, "Once again, summon all the monkeys from the Himalaya, Gajendra, Mandara and Kailasa Mountains. Arouse all those who are prone to sense indulgence and procrastination by telling them that anyone who does not respond within ten days will be killed."

Hanuman immediately dispatched the monkey leaders in all directions. As a result, within the very hour, millions of monkeys began pouring into Kiskindha. After making their presentations to the king, the monkeys were dismissed. Again Laksmana urged Sugriva to go see Lord Rama, and so the king called for a palanquin.
