As he climbed the building, which was as large as a big hill, Hanuman tore it to pieces with his claws. The noise made by the debris crashing to the ground could be heard all over Lanka.

Hanuman shouted, "May there be victory for Lord Rama and Laksmana! My name is Hanuman, and I will destroy Lanka within the very sight of the Raksasas!"

Although the sound of Hanuman's voice struck terror into their hearts, the one hundred temple guards picked up their weapons and surrounded him. Enraged, Hanuman broke off one of the temple's columns. While assuming a terrifying appearance, he whirled it around so that fire was generated as it struck the other pillars. As Hanuman proceeded to kill the guards with that blazing pillar, the entire temple became engulfed in flames. Again and again Hanuman shouted, "Let there be victory for Lord Rama, Laksmana, and all the monkeys!"

Jambumali came on the scene, riding upon a chariot driven by donkeys, and the twanging of his bow filled the sky with its vibrations. Without wasting time, Jambumali struck Hanuman in the mouth with an arrow, and in the arms with ten more. As blood covered his entire face, infuriated Hanuman tore up a huge rock and hurled it with great force. Jambumali smashed it to bits with ten arrows and so Hanuman uprooted a big tree and whirled it around. Jambumali broke that tree to pieces with four arrows before it even left Hanuman's hands. The Raksasa then pierced Hanuman's arms with five arrows, and his chest with ten more.

Hanuman was undaunted and he once again picked up his iron bar. After whirling it around impetuously, Hanuman threw it with great force. The iron bar struck Jambumali so violently that his head, legs, chariot and donkeys could no longer be distinguished from one another.

Ravana became very agitated when he heard about Jambumali's death. Next he ordered seven more sons of his chief minister to attack Hanuman, along with a large army. When the Raksasas approached the asoka grove, riding upon their chariots, they saw Hanuman stationed at the garden's arched gate.

In the shower of arrows that followed, Hanuman became momentarily obscured from view, but by leaping into the sky he avoided the onslaught. After roaring loudly, Hanuman charged. Within moments his hands and feet smashed innumerable Raksasas, while others were torn to pieces by his nails. Still others were crushed by the impact of Hanuman’s chest and thighs, and some simply fell to the ground, having been stunned by Hanuman's roar. When all seven sons of the chief minister had been killed, the remaining warriors panicked and ran away. Hanuman returned to the archway to await further combat.

Next Ravana sent five of his leading generals, along with a large army, saying, "I want you to capture this Hanuman, for he could not be a mere monkey. He must be some kind of super-powerful being.”

Soon after, as the Raksasas surrounded Hanuman, the general, Durdhara, released five arrows that pierced him in the forehead. Infuriated, Hanuman swelled up immensely in size, leaped into the sky, and fell on Durdhara's chariot like lightning striking a mountain. Due to the impact, the chariot was smashed to pieces and Durdhara and the horses fell down, crushed to death.

Enraged at the death of their comrade, two other generals, named Virupaksa and Yupaksa, suddenly sprang into the air and struck Hanuman in the chest with their clubs. Undaunted, Hanuman swooped down and uprooted a tall sala tree. He then struck the two generals dead.

The two remaining generals, Praghasa and Bhasakarna came before Hanuman, piercing him with their spear and dart. Although covered with blood, the enraged Hanuman tore off the peak of a nearby mountain, along with all its animals and trees, and pounded the two Raksasas to a mass of pulp.

With the five generals out of the way, Hanuman effortlessly began destroying the rest of the army. He killed the horses by wielding other dead horses, and he crushed the elephants with the help of other elephants. Hanuman struck dead the warriors with other slain soldiers and smashed their chariots by brandishing pieces of broken chariots. After covering the ground with innumerable mutilated bodies, Hanuman once again stationed himself at the garden archway.

When Ravana heard of this massacre, he simply glanced at his son, Aksa, who was seated close by. Being eager to fight, the prince understood his father's indication and jumped up from his seat in the assembly. After mounting his chariot, which was drawn by eight horses and could travel through the sky, Aksa went to the asoka grove. At a distance, he showered arrows upon Hanuman. Then, after coming close, a fierce duel took place.

Aksa was very powerful. While witnessing the encounter, the earth began to quake, the sun became dim, and the wind ceased to blow. When Aksa managed to pierce his head with three arrows, Hanuman began expanding his body. The young prince was childishly proud of his strength and so he fearlessly came before Hanuman, like an elephant approaching a well that had become covered by grass. After being struck by Aksa's arrows, Hanuman assumed an awesome form and leaped into the air. Aksa closely followed him while constantly releasing his arrows, but Hanuman dodged them while soaring through the sky.

When an arrow pierced his chest, Hanuman mentally praised the skill of his enemy and thought, "Although just a young boy, this Raksasa is fighting very heroically, and so I do not wish to kill him. On the other hand, as the battle progresses, I can see that his prowess goes on increasing. If I neglect him, I may end up being defeated. Therefore I had better kill him at once. After all, a fire that is spreading should be extinguished as quickly as possible."

While increasing his speed, Hanuman killed Aksa's horses with a slap of his hand. When the disabled chariot fell to the ground, it smashed into pieces. Taking his bow and sword, Aksa jumped out of the broken chariot and ascended into the sky like a sage on his way to heaven after quitting his material body by the power of mystic yoga. Hanuman dexterously caught Aksa by his legs, however, and after spinning him around and around, he dashed him violently to the ground. With all his limbs broken and his chest crushed, the son of Ravana gave up his life while vomiting blood.

The celestial rishis watching from the sky gazed upon Hanuman with wonder while Ravana's heart became filled with terror. As Hanuman waited at the gateway, infuriated Ravana checked his anger and summoned his eldest son, Indrajit.

The King of the Raksasas said, "In warfare, you are unparalleled, for you are my equal. You conquered the king of heaven, along with all the demigods, and you received weapons from Lord Brahma. Now, please go and do whatever is necessary to subdue this mysterious enemy."

Hanuman felt very happy to hear the twang of Indrajit's bow as he came before him riding upon his chariot. As Indrajit showered his arrows, Hanuman roared loudly and expanded himself while ascending to the sky to avoid the onslaught. In the battle that followed, Indrajit could not find any opportunity to pierce Hanuman with his arrows, nor could Hanuman get the chance to strike Indrajit.

After witnessing the futility of his weapons, Indrajit considered Hanuman incapable of being killed, and so he began to think of how to capture him instead. With this in mind, Indrajit employed a special brahmastra that immediately tied up Hanuman and made him fall to the ground, practically unconscious.

Hanuman could understand that he was being bound by the power of Lord Brahma, in the form of a weapon. At the same time, he could remember how he had received a benediction from Lord Brahma that such a weapon would lose its effectiveness a short while after being invoked. Because of this, Hanuman thought, "I cannot free myself just now, but still, I have nothing to fear. Instead of considering this to be a setback, I should take it to be a good opportunity to see Ravana close up. Even though I have been captured, I am confident that, by the benediction of Lord Brahma, I will be able to free myself before long."

Some Raksasas came and tied Hanuman with strong ropes, and while doing so they abused him with very harsh words. But, as soon as Hanuman was tied, the effects of the brahmastra became nullified, for that weapon could not tolerate co-existing with another means of bondage. Thus Hanuman allowed himself to be tied up by the Raksasas, and he pretended to feel great pain, just so he would have the chance of meeting Ravana.

Indrajit could see how Hanuman was feigning bondage after being freed form the influence of the brahmastra. Indrajit thought to himself, "Hanuman's capture has been rendered useless by these thoughtless Raksasas. Now that the brahmastra has been nullified, it cannot be invoked again against the same adversary."

While Indrajit was pondering over the matter in this way, the Raksasas dragged Hanuman into the presence of Ravana, excitedly speaking amongst themselves.

"Who is this monkey-like creature?" someone questioned, while others angrily shouted, "Kill him at once!" "Eat him up!" "Let's roast him!"

Seeing Hanuman tied up in front of him, Ravana asked his ministers to do the interrogation. In reply to their questions, Hanuman said, "I am a messenger from Sugriva, the king of the vanaras, who sends you his best wishes for your welfare. The noble-minded Sugriva hopes that you are conducting yourself according to religious principles and that, as a result, your kingdom is prospering."

Hanuman was certainly angry at having been captured, and the knowledge that Ravana had kidnapped Sitadevi surely inflamed his rage, and yet, as he gazed upon the king of the Raksasas, who was seated upon his crystal throne, he thought, "With Ravana's charm, presence of mind, courage, splendor, and auspicious bodily characteristics, he would have surpassed even Indra in glory if he had not become averse to righteousness."

Ravana was also agitated and angry, but at the same time he felt apprehensive while looking at Hanuman. He anxiously wondered, "Is this Nandi, the bull carrier of Lord Siva, who previously cursed me when I mocked him? Or is this Bana, the king of the asuras, disguised as a monkey?"

Prahasta, Ravana's chief minister, assured Hanuman, "If you simply tell us the real reason why you have come here, we will let you go."

Hanuman replied, "As a curious monkey, I came here to meet Ravana, but I knew that an insignificant creature like me would have a very hard time getting the king's audience and so I destroyed the pleasure garden, hoping to be captured and brought into the royal assembly. I never wanted to harm anyone, but when the Raksasa soldiers attacked, I was forced to kill them in self-defense."

"Long ago, I received a benediction from Lord Brahma that I cannot be bound by any weapon or ropes. Because of this, you should understand that I purposely allowed myself to be captured, and that the power of Indrajit's brahmastra has already been nullified."

"Now, please listen as I disclose to you the real reason for my coming here. My name is Hanuman, and I have come as the envoy of Lord Rama, the son of Maharaja Dasaratha. I had been searching a long time for his abducted wife, mother Sitadevi, and it is my good fortune that I have found her here. Î King of the Raksasas, you should know for certain that there is no one who is immune to the arrows of Lord Rama and Laksmana. Not even self-born Brahma, Lord Siva or Indra would dare face Lord Rama on the battlefield."

"O Ravana, you are conversant with religious principles. One who is actually wise would never court disaster by giving up the path of virtue. Please take my good advice and give mother Sitadevi back to Lord Rama before it is too late. Previously, you had become immune to death at the hands of demigods and asuras by virtue of your severe austerities. Cannot you see that, in the same way, your unrighteous act of kidnapping mother Sitadevi will bring about disastrous results? Even I could annihilate Lanka, and so what to speak of Lord Rama, who can dissolve the entire universe and then recreate it as before."

Hanuman's speech made Ravana furious. As his reddish eyes rolled in anger, He commanded, "Kill this monkey at once!"

With a gentle voice, Vibhisana advised, "My dear elder brother, it is not proper to kill a messenger. You are certainly well aware of religious texts, but all such knowledge will be made null and void if you let yourself become swayed by uncontrolled anger."

Ravana did not like such free advice, and so he replied, "There is no sin incurred for killing an evil-doer. Let Hanuman die!"

Vibhisana argued, "In sastra there is no injunction that a messenger can be killed. Such a thing is unheard of! Only mutilation of the limbs, flogging, shaving the head, and branding are punishments that can be administered."

"My dear Ravana, great heroes like you never fall prey to the influence of uncontrolled anger. I suggest that you try to kill the enemy who has sent Hanuman, and not Hanuman himself. Why don't you send your army to fight with Lord Rama and Laksmana, for it is They who seek revenge?"

Becoming a little influenced by his pious brother, Ravana finally relented, saying, "Monkeys always prize their tails very highly. Therefore, set fire to his tail and parade him through the streets so that the people can see what kind of hero he is. After that, release Hanuman, so that he can return to his relatives with a burnt tail and thus suffer great humiliation."

Being thus commanded, the Raksasas first wrapped cotton rags around Hanuman's tail and then soaked the cloth in oil. When the rags were set ablaze, Hanuman expanded himself in a fit of rage and began beating the Raksasas by lashing his tail about. The Raksasas managed to grab hold of Hanuman and tie him more tightly, because he let them do so, desiring to tour Lanka by day just to get a good look at its fortifications.

Soon after, the Raksasas joyfully dragged Hanuman through the streets, announcing to the people that they had captured a spy. As all the women, children and aged curiously came out to see the prisoner, the Raksasi guards informed Sitadevi of Hanuman's plight. Sitadevi became very aggrieved upon receiving this news, and so she meditated upon Agni and prayed, "If there is any pious credit earned by me as a result of my devotion and austerities, let it be utilized so that the fire will seem cool to Hanuman."

Just then, the sacrificial fire maintained by Sitadevi began burning mildly. At the same time, the fire on Hanuman's tail burned coolly, while Vayu blew ice-cold winds. Hanuman wondered, "Why am I not being burned, even though the flames are blazing brightly It feels as if the Raksasas have wrapped ice around my tail! This must be due to the mercy of either Lord Rama, or mother Sitadevi!"

Hanuman then considered, "It is not at all befitting for a great warrior or a servant of Lord Rama to be bound up and made a laughing-stock by these Raksasas! Enough of this humiliation!"

In the twinkling of an eye, Hanuman slipped out of his bonds by diminishing his size. Then, jumping into the air with a shout, Hanuman instantly assumed his gigantic form and picked up an iron bar he found lying nearby. In a moment he killed the guards, and then thought, "What else can I do to torment Ravana and the Raksasas before returning to Lord Rama? Since my tail is ablaze, why not use it to engulf Lanka in a great conflagration?"

Having thus made up his mind, Hanuman jumped onto the roof of Prahasta's palace and set fire there. Then, jumping from rooftop to rooftop, he ignited a great fire all over Lanka, avoiding only the palace of the pious Vibhisana. Hanuman went inside the palaces as well, including Ravana's, and because of the raging wind, the fire soon blazed out of control. As the upper stories of the palaces crumbled and crashed to the ground, the intense heat melted the gold and silver, which then mixed with pearls and jewels and flowed out into the streets like lava.

There was a great uproar amongst the Raksasas as they tried in vain to save themselves and their possessions. Anguished cries grew louder as multitudes of Raksasas, horses and elephants were burnt. Due to the fat from the burning bodies, the fire blazed higher and higher, so that it appeared to be the time of universal devastation.

In a state of panic, the inhabitants of Lanka exclaimed, "Is this Agni himself ravaging our city, in the form of a monkey? Or is it Indra, Brahma, Time personified, or the unlimited energy of Lord Visnu who has come here to destroy us all?”

Finally, after circumambulating the entire city, Hanuman paused for a moment, and as he gazed upon the ravaging fire, he felt great satisfaction. Hanuman then began to think about returning to Lord Rama, and so he first went to put out the fire on his tail by dipping it into the ocean. Then, as he once again turned to look at the fire that consumed Lanka, Hanuman suddenly became filled with a terrible apprehension while listening to the screams of the dying Raksasas.

"What a fool I have been to set fire to Lanka!" Hanuman thought. "What if mother Sitadevi has also been burnt? Oh, just see the result of uncontrolled anger, which is the most sinful condition of the living entity, because it causes him to act indiscriminately. What is there that an enraged man will not do, or refrain from saying? A person under the sway of anger can murder his own guru or parents, and insult the great souls. Only one who has learned to control his anger actually deserves to be called a human being."

"If I have caused mother Sitadevi's death, then I have killed my master as well, and so I shall have to give up my life as atonement. To hell with the foolish nature of monkeys that produces actions born of passion and anger. Passionate acts always give uncertain results, and in this case, I may have become the cause of mass destruction."

"If mother Sitadevi has died, Lord Rama and Laksmana will no longer be able to live. Then, one after another, Bharata, Satrughna, Sugriva and many others will also give up their lives. Without the shelter of these exalted personalities, all other living entities will gradually become disinterested in life. Thus it may be that I have unwittingly caused the destruction of the entire world!"

As Hanuman lamented in this way, he saw some auspicious omens. He then concluded, "Virtuous mother Sitadevi could not have been harmed. After all, by the mercy of Lord Rama, even I was not burnt by the fire, and so what to speak of His beloved consort. The purity of Sitadevi's character alone is sufficient to protect her. The strength of her austerities, her devotion to truth, and her dedication to Lord Rama are more powerful than fire.”

The Siddhas, Caranas and other celestial beings had been praising Hanuman for his heroic exploits. Now, to reassure him, they informed him that mother Sitadevi was safe and sound. Overjoyed and relieved, Hanuman rushed to where mother Sitadevi was staying, and upon seeing her, he shed tears of happiness.

Sitadevi said, "My dear Hanuman, please remain here for just one day. I get relief from my burning grief by your association. After all, your return is uncertain, and so is my survival, due to my intense unhappiness. It seems to me that my Lord, Laksmana and the monkeys will never be able to cross the ocean. Still, you must urge my husband to come here quickly and rescue me, for I do not feel that I can survive much longer under these conditions."

Hanuman tried to comfort Sitadevi by assuring her that Lord Rama would rescue her. Then, having made up his mind to depart, Hanuman went to the top of Mount Arista. Being very eager to return to Lord Rama after the successful completion of his mission, Hanuman began crushing the mountain with the pressure of his feet. As Hanuman expanded his size, the mountain began to crumble, so that the Gandharvas and animals residing there fled in fear.

When Hanuman took his mighty leap, the lofty mountain sank down to become level with the earth. Thereafter, while sailing through the air, Hanuman once again touched the Mainaka Mountain as a token of respect. At last, as he approached Mount Mahendra, Hanuman began roaring in jubilation while waving his tail, for he was very anxious to meet his monkey friends.

As they heard Hanuman roaring, all the monkeys waiting on the shore became very eager to catch a glimpse of their hero.

Jambavan said, "Judging from his exuberance, Hanuman must have been successful in his mission."

All the monkeys excitedly jumped from tree to tree and mountain peak to mountain peak, waving their cloths. When Hanuman finally came into view, they stood up with folded hands to watch him descend upon Mount Mahendra.

Within a short while, the monkeys surrounded Hanuman, shouting with delight as they presented him fruit and roots.

Hanuman first offered respect to his elders and then to prince Angada. In answer to their excited inquiries, Hanuman confirmed that he had found mother Sitadevi. The monkeys were overjoyed and so they embraced Hanuman again and again. While praising him for his heroic deed, Angada sat down to talk with Hanuman.

At that time Jambavan begged Hanuman to narrate the whole story of his jump to Lanka, and so all the monkeys crowded around to hear, waiting with folded hands. Hanuman first mentally offered obeisances to mother Sitadevi and then described all that had happened in great detail. Hanuman assured the monkeys that mother Sitadevi was alright, and he told them how she had rebuked Ravana by telling him that he was not even fit to become Lord Rama's slave.

Hanuman concluded by saying, "Let us attack Lanka at once and bring mother Sitadevi back to Lord Rama at Kiskindha. I know that I can defeat all the Raksasas single-handedly, and so can Angada. Think of how pleased Lord Rama would be if we not only mother found Sitadevi, but rescued her as well! I could have brought mother Sitadevi back here with me, but because I did not have your permission, I restrained myself."

Angada said, "Once, out of respect for the Asvini-kumaras, Lord Brahma gave their sons, Mainda and Dvivida, a benediction, making them incapable of being killed in battle. Because of this, these proud monkeys were able to defeat an entire army of demigods and forcibly drink nectar (amrta). Because they are with us, I think we can easily defeat Ravana and so let us attack Lanka without delay, and return to Lord Rama after rescuing mother Sitadevi."

Jambavan said, "O Prince, your plan lacks wisdom. You have forgotten that we were only ordered to find mother Sitadevi and not fight for her recovery. Even if we were successful, I think Lord Rama would be displeased, for He vowed in front of all the monkeys that He would kill Ravana and rescue mother Sitadevi."

Angada, Hanuman and the others accepted Jambavan's advice, and so without further ado, they left for Kiskindha. On the way, the monkeys came to Madhuvana, a heavenly forest belonging to Sugriva. Being eager to drink honey, they begged Angada to let them stop for awhile. The monkeys ate fruit, roots and honey to their full satisfaction, so that within a short time, they all became intoxicated. While laughing, dancing and singing, the monkeys sported in a very boisterous manner so that the beautiful garden became a shambles.

Dadhimukha, the superintendent of the garden, became very angry. When he came and told the monkeys to stop, they insulted him with harsh words. Just to save the garden from destruction, Dadhimukha tried to chastise some of the monkeys, pacify others, and even strike some with his hand, but this only made the intoxicated monkeys more infuriated, and they responded by kicking him, biting him and scratching him with their claws.

Hanuman encouraged the monkeys, saying, "Enjoy yourselves as you like. I will stop anyone who tries to check you."

Angada echoed this sentiment and so the emboldened monkeys fearlessly thrashed all the guards that came to stop them from stripping the garden of its edibles. Thus, the monkeys continued to act in various ways that are characteristic of those who become intoxicated. Some lay down lethargically while others of a coarser nature became very boisterous and insulting.

In the scuffle that ensued, Angada threw Dadhimukha (who happened to be his grand uncle) to the ground and beat him mercilessly. At last, Dadhimukha and the guards went to complain to Sugriva, thinking that the king would severely punish the monkeys for their misconduct.

When Dadhimukha arrived at Kiskindha, Sugriva could see that he was very agitated. In reply to the king's inquires, Dadhimukha described the misbehavior of Angada and his followers.

Just at this time, Laksmana came to see what was the matter. Sugriva told Him, "Angada and his party are now at Madhuvana, and from the description of their raucous behavior, it appears that their mission was successful. I am sure that Hanuman has found Sitadevi, otherwise, the monkeys would never dare to act with such abandon."

Lord Rama and Laksmana both felt exited to hear this, and Sugriva was also very happy. He told Dadhimukha, "Actually, your complaint conveys good news, and so the mischief of the monkeys must be tolerated. Please return to Madhuvana and tell Angada and the others that I want to see them right away."

Taking this order upon his head, Dadhimukha sprang into the air. When he arrived at Madhuvana, he found that all the monkeys were sober and discharging the transformed honey as urine. Dadhimukha went to Angada and sweetly said, "Please forgive me for having tried to forbid you from enjoying this garden. Î Prince, your uncle was exceedingly pleased to learn of your arrival, and he wants you to return to Kiskindha at once.”

Turning to his followers, Angada humbly said, "I suggest that we return to Kiskindha without delay. Of course, even though I am the prince, I do not consider myself superior to any of you. On the contrary, I feel completely dependent upon you. Therefore, I shall do whatever you recommend. I am standing here, simply awaiting your order."

Being very pleased by this statement, the monkeys replied, "O Prince, your humility is just befitting an exalted personality, and it indicates your eligibility to receive further good fortune. Let us not waste another moment, for Lord Rama and Sugriva await our arrival."

Soon after, the monkeys sprang into the air like so many stones shot from a catapult. Sugriva saw them approaching in the distance and so he went to Lord Rama and said, "Angada has come! I know that he would never have dared return to Kiskindha if he had not found Sita. And, he certainly would never have dared to devastate Madhuvana. That celestially beautiful garden was given to my father by my grandfather, Lord Brahma."

Angada's party descended upon the slopes of Mount Prasravana. As they eagerly came before Lord Rama, Hanuman excitedly declared, "mother Sitadevi is safe and sound. She has remained fixed in her vows of chastity by never once accepting Ravana's advances."

After hearing this, Lord Rama looked at Hanuman with glances laden with great love and respect. The monkeys offered their obeisances to Sugriva, Lord Rama and Laksmana. Being unable to contain themselves, they each repeated whatever they had heard from Hanuman about Sitadevi.

Finally, when the monkeys fell silent, Lord Rama said, "Describe to Me the exact location of Ravana's abode, and tell Me more about Sitadevi. How does she really feel about Me now?"

The monkeys pushed Hanuman forward so that he could give the authoritative reply. Hanuman first bowed toward the South and then he narrated the events leading up to his discovery of Sitadevi.

Hanuman gave Lord Rama the jewel from Sitadevi's head and said, "The devotion of Maharaja Janaka's daughter is completely fixed upon You without deviation. Because of this, in separation from You, she can hardly maintain her life."

At the end of his talk, Hanuman related Sitadevi's three messages. The first one described how Indra's son, in the form of a crow, had scratched her breasts. The second described how Lord Rama had decorated her face with red oxide. The third was Sitadevi's conviction that, being tortured by the Raksasis, she would surely give up her life if Lord Rama did not come to rescue her within a month's time.

As He listened, Lord Rama pressed Sitadevi's jewel to His heart. Both He and Laksmana shed incessant tears. Lord Rama then expressed His grief, saying, "This jewel was presented to Sita by her father at the time of our marriage. Long ago, King Indra had given it to Maharaja Janaka, being pleased by his performance of sacrifices."

"O Hanuman, I have become practically unconscious due to intense grief. Please repeat to Me Sita's messages, for they are just like cool water sprinkled on My head."

Hanuman once again related Sitadevi's messages and concluded by saying, "I had offered to carry her back to You, but she refused, because she was unwilling to voluntarily touch another male. Again and again mother Sitadevi told me, 'You must act in such a way that Lord Rama will quickly come and rescue me. My grief is practically unbearable, and so I do not know how much longer I will be able to go on living. Î Hanuman, how will my Lord, Laksmana and the monkeys be able to cross the ocean and come here? I know that you are able to kill Ravana and bring me back to my husband, but it is my desire that He come here and personally rescue me, so that His spotless reputation will be further enhanced. I do not want to be returned to my Lord the way I was brought to Lanka—carried by another!'"

"I assured mother Sitadevi that the other monkeys are superior to me and can easily jump over the ocean to Lanka. I also promised her that if need be, I would carry You and Laksmana on my back. In this way I was able to pacify mother Sitadevi somewhat, but in consideration of her precarious condition, I urge You to quickly devise some means whereby we can attack Lanka without further delay."

## Yuddha-kanda

Lord Rama was extremely pleased with Hanuman, being grateful for the invaluable service he had rendered. Just to praise him, Lord Rama said, "Except for Garuda, no one else could have done what you did. The best of all servants is one who, when entrusted with some task by his master, accomplishes more that what was requested. A mediocre servant is one who never tries to do more than what was ordered by the master, even though capable of doing so. That servant is the lowest of men who, even though qualified, does not carry out the order of the master.”

"My dear Hanuman, you not only found Sita, but you comforted her with your words as well. You surveyed the entire city of Lanka, you tested the strength of the great Raksasa warriors, and you struck fear into the heart of Ravana. Indeed, your service has practically saved My life. It pains Me greatly that I am unable to properly reward you. Because I am living in exile, the only thing I can call My own is My ability to embrace you."

Saying this, Lord Rama affectionately pressed Hanuman to His heart. Turning to Sugriva, Lord Rama said, "Now we know where Sita is, but how will the monkeys cross the vast ocean? It suddenly seems to Me that all our hopes and hard labor were useless!"

As Lord Rama fell silent, plunged into deep thought, Sugriva replied, "My Lord, You should cast off Your grief, just as an ungrateful person easily gives up kindness. For one who lacks an enthusiastic spirit, all activities become sources of misery, and, for one who is bewildered by grief, all endeavors end in failure. I am excited at the prospect of fighting with Ravana, and I am convinced that the monkeys are up to the task. Why not somehow build a bridge across the ocean? If the monkeys can get to Lanka, I am sure they will come out victorious."

Turning back to Hanuman, Lord Rama said, "I can easily cross the ocean by utilizing My mystic power, or, if I so desire, I can dry it up! Tell Me in more detail about Lanka's fortifications, as well as whatever other relevant information you may have gathered."

Hanuman eagerly replied, "Lanka has four types of defenses. First, it is naturally difficult to attack because it is situated high upon a mountain, encircled by a river, and surrounded by dense forests. Then, there are artificial fortifications. High golden walls surround Lanka, and there are four massive gates facing the four directions. Surrounding the walls are wide moats, and four drawbridges span these moats, leading to each of the gates. As I set fire to Lanka, I made it a point to break all the drawbridges and tear down many sections of the walls."

"My dear Lord Rama, I suggest that only the greatest warriors— Angada, Dvivida, Mainda, Jambavan, Panasa, Nila and myself—jump to Lanka. In this way we can defeat Ravana without having to worry about transporting the entire army of monkeys across the ocean."

Lord Rama said, "It is My vow that I will personally go and destroy Lanka.”

Turning to Sugriva, Lord Rama said, "The sun is now at the meridian so that the auspicious time called abhijit has arrived. This is the opportune time to begin a military campaign, and there is also trembling in my upper right eyelid, indicating victory. Let us assemble all the monkeys so that we can begin our march toward Lanka at once!"

Laksmana and Sugriva applauded Lord Rama's suggestion. Within a moment, hordes of monkeys began to emerge from the caves and wooded mountain slopes. Lord Rama ordered Nila, "I want you to march in front of the army. Take some monkeys with you and spread out in all directions, just in case there are enemy warriors waiting to ambush us. Any weak vanaras should be left behind, because our conquest is a formidable one."

Lord Rama arranged the army so that He would ride on Hanuman's back in the center of the formation, while Laksmana would ride on Angada. Sugriva passed on Lord Rama's orders to the others, and it was not long before the army set out toward the South. The powerful monkeys marched with great bravado, roaring like lions and leaping up and down. Sometimes they somersaulted or rode piggyback, and sometimes they sportingly threw one another about. Cheerful and playfully aggressive, the energetic vanaras lashed their tails about as they clamored up and down hills, uprooting plants, and dislodging great stones.

While going, Laksmana told Lord Rama, "Just see the auspicious signs that have become visible! There is a cool, fragrant breeze blowing from our back, and the forest animals are making sounds indicating their satisfaction. The land itself seems to be in a peaceful mood, and overhead, the sun is shining brightly."

The army marched on, day and night, for they were very eager to fight for Sitadevi's recovery. While going, the monkeys bathed and played in the mountain lakes. They ate the fruit and roots and drank the honey provided by the forests. At last, when they came to Mount Mahendra, Lord Rama climbed to the peak. From there He could see the vast ocean, stretching out in the distance until it touched the horizon. After Lord Rama rejoined them at the bottom of the hill, the monkeys marched on until they came to the seashore.

Lord Rama told Sugriva, "Now we are faced with the very problem we had long feared. How are we going to cross this expansive ocean? Let the monkeys set up camp in the nearby forest while we sit down and devise some means for reaching Lanka."

As they busied themselves making their camp, the hordes of monkeys appeared like a second great ocean, agitated with tossing brown waves. The leaders of the monkeys gazed with wonder at the vast ocean, which is the refuge of the asuras and which goes all the way down to the Patala planetary system. With wide-open eyes they stared at the fathomless sea, infested with aquatic monsters, such as the huge timi fish, and the timingilas that can swallow them. As if stunned with astonishment, the monkey heroes beheld the impassable expanse of wave-clashing, wind-whipped water, having Lanka at its far side.

While sitting at ease, Lord Rama said to Laksmana, "Usually, grief gradually disappears with the passage of time, but in My case, with the passing of each day, My anguish goes on increasing because of separation from Sita. Î Laksmana, the most painful thing for Me is the thought of how Sita's allotted time is steadily slipping away. My heart burns with longing for the time when I will be able to kill Ravana and rescue Sita. Only then will I be able to cast off My grief once and for all, just as a person throws away his old, worn-out clothes."

As Lord Rama continued to vent His anguish by lamenting to Laksmana at great length, the sun gradually sank below the horizon and darkness set in.

Meanwhile, in Lanka, after witnessing Hanuman's devastating prowess, Ravana called for a meeting of all the leading Raksasas. After they had assembled, the Raksasa king said, "Impenetrable Lanka has been ravaged, my palace is in ruins, and many of the best Raksasa warriors are dead. According to authoritative opinion, the root cause of victory is good council, and that is why I have called all of you here."

"There are three classes of men in this world. Before initiating any important work, a wise man will take advice from those who are superior, as well as from friends who have a common interest. Then, according to the advice received, he will exert himself to the best of his capacity while depending upon Destiny for the result. The mediocre person thinks over a matter by himself, considering things in the light of his own intelligence, and then acts accordingly. The vilest of men are those with no faith in God, who act whimsically, without any sense of duty. Being unable to properly judge that which is beneficial and that which is harmful, such persons proceed blindly, saying, 'I shall do it, no matter what!'"

"Similarly, there are three kinds of advice. Good advice is that which is given after an objective study of the problem, and which is in agreement with religious principles. Mediocre advice is that which is given after a heated discussion of the problem, and which places more emphasis on limited self-interest than religious principles. Bad advice is that which is given out of false pride, or to flatter, and which does not properly take into account consideration of the ultimate consequences."

"I am convinced that very soon Lord Rama will attack Lanka, along with a vast army of monkeys. His prowess was demonstrated at Janasthana, and so I have no doubt that He will be able to cross the ocean without difficulty. My dear Raksasas, all of you are very intelligent and so I want your advice about what should be done for our welfare."

Being ignorant of Lord Rama's strength and eager to please their master, the Raksasas replied, "O King, why should you be afraid? You are powerful enough to defeat all your enemies single- handedly. Just remember how you defeated Kuvera, taking Lanka from him. Please remember how Maya Danava fearfully handed over his daughter, Mandodari, to you. Why should you worry? You can rest peacefully while your son, Indrajit, goes and kills Lord Rama and all the monkeys before they even cross the ocean. After defeating the demigods, Indrajit arrested the king of heaven and kept him captive in Lanka. Only at Lord Brahma's request was Indra allowed to go free and return to his heavenly post."

Ravana's commander-in-chief, Prahasta, said, "We have conquered the demigods, Danavas, Gandharvas and Pisacas. Why should we be afraid of mere mortals? Hanuman was only able to exert his prowess because we were not alert, considering him to be mere monkey."

Durmukha stood up and declared, "I will not allow this insult to go unavenged! Wherever they may be—in heaven, on earth, or within the sea—I shall rid the earth of every single vanara!"

While brandishing a club that was stained with flesh and blood, Vajradamstra angrily shouted, "Who cares for a bunch of monkeys? It is Lord Rama and Laksmana that I will crush to death! O King, just give me the order!"

Kumbhakarna's powerful son, Nikumbha, then bellowed, "All of you can remain here with your master. I will go and single- handedly vanquish Lord Rama and the monkeys!"

In this way many of the Raksasa heroes boasted of their prowess by assuring Ravana that they could conquer the enemy single- handedly. While brandishing their weapons, the agitated Raksasas were on the verge of departing for battle, but Vibhisana politely restrained them.

When all were seated and order was restored, Vibhisana said, "My dear elder brother, the wise have advised that violence should only be resorted to after the other three tactics of conciliation have failed. Even then, violence succeeds only against those who are evil, unwary, already under siege by another enemy, or doomed by fate. Lord Rama is supremely powerful and virtuous and He is eager to fight for revenge. In consideration of this, how can you hope to defeat Him? Sitadevi's abduction is the root cause of our present crisis, and so you had better return her to Lord Rama before He destroys Lanka and all its inhabitants."

After hearing Vibhisana's advice, Ravana dismissed the assembly and retired to his quarters. The next morning, Vibhisana came to Ravana as he was seated upon his throne, listening to the brahmanas offer prayers for his welfare.

After taking his seat nearby, Vibhisana said, "My dear brother, ever since you brought Sitadevi here, many inauspicious omens have become visible. The sacrificial fire now gives off sparks and smoke. Snakes are often found inside the kitchens and sacrificial arenas. The sacrificial offerings are sometimes full of ants. Crows perch atop the palaces. Vultures continually hover over the city. Female jackals can be heard crying out ominously every morning and evening. This is all due your sinful act of kidnapping Sitadevi."

"O Ravana, the only atonement for you is to return her to Lord Rama at once. I am speaking honestly, whereas your other ministers simply flatter you because they are afraid that you will become displeased with them."

Ravana was overwhelmed by the passionate desire to enjoy Sitadevi and so he became angry upon hearing Vibhisana's advice. Practically shouting, Ravana replied, "I am not afraid of Lord Rama or anyone else, and so I will never agree to return Sita! My dear younger brother, please leave me alone and go about your business.”

Ravana had become emaciated due to his unfulfilled passion for Sitadevi, and because of his abominable acts, even relatives had begun to disrespect him. Knowing that war was immanent, the king of the Raksasas wanted to further consult his ministers and so he ordered them to convene once again.

When Ravana arrived at the assembly, riding upon his chariot, everyone offered their obeisances to him by bowing their heads to the floor while thousands of trumpets heralded his arrival. First Ravana ordered Prahasta, "Make sure that our army is prepared to defend Lanka from within and without."

After Prahasta had left to put the army on alert, Ravana said, "My dear Raksasas, I am pleased to announce that, after sleeping for six months, Kumbhakarna has awakened and now graces us with his presence. Please listen attentively, for I want all of you to fully understand my position. As you know, I have become obsessed with a single-minded attraction for lovely Sita. Truthfully, I am no longer the master of myself, for I have become the slave of my passion for her."

Next, just to enhance his public image, Ravana spoke untruthfully, saying, "Sita has agreed to become my consort, but only after the expiry of one year, for that is the time she has allotted for Lord Rama to come and rescue her. It is for this reason that I have remained patient, but now it appears that Lord Rama, Laksmana and a vast army of monkeys are preparing to attack Lanka. Truthfully, I do not feel that two human beings and a band of monkeys can pose much of a threat. Still, because one monkey, Hanuman, was able to inflict so much damage, I must admit that victory is uncertain. That is why I have called all of you together. I want you to advise me how I can kill Lord Rama and thus keep beautiful Sita for myself."

Kumbhakarna stood up and spoke sharply, "Foolish king, you should have consulted us when you were planning to kidnap Sita, instead of acting impulsively. That would have saved you from repenting later on. Never mind, you can give up all your anxiety. I will counteract your blunder by killing Lord Rama and Laksmana and devouring all the monkeys."

Mahaparsva said, "O King, why should one decide not to taste the honey that was procured with great endeavor after searching through a snake-infested forest? You can forcibly enjoy Sita to your heart’s content. Who can stop you? No one is as powerful as you, and so you can do whatever you like without fear."

Ravana said, "There is something in my past I have always kept a secret, but now that you have spoken as you have, I will disclose to you an incident that happened long ago. Once, I happened to see the incomparably beautiful Apsara, Punjikasthali, as she was on her way to offer her respects to Lord Brahma. The very sight of the Apsara inflamed me with desire and so I forcibly grabbed her and raped her. After gratifying my urge, I let her go and she ran, naked, to the shelter of Lord Brahma."

The lord became so angry when he learned what I had done that he cursed me by saying, "You wicked king of the Raksasas, your head will split into one hundred pieces if you ever try to forcibly rape any woman again.”

"My dear Mahaparsva, it is out of fear of this curse that I do not drag Sita to my bed by force. I am not afraid of Lord Rama, though, for I know that I am the most powerful created being in the universe. Lord Rama is obviously ignorant of my prowess, and if He dares to attack me, I will make short work of Him.”

Vibhisana said, "My dear Ravana, can't you see that Sitadevi is just like a poisonous snake you have tied around your neck? Use your good intelligence and return Sitadevi to Lord Rama before she becomes the cause of Lanka's destruction and the annihilation of all the Raksasas. I can assure you that there is no Raksasa warrior who will be able to stand before Lord Rama on the battlefield and live to tell about it."

Prahasta heatedly asked, "Why should we be afraid of a mere human being like Lord Rama, when we have nothing to fear from even the greatest demigods and asuras?

As Ravana's well-wisher, Vibhisana replied, "Lord Rama possesses unlimited and inconceivable potencies, on a level with Lord Visnu. Therefore, Prahasta, you will do your king a great service if you dissuade him from fighting with Lord Rama. Actually, you are doing Ravana the greatest disservice by encouraging him to fight."

Then, turning to Ravana, Vibhisana said "I am only thinking of your welfare when I say that you should return Sitadevi to Lord Rama. The minister who measures the relative strengths of the king and his enemy and gives advice accordingly is the true well-wisher.”

Being unable to tolerate his uncle's words any longer, Indrajit excitedly interrupted, saying, "O Vibhisana, you are simply a coward and a eunuch! Your advice has no place in this assembly, for it is devoid of courage and heroism. Previously, I dragged Indra and his carrier, Airavata, to the ground making all the demigods flee in fear. It will be easy for me to kill two ordinary human beings like Rama and Laksmana.

Vibhisana harshly replied, "You are a mere boy. Because your intelligence is not yet developed, you cannot properly decide what is to be done and what is to be avoided. You are actually Ravana's enemy and not his son, because you are dull-headed, indiscriminate, uncultured and wicked. Sitadevi should be given back to Lord Rama, along with abundant gifts, so that the Raksasas may continue to live peacefully."

Ravana also became tired of hearing Vibhisana's advice. Just to chastise his youngest brother, the king said, "It is better to live with an enemy or a poisonous snake than a person who claims to be a friend but is actually a traitor. Especially if he is one's brother! It now appears to be true that a man's relatives are the ones that rejoice the most when he encounters misfortune. Once, when some elephants saw hunters approaching, they recited the following two verses:

Fire and weapons we do not fear,  
The dangerous are the so-called near and dear.  
They are the ones who take special pains  
To make sure all of us are put in chains.

"From cows we derive milk, in brahmanas we find tapasya, in women we see fickleness, and from relatives we are put into danger. O Vibhisana, you are envious, and that is why you cannot tolerate the honor given to me. If anyone else had spoken as you did, I would have killed him immediately, but I will only say this much, wretched brother—you are a disgrace to our noble family!"

Vibhisana also became angered while being rebuked by his brother. Club in hand, he rose into the air, along with his four followers, and declared, "O King, although you are my superior, I can no longer tolerate your words, for you have chosen to follow the path of irreligion. Flatterers that only speak agreeable words are easy to find. On the other hand, persons who speak for one's benefit, even when the truth is unpalatable, are rarely seen.”

"O Ravana, I advised you in the hopes of saving you from being killed by Lord Rama. Still, you have rejected my words. Of course, you are free to do as you like, but I will no longer remain here with you."

Vibhisana and his followers departed, and within an hour they reached the place where Lord Rama was staying. When the monkeys saw Vibhisana hovering overhead, Sugriva told Hanuman, "These Raksasas must have come here to try and kill us!"

The monkeys quickly picked up rocks and uprooted trees while awaiting Sugriva's instructions. Vibhisana then announced, "O King of the monkeys, I am Ravana's youngest brother. I repeatedly advised the king to return Sitadevi to Lord Rama, but he rebuked me with harsh words. I have left my wife and children to come here and take shelter at Lord Rama's lotus feet. Please inform Him of my intentions."

Sugriva went to Lord Rama and said, "One of the enemy Raksasas has come here. He says that he has deserted Ravana, but I feel that he cannot be trusted. You can never trust a Raksasa and so I think that he must be a spy. If we trust him, then, when least expected, he may do us great harm. I suggest that we immediately kill him."

Lord Rama went and asked the others to give their opinions. The monkeys replied, "My dear Lord Rama, You know everything. Therefore, we can understand that You are inquiring from us just to give us respect."

Angada suggested, "If we can use this Raksasa to our advantage, then we could accept him with great caution, but if he poses too much of a threat, it is better that we turn him away."

Sarabha suggested, "Let us assign someone to spy on the Raksasa. After being thoroughly examined, if he is found to actually be our ally, he should be welcomed."

Jambavan warned, "This Raksasa should be regarded with great suspicion," and Mainda advised, "He should be thoroughly interrogated before we make up our mind."

Wise Hanuman, who was also gifted with the art of fine speech, said, "We do not have time to test Vibhisana, but in my opinion, he has come here in all sincerity to take shelter of Lord Rama. He has understood that Ravana is wicked and that Lord Rama is the supreme emblem of purity and righteousness. His peaceful demeanor and considerate speech are indications of the honesty of his purpose, for a deceitful person cannot remain so perfectly composed. It is not possible for anyone to fully conceal his inner intentions. One's facial expressions always give some clue to his thoughts. I think that Vibhisana can be accepted as our ally without reservations."

Lord Rama was very pleased with Hanuman's speech, but still, Sugriva argued, "Since Vibhisana has deserted his brother at a time of adversity, it can be understood that there is no one he would not betray."

Lord Rama said, "I believe that Vibhisana has genuinely rejected Ravana. After all, such dissension often occurs in royal families. Let us welcome him as our ally."

Sugriva meekly protested, "Maybe he was sent by Ravana. Just to be on the safe side, we should immediately capture or kill him. Otherwise, if we trust him, he may turn on us at any moment."

Lord Rama smiled and said, "Do you really think that this Raksasa could harm Me? With the mere tip of My finger I could kill all the Raksasas and asuras. Now, please listen attentively as I narrate to you a story."

"Once there was a hunter who caught a pigeon in his snare and then approached a nearby tree to rest. The pigeon's wife was living in that tree, and when she saw that the hunter had come to her residence, she offered him all hospitality. Because she had nothing else to feed the hunter, the female pigeon offered her own flesh, just to fulfill her duty in the matter of receiving a guest."

"My dear Sugriva, if the female pigeon acted like this, then what must I do? Revealed texts again and again say that when an enemy arrives with folded hands, he must be protected by all means. Besides this moral consideration, as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, it is My eternal principle that if any living being takes shelter of Me, even once, saying 'I am Yours,' then I award that person freedom from all fear. Even if Ravana were to come here and surrender to Me, I would give him all protection."

Sugriva's heart became filled with love while hearing this sublime statement. As tears fell from his eyes, he admitted, "My dear Lord, Your words and deeds are always befitting Your supreme position. I also feel that Vibhisana is sincere. Let us make friendship with him without delay."

After being assured of safety, Vibhisana descended to the earth and fell down flat at Lord Rama's lotus feet. In full surrender he explained, "I am Ravana's youngest brother and my name is Vibhisana. I tried to give my brother good instruction for his benefit, but he responded by harshly rebuking me. Because of this, I have abandoned my home, family and possessions, and left Lanka for good to engage in Your unalloyed devotional service. I now place my life in Your hands and beg You to bless me with Your causeless mercy."

While lovingly gazing upon him, as if drinking him with His eyes, Lord Rama replied by requesting Vibhisana to describe the strengths and weaknesses of the enemy. Vibhisana said, "Because of the benedictions received from Lord Brahma, Ravana has become immune to death at the hands of demigods, Gandharvas, Daityas, Danavas and Nagas. Only human beings can possibly kill Ravana. Because he considered them too insignificant, Ravana did not bother to ask Lord Brahma for immunity from death at their hands."

"Ravana's younger brother, Kumbhakarna, is as big as a great mountain, and as powerful as Indra. Prahasta, the commander-in-chief of the Raksasas, once defeated the Yaksa hero Manibhadra at Mount Kailasa. Ravana's son, Indrajit, is equal to his father in all respects. He wears impenetrable armor, and after propitiating the fire god, he is able to make himself invisible on the battlefield. Besides these, there are millions of other Raksasas, headed by Mahodara, Mahaparsva and Akampana, and all of them are very fierce and can change their forms at will."

Lord Rama said, "My dear Vibhisana, I am well aware of Ravana's prowess. I give you My word that after killing him and all the other Raksasa warriors I will install you upon the royal throne at Lanka."

In response, Vibhisana assured Lord Rama that he would help Him conquer Lanka. Lord Rama was very satisfied with His devotee. After warmly embracing Vibhisana, Lord Rama ordered Laksmana to go and bring water from the sea so that He could immediately perform the installation ceremony. By witnessing the extraordinary mercy of Lord Rama upon the Raksasa, all the monkeys became ecstatic and shouted with joy.

Hanuman and Sugriva said, "O Vibhisana, we feel confident about combating the Raksasas, but we are perplexed about how to cross the vast and unfathomable sea. Perhaps you could advise us."

Vibhisana replied, "I suggest that Lord Rama call upon the presiding deity of the ocean. Previously, His forefather, King Sagara, had excavated the earth and thus extended the ocean's domain in the process. Due to this past service rendered, the ocean will certainly help Lord Rama accomplish His mission out of a sense of gratitude."

Sugriva relayed this proposal to Lord Rama and Laksmana. Lord Rama considered it to be a very good suggestion, but just to honor Sugriva, He said, "I will do whatever you and Laksmana decide.”

Sugriva and Laksmana agreed with Vibhisana and so Lord Rama immediately went to the beach and sat down on a mat of kusa grass, His face turned toward the sea.

Meanwhile, a spy named Sardula spotted the monkeys and reported to Ravana how the army had set up camp, covering a vast area. The Raksasa king then sent his envoy, Suka, to deliver a message to Sugriva.

Taking the form of a bird, Suka flew to where Sugriva was staying. From the sky, he delivered Ravana's message, "I have never done you any harm, and so why are you preparing to attack Lanka? Because the kidnapping of Sita has nothing to do with you, it would be better for you to return to Kiskindha and continue living there peacefully."

As he was speaking, some of the monkeys jumped into the air and captured Suka. After dragging him to the ground, the monkeys cut off his wings and beat him severely.

Suka cried out, "O Lord Rama, those who adhere to righteousness never indulge in killing an envoy."

Lord Rama intervened, and after being released, Suka again rose into the sky and asked Sugriva if he had a message for Ravana.

Sugriva said, "You can reply to your master as follows, "O Ravana, you are just like the stool of your dynasty, for you wish to enjoy the faithful wife of another. As a consequence, when my army of monkeys swarms over the sea to Lanka, Lord Rama will kill you, along with all your relatives."

Angada said, "This bird does not appear to be an envoy. I think he is a spy who will convey information about the strength of our army to the enemy. We should arrest him at once!"

Taking this as an order, the monkeys sprang into the air. After capturing Suka, they bound him with ropes. Suka again appealed to Lord Rama and the Lord mercifully assured him that he would be released as soon as they reached Lanka.

Thereafter, with folded hands, Lord Rama solicited the presiding deity of the ocean as He continued to sit on a kusa grass mat. Lord Rama was determined to reach Lanka by any means, and He was prepared to kill the ocean-god if he refused to co-operate. Finally, after three days and nights, when there was still no response from the ocean-god, Lord Rama became infuriated.

Addressing Laksmana, He said, "I can now practically see that the good qualities of patience, forgiveness, simplicity and politeness are useless when dealing with wretched people who have no trace of virtue! In this world, such persons give more respect to someone who is impudent, aggressive and harsh in speech, and who runs here and there, advertising himself with self-praise. Because of My mildness and forbearance, the ocean-god considers Me to be impotent. That is why he does not even deign to come before Me."

"O Laksmana, give Me My bow so that I can teach this ocean- god a lesson! Watch as I dry up all the water so that the monkeys can march to Lanka on foot without difficulty."

His anger blazing like fire, Lord Rama grabbed His mighty bow. After stringing it, when Lord Rama twanged the bow with great force, the earth began to tremble. Lord Rama then shot His arrows deep into the water, causing the ocean to become agitated with high, tossing waves, terrifying the creatures within.

Next, when Lord Rama invoked the supremely powerful brahmastra, Laksmana put his hand on Lord Rama's bow and said, "My dear brother, please restrain Your anger and do not release any more arrows. There must be a nobler means of drying up the ocean so that the monkeys can march to Lanka."

From their position in the sky, the great brahmarsis were terrified because of Lord Rama's exhibition of anger. Ignoring Laksmana's plea, Lord Rama picked up the brahmastra arrow and loudly threatened, "I will dry up all your water so that you will remain a desert of sand. Î god of the sea, since you are too proud to render Me service, I will utilize My own prowess so that the monkeys can cross you on foot!"

When Lord Rama angrily drew back His bowstring, heaven and earth began to tremble as darkness enveloped the entire sky. Celestial winds raged with fury, uprooting tall trees and tearing off the peaks of mountains. Lightning streaked across the sky, as did hundreds of meteors, and thunder could be heard reverberating in all directions. The ocean overflowed, filling all beings with terror, but Lord Rama remained unmoved, fixed in His determination.

Suddenly, the ocean-god rose up from the water and came before Lord Rama, surrounded by many serpents with flaming mouths. As giant alligators, tortoises and fish were being thrown up by the billowing waves, the presiding deity of the ocean stepped onto the shore. Following him were the presiding goddesses of numerous rivers, such as the Ganga and Indus. Decorated with gold ornaments and a garland of red flowers, dressed in red cloth, and encircled by clouds and wind, the ocean-god approached Lord Rama with folded hands.

The god of the ocean said, "O gentle descendent of Raghu, earth, water, fire, air and ether are eternally imbued with their natural characteristics. Being a great reservoir of water, I am by nature unfathomable and uncrossable. O Lord Rama, it is for You alone that I will make a concession that will enable You to cross my water. If You construct a bridge, I will make it float by supporting its weight with my energy. In this way the hordes of monkeys can attack Lanka, and You can recover Your dear wife, Sitadevi."

While standing with the brahmastra arrow pulled back to His ear, Lord Rama replied, "First, tell Me where I can shoot this arrow, for having placed it on My bowstring, I am unwilling to withdraw it."

The Ocean personified said, "To the north there is a holy place known as Drumakulya, where a fierce tribe of sinful thieves called Abhiras are living. They drink the water of the ocean and I am repulsed by their sinful touch. My Lord, I would be very pleased if You would let Your powerful arrow fall there."

Lord Rama released His arrow as requested, and it fell at Drumakulya. After the brahmastra pierced the earth, water from Rasatala gushed through the crevice and as a result, the entire subterranean region became dried up. The place where the arrow fell became known as Marukantara, and Lord Rama gave the place this benediction, "This land will become verdant with fruit, honey and all kinds of herbs. It will be excellent for raising cattle, and those who reside there will remain free from all diseases."

The Ocean personified said, "My dear Lord Rama, here is Nala, the son of the celestial engineer and architect, Visvakarma. This powerful monkey is Your great devotee and he is as talented as his father. He can oversee the construction of Your bridge."

After saying this, the presiding deity of the ocean disappeared from view. Nala then came before Lord Rama. After offering his obeisances, he said, "Forbearance, conciliation and gifts are wasted upon persons who are ungrateful. I know that the ocean-god has granted You passage only out of fear of punishment, and not from a sense of gratitude."

"My dear Lord, once, long ago, my father, Visvakarma, gave my mother the benediction that she would have a son equal to him in all respects. For this reason I possess all of Visvakarma's architectural and engineering skills, and so I am quite capable of building the required bridge. Although I always had these talents, no one knew of them before. This is because I was never asked about such things, and I do not like to speak about my own abilities. Now, I suggest that the monkeys gather the required materials so that we can begin work as soon as possible."

Thereafter, under Lord Rama's supervision, all the monkeys entered the forests. After tearing off great rocks, uprooting trees, and unearthing whole hills, they brought them to the shore. When all these huge stones and trees were thrown into the ocean, the water splashed high into the sky, creating a magnificent sight.

In this way the bridge was constructed—one hundred yojanas long and ten yojanas wide. The surface was made smooth by lining up tree trunks and covering them with branches full of blossoming flowers. While work was going on, Vibhisana and his ministers kept guard on shore.

During the first day, fourteen yojanas were completed. Twenty more yojanas were built the second day, and on the third day twenty-one yojanas were added. Twenty-two yojanas were constructed the fourth day, and the work was completed on the fifth day.

The demigods and celestial rishis had assembled in the sky to behold the wonderful bridge, which looked like the Milky Way spanning the deep blue sea. Sugriva requested Lord Rama and Laksmana to mount upon the backs of Hanuman and Angada. Within a short time, the entire army, consisting of thousands of crores of monkeys, began their march.

When they reached Suvela Mountain on the northern shore of the island of Lanka, the monkeys became jubilant. Sugriva set up camp, and at that time all the great demigods and rishis came there. While individually bathing the king of the monkeys with water from sacred rivers, they blessed him for obtaining victory.

Lord Rama embraced Laksmana and said, "Make sure that the army stays on constant alert. I can see evil omens that predict the death of many great heroes among the monkeys, bears and Raksasas. Just see how fierce winds are stirring up clouds of dust! The earth sometimes trembles, and dark clouds are raining blood! The evening twilight is very heavily tinged with red, and wild animals are crying out in pitiful tones."

"O Laksmana, I think that we should immediately begin our march!"

The monkeys were soon mobilized, and as they approached Lanka, all the Raksasas heard their loud roars. While gazing upon the golden city, magnificently perched upon the top of Trikuta Mountain, Lord Rama's mind turned to thoughts of Sitadevi.

Lord Rama gave orders for the military commanders to arrange the army in a human-shaped formation, with Himself and Laksmana at the head. All the monkeys picked up big trees and massive boulders. When they came closer to Lanka, Lord Rama ordered Sugriva to release Suka.

Suka went and presented himself before Ravana. When the king saw how Suka's wings had been cut off, he laughingly inquired, "Who has done this?"

Suka replied, "I delivered your message to Sugriva, but as I was doing so, some of the monkeys captured me. While beating me savagely, they cut off my wings. It was only by the mercy of virtuous Lord Rama that I was released. Î King, the army of monkeys has already come here to rescue Sitadevi. You had better return her to Lord Rama, or else attack the monkeys before they swarm over our boundary walls."

Ravana angrily said, "I will never give up Sita! I will kill Lord Rama and all His monkey soldiers, but I must admit that I am amazed that the monkeys could build a bridge over the ocean to come here! Now, I want you and Sarana to disguise yourselves as monkeys and secretly enter the enemy ranks, just to estimate their strength."

Suka and Sarana obediently went to where the monkeys had set up camp. However, due to the vastness of the army, which was spread out throughout the forest, the mountains, and along the shore, the two spies could not even begin to estimate the number of soldiers. Alert Vibhisana then spotted the two disguised Raksasas, and after capturing them, he brought them to Lord Rama.

Being afraid for their lives, Suka and Sarana stood before Lord Rama with folded hands and pleaded, "We have not come here of our own accord. We were sent by Ravana to ascertain the strength of Your army.”

Lord Rama laughed and said, "If you have accomplished your mission, you can return to Ravana right away, but if you have not finished your observations, you can continue your work without fear, being guided by Vibhisana. In return for our hospitality, I only request you to deliver this message to Ravana, 'At dawn tomorrow morning, I and My army of monkeys will destroy Lanka and kill all the Raksasas.'"

Out of gratitude, Suka and Sarana offered obeisances to Lord Rama and said, "May You be victorious!"

They quickly returned to Ravana and said, "We were captured by Vibhisana but then mercifully released by the pious and magnanimous Lord Rama. Due to its vastness, it was impossible for us to estimate the extent of the enemy army. However, we can assure you that Lord Rama, Laksmana, Sugriva and Vibhisana can uproot Lanka and carry it away if they choose to do so, even without the help of the other monkeys. In fact, we are convinced that Lord Rama alone could single-handedly destroy all the Raksasas and their city. Therefore, we advise you to return Sitadevi to Lord Rama and establish an alliance of friendship with Him."

Ravana said, "I will never give back Sita, even if all the demigods and asuras combine to attack me. You are only speaking such rubbish because you are afraid after being mistreated by the monkeys. What have I got to fear?”

Afterwards, Ravana climbed onto the roof of his palace, along with his two spies, so that he could get a good view of the enemy. Ravana asked Sarana to point out and identify the chief monkeys. In response, Sarana showed his master all the great heroes, headed by Hanuman, Sugriva, Angada, Mainda, Dvivida, Sveta, Panasa, Vinata, Gavaya, and finally, Dhumra, the commander-in-chief of the bears, and his younger brother, Jambavan. While pointing them out, Sarana described their physical characteristics and residences, and praised their incomparable prowess.

Next, while describing the extent of the enemy army, Sarana explained the Vedic system of counting, "100,000 is one laksa (lakh). 100 laksas equals one koti (crore). One laksa of kotis is called a sankha, and one laksa sankhas is called a maha-sankha. One laksa maha-sankhas is called a vrnda, and one laksa vrndas is called a maha-vrnda. One laksa maha-vrndas is called a padma, and one laksa padmas is called a maha-padma. One laksa maha-padmas is called a kharva, and one laksa kharvas is a maha-kharva. One laksa maha-kharvas is called a samudra, and one laksa samudras is an ogha. One laksa oghas is called a mahaugha, and the army of monkeys is described by Sarana as consisting of at least 100 koti of mahaughas.

Ravana became highly enraged and agitated at heart after seeing Lord Rama, Laksmana, and all the monkey heroes. As Suka and Sarana hung their heads, Ravana chastised them severely, although trying to restrain his anger.

Ravana said, "You are supposed to be my ministers and yet you are praising the enemy. Your speech is most unpalatable. Both of you are stupid, being ignorant of political science. I must be very fortunate to have retained my sovereignty so long, despite being guided by such ignoramuses as you. How can you speak so foolishly? Do you not have any fear of death? It is only the memory of your past service that keeps me from killing you this very moment!"

Suka and Sarana became very ashamed while hearing Ravana chastise them. Hoping to pacify their master, they said, “O King, my victory be yours!" Suka and Sarana then departed.

Mahodara was standing nearby and so Ravana ordered him to bring some more spies. After awhile, Sardula and others arrived while pronouncing benedictions for Ravana's victory. After being ordered to go and ascertain the enemy's plans, these spies circumambulated Ravana and went to where Lord Rama was staying. Although they were disguised as monkeys, Vibhisana soon detected them and had them arrested. Some of the monkeys began beating the enemy spies, but as before, when it came to His attention, Lord Rama mercifully ordered them to be released.

After returning to Lanka in a stupefied condition, Sardula and his followers came before Ravana and reported, "The army of monkeys is now camped near Suvela Mountain, but it is incapable of being spied upon. Soon after our arrival, Vibhisana detected us. It is only by the grace of Lord Rama that we were able to return here alive."

"O King, it seems to us that Lord Rama is capable of destroying not only all the Raksasas, but the entire universe as well. In any case, you must either return Sitadevi to Lord Rama at once, or else quickly prepare to fight with His army before they reach Lanka's boundary walls."

Ravana thought about Sardula's words for awhile and then said, "I will never return Sitadevi, under any circumstance!"

After retiring to his private quarters, Ravana called for Vidyujjihva, who was a master of conjuring tricks. Ravana told him, "I want you to create an illusory head of Lord Rama, as well as a perfect imitation of His powerful bow, and an arrow. I am going now to the asoka grove to see Sita, and you should also go there and remain hidden. When I call for you, bring me your magical creations."

Ravana went to the asoka grove, being very eager to see Sitadevi. Coming before the anguished daughter of King Janaka, Ravana announced, "Lord Rama has been killed by my commander- in-chief, and so you should give up your stubbornness and become my beloved queen. I will tell you exactly what happened. After crossing the ocean, night set in. Being exhausted, Lord Rama, Laksmana and all the monkey soldiers fell asleep on the shore."

"In the darkness, the great Raksasa warriors went and began slaughtering the monkeys. Prahasta cut off Lord Rama's head as He soundly slept. Laksmana, Hanuman and many other monkey chiefs were also killed, while others managed to run away. Noble lady, I have brought Lord Rama's severed head, just to convince you that I am telling the truth."

Ravana ordered one of the Raksasi guards to call for Vidyujjihva. Within a moment, the magician came there, holding an illusory head of Lord Rama, as well as a bow and arrow. Ravana declared, "Look at the blood-stained head of your husband!"

Turning to Vidyujjihva, he said, "Give Sita Rama's severed head. Let her see the remains of her mortal husband."

As commanded, Vidyujjihva placed the illusory head at Sitadevi's feet and hastily departed. Ravana took the imitation bow, and after throwing it in front of Sitadevi, commanded, "Submit to me, for you have no other hope!"

The features of the illusory head exactly resembled Lord Rama's, and when she saw it, Sitadevi cried out piteously, "O mother Kaikeyi, this is the result of your evil-minded plans. Your cherished goal is now fulfilled and so you must be very happy!"

Sitadevi was sobbing convulsively so that her entire body trembled. After speaking, she fell unconscious onto the ground, like a banana tree uprooted by a strong wind.

After some time, when she came to her senses, Sitadevi sat beside the illusory head and lamented, "O my Lord! Without You I have become a widow and so my life has also come to an end! What greater disaster could befall a woman than the death of her husband before her own? Oh! I am most abominable, for it is I that has caused the death of my husband! It is for my sake alone that He crossed the ocean and died without even a fight. Little did He know that when He married me, He wedded His death as well! I am sure that in a previous life I obstructed the marriage of some poor girl so that now I am suffering in this life. Oh my Lord! Have you departed for the next world without me?"

Sitadevi turned to Ravana and said, "Take me to where my Lord body is lying. When I place my body upon His, you can kill me so that I will attain the same destination as my husband."

Just at that moment, a messenger arrived and informed Ravana that Prahasta was urgently requesting his presence at a meeting of all the ministers. Ravana left, and as soon as he was gone, the illusory head and bow also vanished from Sitadevi's sight.

Upon entering the assembly, Ravana ordered the mobilization of his army. Thus, without further discussion, preparation for war commenced. At this time, Vibhisana's wife, Sarama, came to the asoka grove to console Sitadevi. Sarama had already befriended her at Ravana's urging, for he was afraid that Sitadevi might die prematurely due to intense grief.

Sarama said, "I was hiding behind a bush and so I could see and hear everything. Let me assure you that Lord Rama is not dead. The head you saw was an illusion created by the Raksasa conjuring art. The truth is that Lord Rama has arrived at Lanka, along with Laksmana and a huge army of monkeys. They are this minute preparing to attack Ravana. That is why the Raksasa king suddenly left in such an agitated state. He knows that he cannot defeat Lord Rama and the monkey heroes under His protection."

"Even from here I can hear the Raksasas making preparations, because soon there will be a terrible war between the two armies. Do not worry, Sitadevi, for Lord Rama will kill Ravana without a doubt. If you would like to give Lord Rama a message, I can go now and deliver it to Him."

Sitadevi was greatly relieved to hear this. She replied, "O Sarama, instead of delivering a message, go at once and find out Ravana's plans. Is he going to return me to Lord Rama, or is he going to fight with Him?"

Sarama went to the assembly, and while remaining hidden, she overheard Ravana's conversation with the ministers. After accomplishing her mission, she returned to Sitadevi and told her, "As I was eavesdropping, many elder ministers advised Ravana to return you to your husband. Indeed, they elaborately described Lord Rama's prowess, just to convince him. Ravana's mother, Kaikasi, also spoke to her son, urging him to make peace."

"Ravana remained adamant, however, and I could very well understand that he is only willing to give you up at the time of death. Even as the meeting was going on, Ravana could hear the sound of conch shells, drums and other noises made by the monkeys. Malyavan, Ravana's maternal grandfather, was the next to speak."

Malyavan said, "A wise king never fights with an enemy that possesses superior strength. Therefore, I advise you to return Sitadevi to Lord Rama and establish peaceful relations with Him. Otherwise, Ravana, you can rest assured that virtue has taken the form of the enemy, just to conquer your evil self. Because you persecuted the great rishis, their power of austerity is now directed against you for your destruction. The benedictions you received from Lord Brahma did not give you immunity from death at the hands of human beings or monkeys. Because of this, you should carefully consider what a dangerous position you are now in. My dear grandson, please heed my good advice."

"Many inauspicious signs have become visible, indicating the destruction of Lanka. Threatening clouds are pouring blood. Our horses and elephants have tears in their eyes. Carnivorous animals freely wander in the gardens, crying out ominously. In their dreams, the Raksasas see black women with yellow teeth, plundering their houses and standing before them, laughing.
