Dogs are eating the sacrificial offerings. One species of animal is seen mating with a member of another species. Time personified, appearing in a huge black form with shaved head, is seen peering into all the houses of Lanka every morning and evening."

"O Ravana, I consider Lord Rama to be Lord Visnu Himself, appearing in human form. Therefore, you had better go and surrender to Him, so that you can rid yourself of this calamity."

Sarama continued speaking to Sitadevi, "Ravana could not accept this good advice, however. He angrily replied, 'You are a rascal, for you are taking the enemy's side. You are very eager to glorify Rama's prowess, but what do you think of me? I can understand that you are praising the enemy because you are envious of me, or because you have been won over to His side, or because you are afraid of Him, but I can assure you that very soon you will witness Rama's death at my hands.'"

Malyavan remained silent. After offering proper benedictions to the king, he retired to his quarters. Thereafter, Ravana made arrangements for Lanka's defense by posting Prahasta at the eastern gate, Mahaparsva and Mahodara at the southern gate, Indrajit at the western gate, Suka and Sarana, along with himself, at the northern gate, and Virupaksa in the center of the city. The king then dismissed his ministers and retired to the interior of his palace.

Meanwhile, as Lord Rama and the army of monkeys approached Lanka, they discussed amongst themselves about how they could best besiege the city. Vibhisana said, "Along with my ministers— Anala, Sampati, Panasa and Pramati—I took the form of a bird and surveyed Ravana's military arrangements. My dear Lord Rama, I am confident. Just as Ravana had previously defeated Kuvera by invading Lanka along with 60 Lakh Raksasas, You will also gain victory with the help of these hordes of monkeys."

Lord Rama then gave the following order, "Nila will lead the attack on Prahasta at the eastern gate. Angada will lead the attack against Mahaparsva and Mahodara at the southern gate. Hanuman will lead the assault at the western gate. Laksmana and I will lead the attack on Ravana at the northern gate. Sugriva, Jambavan and Vibhisana will stay in the center of our army to provide assistance wherever needed. I want only seven of us to fight in the form of human beings—Laksmana, Vibhisana, his four ministers, and Me. The others should retain their forms as monkeys, for that will enable us to easily distinguish them from the Raksasas."

The sun was beginning to sink below the horizon and so Lord Rama, Laksmana and the monkey leaders climbed to the peak of Suvela Mountain to spend the night. From the mountaintop they got a splendid view of Lanka, even though darkness had already set in. With its innumerable twinkling lights, the city looked as if it were suspended in the sky, and it could be seen that the Raksasas were prepared to fight.

The next morning, in full daylight, everyone was amazed to see the heavenly city, with its array of flowering gardens filled with celestial trees and singing birds. Lanka was beautifully situated on a leveled peak of Trikuta Mountain, and it was huge. The walled city was ten by twenty yojanas, and in the center stood Ravana's magnificent palace, supported by 1000 pillars. As Lord Rama gazed at the city with great admiration, He happened to spot Ravana perched atop the northern gate. Having a canopy over his head, Ravana was being fanned by his personal servants.

Sugriva had ordered many of the monkeys to go on ahead, jumping from mountain top to mountain top, to occupy Lanka's outer gardens. When Sugriva also spotted Ravana, he impetuously jumped from the peak of Mount Suvela right to where the Raksasa king was sitting. After momentarily gazing at Ravana with great disdain, Sugriva announced, "I am a servant of Lord Rama, and I will kill you this very day!"

Saying this, Sugriva suddenly pounced on Ravana, knocking off his crown. Although startled, Ravana managed to grab Sugriva, and while uttering similar threats, he threw the monkey king to the ground. Sugriva bounced back like a rubber ball, and after grabbing Ravana, threw him down with great force.

A fierce wrestling match took place as the two heroes scratched one another with their nails, so that both became covered with blood and perspiration. After striking one another with their fists and wrestling for a long time, Sugriva and Ravana suddenly fell from the gate into the area between the boundary wall and the moat. Jumping to their feet, the two continued to fight, gradually exhibiting the complete art of wrestling.

Finally, Ravana realized that he would not be able to defeat Sugriva by mere physical strength and so he began to utilize his mystic powers. Understanding this, Sugriva decided to abandon the fight. After bounding into the air, he returned to where Lord Rama was staying.

Sugriva felt very blissful at having performed such a heroic feat, and his followers enthusiastically jumped up and down. Lord Rama went and embraced Sugriva, but at the same time, He chastised him out of love.

Lord Rama said, "You have acted rashly, for you dared to do something without My sanction. Besides this, a king should never take such a risk, because the death of a ruler is a great calamity for the entire nation. Î Sugriva, if Ravana had killed you, I certainly would have killed him in retaliation. Then, after installing Vibhisana upon the throne at Lanka and Bharata upon the throne at Ayodhya, I would have given up My life for having allowed you to be killed in My presence."

Sugriva said, "After seeing that rascal Ravana, the abductor of mother Sitadevi, I could not bear to ignore him."

Lord Rama said, "Never mind. You wonderfully displayed your heroism, and all the monkey soldiers have become inspired by your courageous example."

Turning to Laksmana, Lord Rama said, "By observing various omens, I can understand that there will soon be a great destruction of prominent monkeys, bears and Raksasas. Let us attack Lanka without wasting any more time.”

Lord Rama climbed down Suvela Mountain. After reviewing His troops, He began the march to Lanka, bow in hand, while the monkeys that followed him uprooted trees and picked up boulders. Before long, they arrived at the city's boundary walls. Lord Rama stationed Himself outside the northern gate, Nila took up his position outside the eastern gate, Angada went to the southern gate, and Hanuman approached the western gate. Sugriva placed his army between the northern and western gates, and in this way the monkeys completely surrounded the walled city, awaiting the encounter.

The Raksasa warriors were astonished to see how an apparently unlimited number of monkey soldiers had completely surrounded Lanka, having situated themselves between the moat and boundary walls. With weapons in hand, the leaders fearfully rushed to Ravana's palace to inform the king of how the city was about to be besieged. After receiving this report, Ravana hastily went onto the balcony to survey the situation.

When he saw how the entire earth had become brown due to being covered by multitudes of monkeys, the Raksasa king also became highly astonished. For a long time Ravana stood motionlessly staring at Lord Rama, wondering what he should do next.

Lord Rama ordered Angada to go and deliver a message as His envoy. Leaping high into the air, Angada quickly came before Ravana, who was now sitting atop the northern gate, surrounded by his ministers.

While staying somewhat apart, he announced, "My name is Angada, the nephew of King Sugriva and successor to the throne at Kiskindha. I have come here as an envoy to deliver the following message spoken by Lord Rama, 'O King of the Raksasas, now that all your pious credits have become exhausted, I am going to kill you in retaliation for the atrocities you committed against the rishis. If you do not voluntarily surrender to Me at once, I will rid the entire earth of Raksasas. Either submit to Me, or else come before Me so that I can purify you with the onslaught of My deadly arrows. If you are unwilling to return Sitadevi and bow before Me, then I advise you to take a good look at Lanka, for it will be your last.'"

Ravana's rage flared up when he heard this. He ordered his ministers to capture Angada and kill him. When four Raksasas came to seize him, Angada allowed them to do so, for he wanted to display his superior prowess. Suddenly, Angada jumped onto the top of Ravana's palace, carrying with him the four Raksasas that clutched his arms. Due to the force of his leap, however, the Raksasas lost their grip and fell to the ground, within the sight of Ravana. Angada proceeded to kick the palace roof so that it began to crumble while Ravana stood, gazing helplessly.

Having thus vexed the Raksasa king, Angada let out a loud roar and returned to where Lord Rama was standing. Having witnessed Angada's prowess, Ravana began to foresee his destruction and thus he sighed heavily, again and again.

At this time, Lord Rama's mind once again turned to thoughts of poor Sitadevi, and so He ordered His army, "Make short work of the Raksasas!”

Hearing this, the monkeys responded, "All victory to Lord Rama and Laksmana!"

Lanka resounded with the vibration. The monkey warriors started scaling the defense walls after breaking them with their trees and stones. Seeing this, Ravana ordered his troops to advance quickly, so that amidst a terrible roaring sound, the fierce conflict commenced.

The Raksasas struck the monkeys with their clubs and other weapons, while the monkeys countered by using trees and stones, as well as their claws and teeth. Stationed on top of the defense wall, the Raksasas pierced many monkeys with their weapons. At the same time, numerous monkeys jumped up and forcibly dragged the Raksasas down to the ground, so that the earth soon became a muddy mixture containing flesh and blood.

As Hanuman fought with Jambumali, Angada with Indrajit, Nila with Nikumbha, and Sugriva with Praghasa, the bodies of the slain warriors were carried away by the rivers of blood created by the massacre.

Indrajit struck Angada with his mace, but the prince deftly grabbed it out of his hands and smashed it against his chariot. Jambumali pierced Hanuman with a spear, but the son of Vayu jumped onto the Raksasa's chariot and killed him with a slap of the hand. Lord Rama, Sugriva and Nala killed numerous Raksasas, and thus the heroic army of monkeys inflicted heavy losses upon the enemy. In that fierce battle, numerous headless trunks of both Raksasas and monkeys were seen darting here and there, adding to the ghastliness of the scene.

Finally, night fell. In the darkness, the monkeys questioned, "Are you a monkey?" When someone was found to be an enemy, he was attacked with great antagonism. Although the Raksasas were easily visible because of their golden armor, it was their nature to become more enlivened at night. Both sides suffered heavy losses.

Hordes of Raksasas attacked Lord Rama in the darkness, and their loud roaring created a tumultuous noise. Keeping the giant monkey Golangula on one side and Jambavan's brother Dhumra on the other, Lord Rama lit up the night sky with His golden arrows. At this time Angada managed to smash Indrajit's chariot and kill his driver. Being injured, Indrajit abandoned his disabled chariot and vanished from view as the demigods and celestial rishis watched from their positions in the sky.

While remaining invisible, enraged Indrajit proceeded to shower arrows in the form of serpents. Due to a benediction Indrajit had received from Lord Brahma, these snake-arrows were able to bind Lord Rama and Laksmana so tightly that They were hardly able to move. Lord Rama dispatched ten of the foremost monkeys, including Hanuman, Nila and Angada, to search for Indrajit, but as the monkeys sprang into the air, exploring all directions, invisible Indrajit pierced them with innumerable arrows. Simultaneously, he showered arrows upon Lord Rama and Laksmana until blood flowed profusely from Their wounds, covering Their entire bodies.

Indrajit announced, "O Rama and Laksmana, listen to me. Even Indra cannot see me, and so what to speak of others. Now it will be my great pleasure to dispatch You both to the abode of Yamaraja with my unceasing torrents of arrows."

As Indrajit continued to pierce Lord Rama and Laksmana, there finally remained no space to place a finger between the arrows that completely covered Their bodies. At last, when Lord Rama's bow dropped from His hand as He fell to the ground, Laksmana gave up all hope of survival and fainted. Seeing this pitiful sight, all the monkeys became exceedingly despondent and tears came to their eyes.

As the leaders of the monkeys came and surrounded Lord Rama and Laksmana, Indrajit ecstatically announced to his followers, "The two mighty warriors have fallen victim to my magical snake-arrows imbued with the power of Brahma. Not even Indra and all the demigods could save Lord Rama and Laksmana now."

The Raksasas enthusiastically responded by shouting," Rama is dead! Indrajit has single-handedly conquered the enemy!"

Convinced that Lord Rama and Laksmana were dead, Indrajit joyfully departed for Ravana's palace, intoxicated by his victory. Sugriva and Vibhisana came to where Lord Rama and Laksmana were lying motionless, soaked with blood and breathing only slightly. Sugriva was especially pained to see how the two brothers were completely enmeshed in the serpentine arrows, and so Vibhisana took special care to comfort him.

While wiping the tears from Sugriva's eyes, Vibhisana said, "O King, I know that Lord Rama and Laksmana can be revived. Please rest assured that They will not die. Guard Lord Rama carefully while I rally our army and restore the monkeys to confidence.”

Meanwhile, as all the foremost monkeys guarded Lord Rama and Laksmana, Indrajit went to Ravana and reported the news of his victory with great pride. When Ravana heard that Lord Rama and Laksmana had been killed, he jumped to his feet and lovingly embraced his son, saying, "My dear boy, how did you accomplish this most difficult feat? Tell me everything!"

Indrajit replied, "While remaining invisible in the sky, I bound Rama and Laksmana with my serpentine arrows. Then I deeply pierced Them until They lay lifeless upon the ground. All the monkey warriors have given up fighting out of hopelessness."

Thinking that Lord Rama and Laksmana were dead, Ravana gave up all fear and anxiety. After dismissing Indrajit, he called for the Raksasis guarding Sitadevi. Confident that she would now submit to him, Ravana jubilantly said, "Rama and Laksmana have been slain by my incomparable son, Indrajit. I want you to immediately go and convey this news to Sita. Then, take her in the Puspaka chariot so that she can see for herself how her husband is lying dead upon the battlefield."

After dismissing the Raksasis, Ravana had the news of Lord Rama and Laksmana's deaths broadcast all over Lanka for the pleasure of the citizens. When the Raksasi guards informed Sitadevi of her husband's defeat, she felt a terrible shock and fell to the ground while sobbing hysterically.

The Raksasis helped Sitadevi onto the Puspaka chariot, along with their leader, Trijata. Within a moment, they rose high into the sky. While flying over the battlefield, Sitadevi could see a large number of slain monkeys and Raksasas. Then, she spotted Lord Rama and Laksmana, lying upon the ground and surrounded by many grieving monkeys. Sitadevi could hear the Raksasas rejoicing as she gazed upon Lord Rama's arrow-riddled body and cast-aside bow.

Being unable to contain her grief, Sitadevi began to wail aloud while lamenting her fate, "Formerly, learned brahmanas who were masters of astrology and palmistry had assured me, saying, "O fortunate princess, you will bear sons, and you will never have to suffer as a widow. In the future you will become the queen of an \| illustrious king who will perform many great sacrifices. Now that my Lord is dead, I can understand that these brahmanas were all liars and cheaters."

"The brahmanas used to assure me of my good fortune by describing my auspicious bodily features. They said, 'O princess, you have fine dark hair and your eyebrows are curved and not joined. Your closely set teeth are even, and your rosy fingers and toes have no space between them. Your thighs are rounded, shapely and hairless, and your ample breasts touch one another. Your navel is deep, your skin is very soft, and your complexion is fair and brilliant. All your toes touch the ground as you walk, and on the soles of your feet are the auspicious marks of lotus flowers, indicating an exalted royal birth. Combined with your lotus-petal eyes and gentle smile, all these symptoms indicate that the highest good fortune awaits you.’ Oh, of what use are these auspicious marks now that my husband is dead? Without my Lord, my good fortune has come to an end!"

Trijata approached Sitadevi and said, "O Princess, you can rest assured that Lord Rama and Laksmana are not dead, for the Puspaka chariot will never carry a widow. Look at the monkeys. They are not in a state of confusion. They are guarding Lord Rama and Laksmana with great care. What would be the necessity of this if Lord Rama and Laksmana were dead? Even from here I can see the luster in Lord Rama and Laksmana's faces, which departs at the time of death. My dear Sitadevi, do not grieve unnecessarily, for it is certain that your husband is still alive."

Sitadevi became a little encouraged by Trisata's words. She was then brought back to the asoka grove. By meditating upon the piteous condition of her husband, though, she once again fell into the pit of hopelessness and despair.

Meanwhile, Lord Rama regained consciousness. Seeing Laksmana by His side, soaked with blood and without any sign of life, Lord Rama exclaimed with great anguish, "Even if I succeed in recovering Sita, the whole endeavor will be rendered useless if Laksmana dies! I could always find another consort to replace the daughter of King Janaka, but I could never find such a helpful friend like Laksmana! If Laksmana dies, then I will also give up My life, for I cannot bear the thought of returning to Ayodhya without Him. How could I dare face His mother after letting Him die on the battlefield in My presence?"

Turning to Sugriva, Lord Rama said, "The monkey warriors should now retreat, for without Laksmana and I to protect them, they will be highly vulnerable. Noble king of the vanaras, I want to express My unlimited gratitude to all of you for your sincere friendship, and the wonderful valor you have displayed while risking your lives in My service."

Tears came to the eyes of all the monkeys who heard Lord Rama's sweet and pathetic words. Vibhisana then returned after having restored the army to confidence. When Vibhisana saw Lord Rama and Laksmana lying upon Their beds of arrows, he broke down and cried. Sugriva embraced him and said, "You should not doubt that Lord Rama and Laksmana will soon recover and go on to defeat Ravana in battle."

Sugriva said to his father-in-law, Susena, "You should arrange for Lord Rama and Laksmana to return to Kiskindha so that they can safely recover from their wounds. Let all the monkeys accompany them. I will remain here alone. After single-handedly defeating Ravana, I will return to Kiskindha with mother Sitadevi."

Susena said, "Long, long ago, when there was a war between the demigods and Daityas, the demons were able to kill many of the celestials by means of their mystic illusions. Brhaspati was able to revive the slain demigods by employing mantras and special medicinal herbs."

"I suggest that Panasa and Sampati go to the ocean of milk, for they are familiar with these herbs, which are called sanjivakarani and visalya. Created by Lord Brahma, the sanjivakarani herb brings a practically dead man back to life, and the herb called visalya instantly cures all wounds created by arrows. These herbs can be found on the Candra and Drona Mountains, which had risen up in the middle of the milk-ocean as it was being churned to produce nectar. Perhaps Hanuman should go there, since he can make the journey in the shortest time."

As Susena was speaking, a fierce wind began to blow, bringing with it dense clouds and streaks of lightning. The sea became agitated with high waves, and the mountains appeared to tremble. As tall trees were being knocked down and blown into the ocean, a gigantic bird-like creature suddenly appeared. Upon his arrival, all the snake-arrows binding Lord Rama and Laksmana instantly fled. This creature came and wiped Lord Rama and Laksmana's faces with his hands. Just by his touch, Their bodies regained Their normal bright, shining complexions and renewed strength. When the mysterious bird raised Him up and embraced Him in great happiness, Lord Rama lovingly said, "We are both eternally indebted to you. If you think that We are worthy, please inform Us of your identity."

The gigantic bird replied, "I am Your eternal servant, Garuda, the son of Vinata. Even all the demigods with Indra at their head could not have released You from the bondage of Indrajit's snake- arrows. These snakes are the sons of Kadru, and were converted into arrows by Indrajit's mystic power that he received from Lord Brahma."

"My dear Lord, You should be careful while fighting with the Raksasas, for they are very tricky, whereas heroes like Yourself are straightforward. I now wish to depart, but before doing so, let me assure You that You will come out victorious and get back Your beloved Sitadevi."

Garuda circumambulated Lord Rama, and after embracing Him once again, he soared into the sky. When they saw that Lord Rama and Laksmana had completely recovered from Their wounds, all the monkeys jumped for joy. With great ecstasy they beat upon drums, lashed their tails about, and roared like lions, creating a great tumult. After once again picking up trees and huge boulders, the monkeys stood ready to fight.

When Ravana heard the joyful uproar of the monkeys, he could guess that Lord Rama and Laksmana had recovered. Just to make sure, he ordered some Raksasas to go and ascertain the reason for the monkeys' bravado. After climbing onto the defense wall, the Raksasas saw that Lord Rama and Laksmana were alive and well, and so they fearfully rushed back to break the news to Ravana. His doubts having been confirmed, Ravana became very anxious, so that his majestic face turned pale.

Becoming enraged, the king ordered the great warrior, Dhumraksa, to go and attack Lord Rama. After hurriedly mobilizing his army, Dhumraksa mounted his chariot, which was drawn by donkeys. While exhibiting his great pride by laughing robustly, he headed for the western gate, where Hanuman was stationed.

As Dhumraksa rode through the streets of Lanka, a big vulture came and sat on his chariot, and other carnivorous birds perched on his flagpole. Suddenly, someone threw a headless trunk in his path, and the clouds began pouring rain mixed with blood. Dhumraksa became fearful by seeing these ominous signs, but still he courageously approached the monkey soldiers, who were eager for combat.

A fierce battle began. The monkeys smashed the Raksasas with their huge rocks and trees, and tore them to shreds with their sharp teeth and nails. Because of the monkeys' superior prowess, the Raksasa army began to panic and run away from the battlefield.

Seeing this, Dhumraksa flared up with rage and began afflicting the monkeys so severely that they also began to flee here and there. This angered Hanuman. Picking up a huge rock, he hurled it violently against Dhumraksa's chariot, smashing it to pieces. The Raksasa saved himself by jumping to the ground, and Hanuman continued to go on a rampage. Picking up a huge slab of stone, Hanuman once again rushed at Dhumraksa. The Raksasa advanced to meet him and deftly smashed him on the head with his club. Hanuman hardly minded the blow, however, and when he hurled the rock upon Dhumraksa's head, the Raksasa hero fell down dead with all his limbs shattered.

Dhumraksa's army fearfully fled to the shelter of Lanka, and Ravana next sent Vajradamstra to fight. This Raksasa hero led his army to the southern gate, where Angada was stationed, but while going, he saw showers of meteors streaking across the sky and ferocious she-jackals belching fire. Still, Vajradamstra took courage and so a heated battle commenced between the monkeys and Raksasas.

When Angada hurled a huge boulder, smashing his chariot to pieces, Vajradamstra jumped to safety. Angada quickly picked up another huge rock and smashed it on Vajradamstra's head, causing him to fall to the ground unconscious, clasping his mace to his chest while vomiting blood. Quickly regaining consciousness, Vajradamstra came before Angada and struck him severely in the chest with his club.

The two great warriors fought with their fists, and as they gradually became exhausted, blood began to flow from their mouths. After a brief lull, Angada picked up a tree trunk while Vajradamstra wielded a sword and shield. After fighting for some time, both fell to their knees in exhaustion. Seizing the opportunity, Angada summoned all his strength and, after grabbing the Raksasa's sword, he stood up and sliced off his head.

Upon seeing their general slain, the remnants of the Raksasa army fled to the shelter of Lanka. Ravana next called for Akampana and said, "You are well-versed in the use of all kinds of weapons, and your eagerness for battle is second to none. Go now and exterminate the army of monkeys. Do away with Lord Rama and Laksmana once and for all!"

While going, Akampana's left eye began to twitch convulsively, his voice became choked, and his horses appeared to be depressed. Without minding these inauspicious signs, Akampana entered the battlefield. In the gruesome encounter that followed, the dust raised by the opposing armies became so thick that no one could tell who was their friend and who was their foe. Thus, monkeys began killing monkeys while Raksasas killed other Raksasas.

When Hanuman approached him to fight, Akampana sent forth torrents of arrows. Hanuman simply laughed, however, and tore off a huge mass of rocks from a mountain peak. As he rushed toward his foe, whirling the rocks around with one hand and roaring loudly, Akampana easily tore the crag to pieces with his arrows. At this, Hanuman flared up with rage. After uprooting a huge asvattha tree, he once again rushed impetuously at the Raksasa.

When they saw Hanuman in this fierce aspect, smashing all the chariots and trees that stood in his path, the Raksasa soldiers ran from the battlefield. Akampana stood fast, though, and as Hanuman approached, he pierced him deeply with fourteen arrows. Still, Hanuman did not waver. Coming right up to Akampana, he smashed the rocks on the Raksasa's head, making him fall down dead to the ground.

As the leaderless Raksasas retreated to the safety of Lanka, Lord Rama, Laksmana and the monkey warriors came and surrounded Hanuman, showering upon him their heartfelt congratulations.

It was before noon when Ravana heard about Akampana's death, and the news visibly disturbed him. The Raksasa king called for his ministers and took a tour of Lanka to inspect the city's fortifications. Turning to his minister-in-chief, Ravana said, "My dear Prahasta, only you, me, Kumbhakarna, Indrajit and Nikumbha are capable of defeating the monkeys. We are very hard pressed and so I want you to be the next to attack the enemy."

Prahasta replied, "O King, previously I had advised you to return Sita to Rama, but now that war has been declared, I want you to know that I am prepared to lay down my life for your sake."

Prahasta eagerly mobilized his army. Within an hour he mounted his chariot and departed. While going, Prahasta saw carnivorous birds circling counter-clockwise overhead. Meteors streaked across the sky. Female jackals howled ominously. A vulture came and perched atop his flagstaff as cold piercing winds blew and dark clouds showered rain mixed with blood. The whip repeatedly slipped from his chariot driver's hands, and the horses stumbled, even though the path was level. Prahasta's bodily luster faded, and yet, as the monkeys and Raksasas roared challengingly at each other, he confidently advanced toward Sugriva's army, like a moth rushing into a fire.

In the battle that followed, Prahasta slaughtered the monkeys in great numbers with his torrents of arrows. When Nila came to challenge him, Prahasta's arrows pierced right through his body and entered the earth. Still, Nila was undisturbed. As Prahasta rushed toward him, Nila uprooted a huge tree and managed to kill his horses and break his bow. Picking up a club, Prahasta jumped from his disabled chariot and the two continued fighting hand to hand.

Prahasta struck Nila violently with his club, so that blood streamed from the wound. Without wavering, Nila countered by striking Prahasta in the chest with a tree. Not minding the blow, Prahasta rushed at NiIa as he was hurriedly picking up a boulder. When Prahasta came near, NiIa smashed the rock on his head. As the boulder shattered to pieces, Ravana's commander-in-chief fell to the ground, mortally wounded. The Raksasa army panicked, retreating to Lanka, while Laksmana came to congratulate NiIa.

When Ravana learned about Prahasta's death, his heart became afflicted with an unbearable torment. Addressing his ministers, the Raksasa king said, "I can no longer remain disdainful of my powerful enemies. Therefore, I shall now personally enter the battlefield."

Ravana mounted his splendid chariot and, surrounded by his army, came out of the city to fight. When Lord Rama saw the huge Raksasa army approaching, He questioned Vibhisana about it. Then, when Lord Rama caught sight of Ravana, He exclaimed, "Due to his extraordinary effulgence and prowess, it is difficult for Me to look at the Raksasa king! Still, it is certainly My good fortune that he has come within view so that I can at last vent the anger I have restrained for so long."

Ravana had kept a large army inside Lanka, warning them to be on the alert in case the monkeys took advantage of his absence. As he came out from the city, Ravana divided the monkeys into halves, while Lord Rama and Laksmana took up Their bows in anticipation. The battle began when Sugriva picked up a mass of rocks, and after suddenly darting forward, hurled it impetuously at Ravana. The Raksasa king effortlessly smashed the rocks to pieces with his arrows and then released another shaft that deeply pierced Sugriva. When Sugriva fell to the ground, moaning in pain, the Raksasas jumped up and down while shouting jubilantly.

Six more monkey chiefs picked up boulders and rushed at Ravana, but again he easily tore the rocks to pieces. Ravana proceeded to wound these heroic monkeys, while at the same time slaughtering numerous others with his showers of arrows. Finally, being very hard-pressed, the monkeys took shelter of Lord Rama.

When Lord Rama proceeded toward Ravana, Laksmana came and implored Him for permission to fight first, single-handedly. Lord Rama gave His consent, and as Laksmana approached Ravana for battle, Hanuman assisted Him by hurling rocks to foil the Raksasa king's arrows. After some time, however, being unable to contain himself, Hanuman suddenly darted toward Ravana.

With his right arm raised above his head, Hanuman challenged, "The benediction you received from Lord Brahma does not grant you immunity to death at the hands of monkeys. I will now strike you dead with a single blow of my mighty fist!"

Ravana said, "I invite you to hit me once as you like. Then I will strike you dead!"

Hanuman said, "Why are you speaking so foolishly? Don't you remember how easily I killed your son Aksa?"

At this, Ravana suddenly darted forward and slapped Hanuman on the chest, making him stagger backward. When Hanuman recovered his balance, he angrily retaliated by violently smacking Ravana with the palm of his hand. Ravana was visibly shaken, and so the demigods and rishis watching from the sky applauded Hanuman. When Ravana recovered himself, he also praised Hanuman as being a worthy adversary.

Still, Hanuman said, "I can now understand that my prowess is very insignificant, for you are still alive and well. Now, if you still consider me a worthy opponent, hit me once more."

The enraged Ravana pounded Hanuman in the chest with his clenched fist, making him reel backwards once again. Then, leaving Hanuman aside, Ravana turned his attention toward Nila and began piercing him with a steady stream of arrows. Although greatly harassed, Nila picked up a boulder and hurled it at Ravana. As the king of the Raksasas was engaged in breaking it to pieces, Hanuman recovered, but restrained himself, for he was unwilling to attack someone engaged in combat with another.

As Nila tore up one tall tree after another, Ravana cut them to pieces with his arrows while oppressing other monkey soldiers at the same time. Then, to avoid Ravana's arrows, Nila shrank to a tiny size and jumped onto his flagpole. The Raksasa king flared up in anger upon seeing this trick. As Nila proceeded to dart from Ravana's bow to the flagpole and back again, Lord Rama, Laksmana and Hanuman were astonished to see this. Ravana also appreciated the daring feat, but by invoking a powerful fire weapon he was able to strike Nila in the chest, making him fall to his knees.

With Nila out of the way, Ravana next approached Laksmana. After an exchange of harsh words, Ravana released seven arrows that Laksmana easily tore to pieces. Becoming excited, Ravana released showers of arrows, but Laksmana continued to neutralize them all.

Laksmana then took the offensive by shooting arrows at the astonished Ravana, but the Raksasa king was also able to cut them to pieces. Finally, Ravana released a brahmastra that pierced Laksmana in the forehead, making Him fall to the ground. It was with great difficulty that Laksmana recovered, and after picking up His bow, He renewed His attack on Ravana. Laksmana was able to break Ravana's bow. He then pierced him with three powerful arrows, so that Ravana fainted to the ground, his body soaked in blood. It was with a great deal of effort that he regained his composure and raised himself up once again.

Desiring to put an end to his adversary, Ravana picked up a lance that had been given to him by Lord Brahma and violently hurled it. Although Laksmana tried his best to counteract it with his weapons, that powerful lance pierced his chest, causing him to fall onto the ground in great pain. As Laksmana was losing consciousness, Ravana came to arrest Him, but as the Raksasa king was about to grab Him, the son of Sumitra remembered that He is a direct expansion of Lord Visnu and as a result He began exhibiting His unlimited prowess. Because of this, even though Ravana was capable of lifting a mountain, he could not pick up Laksmana even by exerting all his strength.

As Ravana was getting back on his chariot, Hanuman suddenly darted forward and struck him violently on the chest with his fist. While blood flowed from his ten mouths, twenty ears and twenty eyes, Ravana fell unconscious onto the floor of his chariot, causing all the monkeys to shout with joy. Hanuman then went to where Laksmana lay injured. Out of affection, Laksmana made Himself very light so that Hanuman could carry Him to where Lord Rama was staying. At that time, the lance came out from Laksmana's body, its mission accomplished, and returned to Ravana's chariot.

Meanwhile, Ravana regained consciousness. He once again picked up his bow at the time when Laksmana got up, His wounds entirely healed by the inconceivable potency of Lord Visnu. Because Ravana had struck down so many monkeys, Lord Rama decided to personally approach him for combat. At Hanuman's request, Lord Rama mounted upon his shoulders and proceeded with great haste while challenging Ravana to fight.

Feeling great enmity toward Lord Rama, Ravana first showered his arrows upon Hanuman. Hanuman remained undeterred, and so Lord Rama was able to come right up to Ravana’s chariot and smash it to pieces. Then, shooting an effulgent arrow, Lord Rama deeply pierced Ravana in the chest, making him reel dizzily and drop his bow.

After cutting off Ravana's flag, Lord Rama declared, "You have performed a heroic feat by killing innumerable monkeys on the battlefield, but because of this, you must be exhausted and so I will refrain from killing you. You may return to Lanka and rest. Later on, I will fight with you once more.”

His defeat and Lord Rama's words crushed Ravana's pride. As he returned to Lanka with his head hanging down, Lord Rama and Laksmana went and extracted the arrows from the wounded monkeys' bodies. Having witnessed Ravana's humiliation, the demigods and rishis stationed in the sky rejoiced, feeling confident that their mission would soon be accomplished.

Ravana sat down despondently upon his throne. After summoning his ministers, he explained, "Long ago, when I had received benedictions from Lord Brahma, he warned me to beware of human beings. Then once, later on, a king in the line of Iksvaku, named Anaranya, cursed me by saying, 'In the future, someone will appear in my dynasty who will kill you, along with all your relatives.”

"Later on, Vedavati cursed me after I raped her, and in fact I believe that she has become the daughter of King Janaka, just to bring about my destruction. Another time, when I lifted Mount Kailasa, Uma became frightened and so cursed me by saying,"Wicked Raksasa! A woman will one day become the cause of your death!" After that, I once laughed at Nandisvara, because of his monkey-like facial features. He angrily cursed me, saying, "Your entire dynasty will be destroyed by an army of monkeys!" Rambha, Punjikasthali, Nalakuvara and Lord Brahma all cursed me for raping innocent women. Now I can see that the seeds of my past sins are finally bearing fruit.”

"Quickly, go and awaken Kumbhakarna, for there is no one who can excel him on the battlefield. He fell asleep nine days ago, due to the curse of Lord Brahma, and he normally sleeps for six months without interruption. What is the use of his unparalleled prowess if he does not come to help me now?"

The ministers went with a large crew to Kumbhakarna's residence, but they were perplexed as to what method they could employ to awaken him before his natural time. Taking with them enormous quantities of food, as well as other articles, such as perfumes and garlands, the Raksasas entered Kumbhakarna's cave-like subterranean abode, which measured one yojana in length and breadth.

As soon as they opened the door to Kumbhakarna's room, the Raksasas were blown back by the hurricane-strength winds exhaled from his nostrils. At last, after entering the room with great difficulty, they began their attempts to awaken the sleeping giant.

First, mountains of meat and vats of blood were placed before sleeping Kumbhakarna. Then, the Raksasas smeared sandalwood paste and perfumes all over his body, which was entirely covered with bristly hair. Even though the Raksasas loudly praised his glories, Kumbhakarna continued to sleep soundly. Next, the Raksasas tried roaring loudly, blowing conch shells and beating drums next to Kumbhakarna's ears. They clapped their hands and stomped their feet. They screamed, they wailed, and they shouted. They clanged gongs, crashed cymbals and pushed and pulled his arms and legs, and yet, despite all this, Kumbhakarna did not stir.

Next, the Raksasas picked up big hammers and clubs and beat Kumbhakarna's body, while others pounded him repeatedly with their fists. The problem was that it was very difficult to stand before Kumbhakarna when he exhaled, because of the fierce wind blowing from his nostrils. As a consequence, when Kumbhakarna breathed in and out, the 10,000 Raksasas surrounding him appeared to move back and forth like waves of the ocean.

Next, strong elephants, horses, camels and donkeys were prodded to walk over Kumbhakarna's body, while some of the Raksasas beat his limbs with big logs. Still, despite all this, colossal Kumbhakarna was not aroused from his sleep. Finally, the Raksasas became angry and frustrated and so they began pulling Kumbhakarna's hair and bite his ears. They even tried pouring buckets of water in his ears, but still he did not stir. The Raksasas then beat Kumbhakarna with clubs studded with nails, while making 1000 elephants walk over his body. Only then did the giant Raksasa awaken, for it felt to him as if someone had gently touched him.

Famished and still drowsy, Kumbhakarna stretched his arms and yawned. Although some of the Raksasas were still throwing boulders on him, he could not even feel this. Yawning again and again, Kumbhakarna got up. Indeed, he looked like Kala awakening for the destruction of all beings. Kumbhakarna greedily ate all the meat and drank all the blood that had been put in front of him. When the Raksasas saw that he was satiated, they came before him with folded hands.

With eyes still clouded by sleep, Kumbhakarna glanced at them and angrily inquired, "Why did you wake me untimely? I am sure that some great danger has overtaken you that only I can counteract. Otherwise, no one would dare come here and disturb me."

A minister named Yupaksa replied, "Lanka has been besieged by an army of huge monkeys, led by Rama and Laksmana. Many Raksasa heroes have already been killed, and when Ravana went to fight, he was released after being defeated by Rama."

Kumbhakarna said, "I will go at once and drink Rama and Laksmana's blood! After having thus eliminated the cause of his fears, I will go and present myself before Ravana."

Mahodara suggested, "You had better see Ravana first to receive his orders. After all, he is your elder brother, and the king as well.”

Some of the Raksasas went and reported to Ravana, "After great endeavor, we have succeeded in awakening Kumbhakarna. Would you like to see him first, or should he directly go and fight with Rama?"

Ravana replied, "Let Kumbhakarna come here first, so that I can properly honor him. Just make sure you supply him with enough meat and wine so that he comes here in a good mood."

Kumbhakarna got up and washed his face. As Ravana instructed, the Raksasas brought in mountains of food, along with two hundred buckets of wine. After guzzling all this, Kumbhakarna's energy became replenished.

Thereafter, when the colossal Raksasa came out of his subterranean cavern to meet his elder brother, the monkeys were astonished to see him. Some immediately fell to the ground out of extreme fright. Others, who were more courageous, took shelter of Lord Rama, while those who were not so courageous ran away in all directions.

Kumbhakarna had expanded his already gigantic size, just to make the enemy afraid. When Lord Rama saw Kumbhakarna, He exclaimed, "Never in My life have I seen such a gigantic living entity! Î Vibhisana, who is this monster that towers above us like a second Mount Meru?"

Vibhisana replied, "This is the son of the sage, Visrava, named Kumbhakarna. He is the biggest of all the Raksasas, and he has defeated even the lord of death, Yamaraja."

Vibhisana informed Lord Rama of Kumbhakarna's history as follows, "As soon as he was born, Kumbhakarna began hungrily devouring thousands of living creatures. Because of this, the created beings took shelter of Indra. The king of heaven became very angry when he learned what was happening. Thereafter, when Indra attacked Kumbhakarna with his thunderbolt, the son of Visrava was knocked unconscious. After quickly recovering, Kumbhakarna tore out one of Airavata's tusks and smashed it against Indra's chest, hurting him severely. The depressed king of heaven took all the created beings and went to see Lord Brahma."

After offering obeisances, they complained, "The son of Visrava, named Kumbhakarna, is tormenting the rishis and kidnapping many men’s wives. Besides this, he is devouring us at such a rate that we will soon become extinct!"

Lord Brahma absorbed his mind in meditation. When, by his mystic vision, he saw Kumbhakarna, Lord Brahma became greatly alarmed at the sight. After pondering over the situation, he went to Kumbhakarna and said, "You have been created for the destruction of the world, but now is the time for universal maintenance. Therefore, for the welfare of all living beings, you shall remain continually buried in deep sleep from this day on."

Kumbhakarna immediately became overpowered by Lord Brahma's curse, and so he fell down right there, in the presence of his elder brother. Ravana pleaded, "O Grandsire, you have cut down a tree that was just about to bear fruit. O supreme teacher within the universe, it is not proper for you to curse your great-grandson like this. Of course, I know that your words cannot be futile, but you should at least allow Kumbhakarna some fixed time for sleeping, and for awakening as well."

Lord Brahma replied, "I shall grant your wish. Kumbhakarna shall sleep continuously for six months, and then awaken for one day. At that time he can wander over the earth and devour whatever he likes before once again being forced into another prolonged slumber."

Vibhisana concluded his narration by saying, "Ravana has awakened Kumbhakarna prematurely, being hard-pressed in battle. Just by seeing this monstrous Raksasa, our monkey soldiers have been thrown into a state of confusion. I suggest that we tell the monkeys that Kumbhakarna is a tall mechanical device. In this way, they will give up their fear."

As Kumbhakarna entered Ravana's palace, Lord Rama ordered NiIa to rally the monkeys and attack the city gates. Fierce fighting was thus going on as Kumbhakarna, along with thousand of his followers, came before Ravana, who was seated upon the Puspaka chariot in a disturbed state of mind. Ravana became enlivened upon seeing his brother, however. After seating Kumbhakarna by his side, Ravana warmly embraced him.

Kumbhakarna asked, "My dear brother, what can I do for you?"

Ravana replied, "As you know, Rama has attacked Lanka with a huge army of monkeys, and many of the foremost Raksasas have been killed. My dear brother, I am depending upon you to kill Rama and Laksmana, for I can see no one else who is capable of doing so."

Kumbhakarna heartily laughed and said, "What was predicted by Vibhisana and other intelligent ministers has now come to pass. Î Ravana, you are a rascal for ignoring the good advice of your sincere well-wishers. Vibhisana and Mandodari were correct when they advised you to return Sita to Rama."

"O King, it is still not too late for you to rectify your mistakes, although the decision is certainly yours alone."

Ravana became angry while hearing this. Restraining his rage, he said, "It is futile for you to talk like this now that the battle is in full swing. My dear brother, even if I did make a mistake by kidnapping Sita, I request you to nullify the error by manifesting your unparalleled prowess."

To pacify his brother, Kumbhakarna sweetly said, "O King, do not worry. I promise that I will kill Rama and Laksmana, as well as all the monkeys, headed by Sugriva and Hanuman. These so-called heroes have played havoc with our army only because I was absent from the battlefield. Now you will see how insignificant the enemy is in the face of my supreme might!"

Desiring to please Ravana, Mahodara interrupted Kumbhakarna, saying, "You are arrogant and foolish for daring to criticize your elder brother. He is the king, and so he can do whatever he likes. There was nothing wrong in Ravana's kidnapping Sitadevi, because kings are naturally inclined to exhibit their prowess by performing such acts. Proud giant, let me assure you that you will not be able to defeat Rama, simply by your strength, as you are claiming."

Turning to Ravana, Mahodara said, "O King, I have devised a nice plan for winning Sita. Let Kumbhakarna and I, along with other Raksasa heroes, attack Rama all at once. If we are able to kill Him, victory will be ours, but if we cannot defeat Rama, we can still come back to Lanka and claim that we have devoured Him. This false story should then be broadcast all over Lanka, to the accompaniment of drums and the rewarding of soldiers and servants. In this way the rumor will soon reach Sita."

"My dear Ravana, while Sita is drowning in an ocean of grief, you should go there. Tempt her with promises of royal opulence and comfort, and try your best to gain her confidence. At that time Sita will feel that she is without any other protector, so I am sure that she will submit to you at last."

"O King, if you fight with Rama, you will surely lay down your life upon the battlefield, but if you take my advice, you can win Sita without a struggle."

Kumbhakarna spoke harshly, "O Mahodara, your words would only appeal to cowardly kings. I have already made up my mind to kill Rama, and my determination cannot possibly go in vain. The rest of you have bungled the war so that Lanka has become divested of much of its population. Now I will rectify the situation, and I don't need anyone else's help."

Ravana heartily laughed and said, "What you say is true. Mahodara must be very afraid of Rama. My dear Kumbhakarna, no one can stand before you when you enter the battlefield in a fit of anger. Please go now and fight, and thus put an end to our anxieties. Do not go alone, though. Surround yourself with a large army so that you will be even more invincible."

Ravana felt rejuvenated after considering the prowess of his colossal brother. As Kumbhakarna picked up a fierce dart and prepared to depart, Ravana came and placed a golden necklace and flower garlands around his neck. After putting on a suit of impenetrable armor, Kumbhakarna circumambulated Ravana and bowed down before him. Ravana embraced Kumbhakarna and offered his blessings in return.

As he approached the battlefield, Kumbhakarna expanded his body to become six hundred bow lengths in height and a hundred bow lengths in width. And yet, even though he was determined to kill Lord Rama and Laksmana, and devour all the monkeys, Kumbhakarna began to see many fearful omens. Meteors streaked across the sky, his left arm throbbed and his left eye twitched. A vulture came and alighted on the spear he carried, but under the sway of destiny, the puffed-up Kumbhakarna did not pay any attention to these inauspicious signs.

When Kumbhakarna entered the battlefield by stepping over the defense wall, all the monkeys panicked and ran away. Angada managed to rally the army, and so they proceeded to shower Kumbhakarna with numerous trees and boulders, but all these missiles simply shattered when they struck the giant Raksasas body. In retaliation, Kumbhakarna created a great slaughter of the monkeys, so that once again they panicked and fled. In the stampede that ensued, some of the monkeys fell from the cliffs into the ocean while keeping their eyes closed out of fear.

Angada tried to encourage the monkeys by saying, "It is better to die gloriously in battle than to flee in the hope of living a life of ease. Such cowards have to pass their lives being ridiculed by their relatives, and such insults are more painful than death."

Still, the monkeys continued to flee, considering their lives to be very dear. Only when Angada assured the monkeys that Lord Rama would kill Kumbhakarna did they gradually turn back toward the battlefield.

Thereafter, when the monkeys attacked Kumbhakarna, he struck down thousands of them each time he wielded his gigantic club. At the same time, the huge Raksasa grabbed as many as thirty monkeys in his arms and stuffed them into his mouth. Dvivida threw a huge boulder at Kumbhakarna, and when it missed the mark and landed upon the Raksasa army, it crushed many soldiers.

From the sky, Hanuman tried to throw huge slabs of stone upon Kumbhakarna, but the colossal Raksasa easily tore them to pieces with his hands. When Kumbhakarna picked up a spear and rushed at the monkeys, Hanuman blocked his path and hurled a great mass of rocks. Kumbhakarna avoided the blow, however, and when he threw the spear in retaliation, it pierced Hanuman in the chest, causing him to vomit blood while crying out in great pain.

While the Raksasas rejoiced at seeing Hanuman's plight, the monkeys scattered in all directions. Rsabha, Sarabha, NiIa, Gavaksa and Gandhamadana then pounced upon Kumbhakarna all at once. Still, the giant Raksasa felt their powerful blows to be no more severe than loving caresses. Kumbhakarna caught hold of Rsabha, and after squeezing him in his hand, threw him unconscious onto the ground. Kumbhakarna then struck the other four monkey heroes, making them fall down wounded.

Seeing this, the other monkeys became infuriated. They attacked Kumbhakarna all at once by the thousands, climbing over his body as if it were a great mountain. While the monkeys bit him with their sharp teeth, Kumbhakarna grabbed them as fast as he could and stuffed them into his gaping mouth.

In this way, as Kumbhakarna ranged over the battlefield, he devoured innumerable monkeys, although some of them managed to escape through his nostrils and ears after having entered his mouth.

As the monkeys took shelter of Lord Rama, Angada suddenly rushed at Kumbhakarna and flung a big rock on his head. This only served to enrage the giant, however, who then rushed at Angada while hurling his spear. Angada dodged the spear, and while doing so he slapped Kumbhakarna on the chest, making him fall to the ground in a daze. Kumbhakarna quickly recovered, and after standing up, he smacked Angada with the back of his hand, making him fall down almost bereft of consciousness.

Kumbhakarna picked up his spear and rushed at Sugriva, who quickly grabbed a boulder to meet his onrushing foe. When Sugriva hurled the rock at Kumbhakarna’s chest, it merely shattered to pieces. Seeing this, the Raksasas shouted with joy, while the monkeys became despondent. While roaring tumultuously, Kumbhakarna violently hurled his spear.

Hanuman had been watching the duel very attentively, and upon seeing Sugriva's plight, he suddenly jumped into the air. After catching the spear, Hanuman broke it in half over his knee. The monkeys roared with delight upon seeing this, while Kumbhakarna began to feel a little disheartened. Still, in great agitation he tore off a mass of rocks and hurled it impetuously at Sugriva. When it struck him, Sugriva fell down unconscious and so Kumbhakarna quickly went and picked him up. After tucking Sugriva under his armpit, the giant began carrying him back to Lanka.

Seeing this, Hanuman thought, "If Sugriva is taken prisoner, our cause is lost. I must expand myself to become as huge as a mountain and kill Kumbhakarna!"

However, the next moment, Hanuman considered, "I am sure that Sugriva will regain consciousness very soon and manage to free himself. If the king has to be rescued by another, he will certainly become disheartened."

Kumbhakarna entered Lanka with Sugriva in his grips, while the citizens rejoiced, showering fried grains and scented water upon the victorious hero. These offerings helped Sugriva regain consciousness. After sizing up the situation, he began to struggle to get free. First, Sugriva tore off Kumbhakarna's earlobes with his sharp nails. Then he bit off the Raksasa's nose while splitting open his sides with his toes. Sharply pained, Kumbhakarna angrily threw Sugriva to the ground and began beating him. Sugriva finally jumped into the air and returned to Lord Rama's side in an instant.

Being famished, enraged Kumbhakarna picked up a huge hammer and once again entered the enemy ranks so that he could eat the monkeys. Taking handfuls of monkeys, and Raksasas as well, he stuffed them into his mouth while streams of blood and fat poured out from the corners. Although the panic-stricken monkeys tried to take shelter of Lord Rama, Kumbhakarna ran here and there, encircling hundreds of them at a time with his outstretched arms.

Laksmana then came to attack Kumbhakarna, showering His arrows. The giant Raksasas easily deflected these and said with disdain, "I want to fight with Rama, and not with His younger brother!"

In reply, Laksmana pointed to Lord Rama and so Kumbhakarna proceeded toward Him. Lord Rama quickly invoked a rudra weapon at the onrushing giant. When that weapon penetrated Kumbhakarna's chest, he staggered, dropping his club and strewing his other weapons here and there. Many monkeys then pounced on Kumbhakarna, hoping to take advantage of his setback, but the Raksasa beat them back with his fists.

After getting up, Kumbhakarna grabbed a mass of rocks and rushed toward Lord Rama once again. Lord Rama quickly tore the rocks to pieces with seven arrows, and the falling debris knocked down more than two hundred monkey soldiers.

Laksmana said to Lord Rama, "Kumbhakarna is so intoxicated because of drinking blood that he is devouring the monkeys and Raksasas without discriminating between them. I think that thousands of monkeys should go and climb all over Kumbhakarna's body. Being weighed down, he can easily be defeated."

Thereafter, innumerable monkeys proceeded to crawl over Kumbhakarna's body. As the Raksasa tried to shake them off while simultaneously rummaging around, looking for something to eat, Lord Rama took the opportunity to attack. With His bowstring stretched back to His ear, Lord Rama challenged, "You worst of Raksasas, stand before Me for a moment so that My arrows can dispatch you to the abode of Yamaraja!"

Looking up, Kumbhakarna was overjoyed to see Lord Rama in front of him. While laughing gleefully, he exclaimed, "What good fortune! Since You have so kindly given me this opportunity, I will devour You at once!"

As Lord Rama sent forth showers of arrows, Kumbhakarna, without being disturbed, picked up a terrible mace, hoping to kill his enemy without further delay. In response, Lord Rama invoked a powerful vayu weapon, severing Kumbhakarna's right arm that was grasping the gigantic club. The colossal Raksasa screamed with agonizing pain. When the arm fell to the ground, it crushed to death an entire division of monkey soldiers. Having become mad with rage, Kumbhakarna tore up a large tree with his left arm and rushed at Lord Rama. The Lord invoked a mighty aindra weapon that severed Kumbhakarna's left arm, and when it fell to the ground, both monkeys and Raksasas were crushed.

Even though he was without arms, Kumbhakarna madly rushed at Lord Rama while roaring hideously. With two arrows, Lord Rama cut off his huge feet. Still, Kumbhakarna opened his terrible mouth and hobbled frantically toward Lord Rama in the hopes of devouring Him. As Kumbhakarna steadily approached, Lord Rama filled his gaping mouth with so many arrows that he began to gasp for breath. Finally, as Kumbhakarna began to topple while losing consciousness because of extreme pain, Lord Rama discharged another aindra weapon. After illuminating all directions as it streaked across the sky, this wonderfully effulgent weapon tore off Kumbhakarna's head.

As the gigantic trunk of Kumbhakarna's body crashed into the sea, crushing to death huge aquatics, the severed head, which shone like the full moon, fell down onto the king's highway in Lanka, demolishing big palaces, as well as sections of the defense wall. From the sky, the demigods shouted ecstatically and all the monkey soldiers glorified Lord Rama's victory as if they had five mouths. Lord Rama felt very blissful for having accomplished such a heroic feat.

When Ravana heard the news of Kumbhakarna's death, of how his body was half-submerged in the sea, and of how his head was blocking the city gate, it was too much for him and so he fainted, slumping down onto his throne.

After regaining consciousness, Ravana moaned, "Without Kumbhakarna, life has no meaning for me. What a fool I was not to have listened to the good advice of my pious brother, Vibhisana!”

One of Ravana's sons, Trisira, said, "My dear father, please do not lament like this. I am immensely powerful, and I have weapons that were given to me by Lord Brahma. My brothers—Devantaka, Narantaka and Atikaya—are also extremely powerful and expert in the conjuring arts. They can fight while flying in the sky. We will all go together and kill Rama, Laksmana and the monkey warriors. Now, please give up your despair!"

Ravana became somewhat enlivened by Trisira's assurances. After decorating his four sons with ornaments and garlands, the king sent them to fight, along with Mahaparsva and Mahodara. As the six Raksasa heroes went out of the city, followed by an enormous army, the soldiers on both sides roared impetuously. Before long, the battlefield became difficult to traverse, because of being littered with dead bodies, severed limbs, smashed chariots, trees and boulders.

When ordered by Sugriva to attack Ravana's son, Narantaka, Angada approached him unarmed, while challenging, "Why are you wasting your time fighting with common monkeys? Throw your spear at my chest if you consider yourself to be a great hero!"

Biting his lip with rage, Narantaka hurled his spear, but when it struck Angada's chest, it broke and fell to the ground. Angada quickly darted forward and smashed his clenched fist on the head of Narantaka's horse. The horse fell down dead with a fractured skull, forcing the infuriated Narantaka to jump to the ground. He then retaliated by violently hitting Angada on the head with his fist. As blood poured from the wound, Angada reeled back dizzily. After quickly regaining his composure, Angada rushed forward and smashed Narantaka with his fist. That forceful blow completely smashed the Raksasa's chest, making him fall down dead onto the battlefield.

Lord Rama was very pleased and astonished by the prince's great prowess, but Mahodara could not tolerate the death of his nephew, and so, along with Devantaka and Trisira, he angrily rushed at Angada. When he saw the three Raksasas approaching, Angada tore up a giant tree and hurled it at Devantaka. Trisira easily cut the tree to pieces with his arrows and so Angada proceeded to throw more trees and boulders, but all to no avail.

Mahodara came up to Angada, riding upon an elephant, and struck him in the chest with his spiked club. Angada was not the least disturbed by the blow, and he retaliated by violently striking the elephant with his hand. When the elephant fell down dead, Angada tore out one of its tusks and hurled it at Devantaka, wounding him severely. Devantaka soon recovered and struck Angada with his club, making him fall down to his knees. As Angada got back on his feet, Trisira struck him in the forehead with three arrows and so NiIa and Hanuman came to his aid.

Devantaka rushed at Hanuman, wielding his club. Hanuman leapt into the air to avoid the blow, and at the same time struck the Raksasa in the head with his clenched fist. With his skull smashed in, Devantaka fell dead onto the battlefield.

Meanwhile, as Trisira released torrents of arrows at NiIa, Mahodara mounted another elephant to continue fighting. As all his limbs became pierced, NiIa momentarily lost consciousness. Then, after recovering, he tore off some rocks from a mountain peak. Springing into the air, NiIa suddenly smashed them on Mahodara's head. Seeing Mahodara fall down dead right before his eyes, Trisira became mad with rage. As Trisira began showering his arrows, Hanuman retaliated, but the three-headed Raksasa tore all his weapons to pieces.

Becoming frustrated, Hanuman jumped onto Trisira's horse and tore at its hide with his sharp nails. Trisira tried to pierce Hanuman with his spear, but the son of Vayu grabbed it out of his hands and broke it in half. Trisira quickly drew his sword and slashed Hanuman across the chest. In spite of being wounded, Hanuman retaliated by slapping Trisira in the chest, making him fall off his horse, dazed. Hanuman jumped from the horse, and after picking up the sword that had fallen from Trisira's hand, he loudly roared.

This was intolerable for Trisira. He quickly jumped up and punched Hanuman in the chest. Hanuman angrily grabbed one of Trisira's necks and then severed all three of his heads, one after another, with his sword.

As the monkeys roared triumphantly, Mahaparsva picked up his iron club while Rsabha came to challenge him. Mahaparsva struck Rsabha in the chest with his club, making him fall to the ground. Rsabha got up, and after rushing at Mahaparsva, struck him severely in the chest with his fist. The Raksasa collapsed to the ground, soaked with his own blood. As Mahaparsva struggled to recover his strength, Rsabha grabbed his iron club.

After a brief exchange of words, Rsabha struck Mahaprarshva in the chest. As blood gushed from his wounds, the Raksasa tried to grab back his weapon, but before he could do so, Rsabha brought down the spiked club on his head, making him fall down to the ground dead, his eyes and teeth completely crushed.

As the soldiers of the Raksasa army fled in fear, gigantic Atikaya launched an attack on the monkeys while riding upon his chariot. When the monkeys saw the colossal Raksasa, they thought that Kumbhakarna had come back to life, and so they fearfully took shelter of Lord Rama.

Lord Rama was also surprised to see the giant, and so He inquired about him from Vibhisana. Vibhisana explained, "This monstrous Raksasa is the son of Ravana and Dhanyamalini, and his name is Atikaya. After performing severe austerities, he received benedictions from Lord Brahma, making him extraordinarily powerful. My dear Lord Rama, in previous battles, Atikaya was able to counteract Indra's thunderbolt and Varuna's nooses. Therefore, You should kill him at once, before he exterminates the entire army of monkeys."

In the battle that followed, Atikaya fought with the monkeys for awhile. Then, leaving them aside, he rushed at Lord Rama while challenging Him with insulting words. This greatly angered Laksmana, and so he rushed at Atikaya.

The gigantic Raksasa was astonished to hear the twanging of Laksmana's bow, but he nonetheless selected an arrow and said, "You are a mere child, and so You had better leave the battlefield while You are still able to do so. Why are You foolishly coming before me, as if You want to give up Your life?"

This made Laksmana's anger flare up more brightly and so He replied with similarly harsh words. Then, as Atikaya placed his arrow upon his bowstring, the demigods appeared overhead, being curious to witness the duel. As the Raksasa proceeded to release his arrows, Laksmana broke them with His own. Laksmana then pierced Atikaya’s forehead with an arrow, making him tremble violently. After recovering, Atikaya praised Laksmana for being a worthy opponent.

As the fighting continued, Atikaya released an especially powerful arrow that pierced Laksmana's chest. Although bleeding profusely, Laksmana took out an arrow and empowered it with a mantra pertaining to the fire-god, Agni. When Laksmana shot that arrow, Atikaya employed a surya weapon to neutralize it. While soaring through the air, these two arrows collided and reduced each other to ashes before falling to the ground. Atikaya then released a tvasta weapon and so Laksmana counteracted it with an aindra weapon. Next, Atikaya discharged a yama weapon and Laksmana countered with a vayu weapon.

Laksmana proceeded to send forth a steady stream of arrows, but they simply bounced off Atikaya's armor and fell to the ground. As Laksmana continued shooting His arrows, Atikaya pierced Him in the chest with a single shaft, making Him fall to the ground, unconscious.

When Laksmana recovered, He continued His assault, killing Atikaya's horses and driver, but despite shooting His most powerful arrows, He could not even slightly wound the son of Ravana. At this time Vayu appeared before Laksmana and said, "This armor, which was given to Atikaya by Lord Brahma, is impenetrable. Because of this, You should use Your brahmastra to vanquish Your enemy."

Laksmana fitted that ultimate weapon onto his bowstring and discharged it. Atikaya shot numerous arrows in an attempt to counteract the brahmastra, but when he saw that it was unimpeded, he hurled spears, clubs and axes. This was also to no avail, and the brahmastra struck off Atikaya's enormous head. As the severed head rolled upon the ground, the dejected Raksasas rushed back to the shelter of Lanka, while the monkeys ran to Laksmana, congratulating Him.

After receiving news of Atikaya's death, Ravana became very aggrieved and depressed. In a mood of hopelessness, he brooded, "Rama and Laksmana are inconceivably powerful! They have already killed the best of my warriors! I can understand that Rama is Lord Narayana Himself, appearing in human form. Although I had previously been informed of this, I had considered such talk to be the ravings of cowards and fanatics. Who will be able to defeat Rama?"

Seeing his father with tears in his eyes and overwhelmed by lamentation, Indrajit spoke up, "As long as I am still alive, there is no reason for you to grieve like this. My dear father, I promise that I will kill Rama and Laksmana this very day!"

After receiving his father's blessings, Indrajit first went to his sacrificial altar. There, he grabbed a goat by the neck and threw it into the sacrificial fire as an offering. At once, the fire flared up brightly, indicating victory for Ravana's pet son.

From out of the flames arose Agni, to personally accept the sacrificial offering. Indrajit chanted mantras that enabled him and his chariot, which was drawn by donkeys, and well as all his other paraphernalia, to remain invisible. After dispatching his army, Indrajit ascended to the sky, and while remaining invisible, he began raining arrows upon the monkey soldiers.

The monkeys tried to retaliate by hurling stones and trees in the direction from which the arrows were coming, but Indrajit tore all these to pieces. He then invoked the brahmastra, causing heaven and earth to tremble, and the countless arrows produced by that supreme weapon pierced all the great monkey warriors, making them fall to the ground. While remaining invisible, Indrajit continued to shower spears, swords and axes upon his enemy.

The bewildered monkeys could only see the shining weapons falling from the sky, but not their assailant. Even Hanuman, Sugriva, Jambavan and NiIa fell to the ground wounded, and Lord Rama and Laksmana also appeared to be eclipsed by Indrajit's weapons.

Undisturbed, although covered with arrows, Lord Rama said, "O Laksmana, I can understand that Indrajit is releasing arrows surcharged with the power of Brahma. As long as this powerful Raksasa remains invisible, no one will be able to defeat him. Therefore, I think it best if We tolerate his attack. When Indrajit sees that We have fallen unconscious, he will surely consider himself to be victorious and return to Lanka to inform Ravana.''

Thus it came to be that Lord Rama and Laksmana were seriously wounded by Indrajit's arrows and fell to the ground unconscious. Seeing that his enemies were vanquished, Indrajit shouted with joy and returned to Lanka, to tell his father the good news.

The monkeys became exceedingly depressed upon seeing Lord Rama and Laksmana's condition, but Vibhisana informed them, "You should not lament like this. Lord Rama and Laksmana have voluntarily put Themselves in this helpless condition just to honor the weapon of Lord Brahma. Very soon, They will once again rise up to vanquish the enemy."

Hanuman offered his respects to the brahmastra and suggested, "The fighting has subsided and night has fallen. Our first business should be to go and restore the remaining monkey soldiers to confidence."

Hanuman and Vibhisana took torches and began wandering over the battlefield. They saw Sugriva, Angada, NiIa, Nala and another 670 million monkeys lying upon the ground, having been struck down by Indrajit's brahmastra. After searching for Jambavan, they found him so severely wounded that he was unable to see. Still, Jambavan inquired, "Is Hanuman still alive?"

Vibhisana asked, "Why have you inquired about Hanuman first, passing over Lord Rama, Laksmana, Sugriva and Angada?"

Jambavan replied, "If Hanuman is alive, the army is still intact, even if it appears to be massacred. On the other hand, if Hanuman is dead, the entire army is destroyed, even if all the soldiers appear to be well."

Hearing this, Hanuman came and clasped Jambavan's feet, saying, "You can rest assured that I am fine!"

Jambavan instructed, "Go quickly to the Himalayas and locate the Rsabha Mountain. Between Rsabha and Kailasa is an effulgent mountain covered with powerful medicinal herbs that illuminate all directions. You must gather four of these herbs and bring them back here. They are— mrta-sanjivani, which can restore even a dead person to life; salya-karani, which is used while extracting weapons, and for healing wounds; savarna-karani, which restores the body to its original luster; and sandhana-karani, which is used for healing fractured bones and joining severed limbs. If you can procure these herbs, all the monkey heroes that lost their lives can be revived.

Hanuman was very happy to receive this command, and he began to swell with increased energy. After going to the peak of Trikuta Mountain, he squatted down in preparation for a tremendous leap. Due to the pressure, the mountain peak crumbled, and the falling trees burst into flames because of the friction. Lanka's palaces began to collapse as the entire city shook. Because of this, all the citizens became very afraid.

First, Hanuman jumped to the Malaya Mountain on the other side of the sea. After further expanding himself, he bowed to the sun-god and leaped into the sky, causing huge rocks and trees to follow in his wake.

While soaring through the air at great speed, Hanuman soon sighted the Himalayan Mountains with their golden peaks. He saw the seat of Lord Brahma, the abode of Hayagriva, the residence of the presiding deity of the brahmastra, as well as the residences of Indra, Yama, Kuvera and Agni. After finding Mount Rsabha and Mount Kailasa, Hanuman located between them the mountain Jambavan had described, and he was astonished to see its blazing effulgence.

Hanuman frantically searched all over the mountain for the required herbs, but since the plants had hidden themselves upon sensing the arrival of an intruder, he could not locate them. At last, becoming frustrated, Hanuman roared ferociously and challenged, "O mountain, if you act in such a way as to impede the service of Lord Rama, I will smash you into millions of tiny pieces!"

When there was no response, Hanuman ripped off the top of the mountain, sprang into the air, and quickly flew the 1000 yojanas back to Lanka. As all the monkeys shouted with glee, Hanuman came in for a landing upon the peak of Mount Trikuta.

Thereafter, when Lord Rama and Laksmana were made to inhale the fragrance of the herbs, all Their wounds were immediately healed. The herbs were then administered to all the wounded monkeys, and as a result, they sprang to their feet, as if rousing up from a sound sleep. In fact, even those monkeys that had been killed were instantly revived to perfect health.

After the accomplishment of his mission, Hanuman quickly returned to the Himalayas and put the mountain peak back in its proper place. This whole episode took place in just one day, and when Hanuman returned to Lanka, it was already dark.

Sugriva said, "My dear Hanuman, since all the dead Raksasas have been thrown into the ocean by the order of Ravana, we cannot estimate how many enemy soldiers have been killed. Still, we know that all of Ravana's sons, except Indrajit, have been slain, and so Lanka is practically defenseless. I suggest that we all take torches and invade the city at night, just to throw the enemy into chaos."

Soon thereafter, the monkeys began their march to Lanka. When they came to the city gates, the Raksasa guards fled in fear, and so they were able to enter Lanka without a fight. With torches in hand, the monkeys ran through the streets, setting all the palaces ablaze. Within a short while, fire raged on all sides, while thousands of buildings toppled to the ground. The whole of Lanka was in a state of confusion as burnt Raksasas and animals ran wildly in all directions, wailing in anguish.

At this time Lord Rama twanged His bowstring. The sound could be heard above the clamor and it struck terror into the hearts of the Raksasas. Lord Rama destroyed Lanka's principal gate with showers of arrows, and upon witnessing this destruction, Ravana became highly enraged. He dispatched the sons of Kumbhakarna—Kumbha and Nikumbha—along with many other leading Raksasa warriors, including Yupaksa, Sontaksa, Prajangha and Kampana. Both the Raksasas and monkeys were eager for battle, so the fighting that followed was very fierce.

Angada killed Kampana and then severed Prajangha's head with his hand. After this, Yupaksa and Sontaksa wrestled with Mainda and Dvivida. Dvivida tore at Sontaksa's face with his nails, and after throwing him to the ground, he crushed him to death with his knees. Mainda pressed Yupaksa tightly in his arms, squeezing the life right out of his body.

Kumbha severely wounded Mainda and Dvivida, and upon seeing the plight of his two maternal uncles, Angada became enraged. He rushed at Kumbha to gain revenge, so that a fierce duel took place between the two. After receiving numerous wounds, Angada fainted onto the ground and so some of the monkeys went to inform Lord Rama. The Lord then dispatched Jambha and Sugriva to come to Angada's aid. After showering one another with weapons, Sugriva managed to come within reach of Kumbha's chariot.

All of a sudden, Sugriva jumped onto Kumbha's chariot, snatched the bow right out of his hands and broke it in half. After jumping back to the ground, Sugriva said, "O Kumbha, you have certainly exhibited great prowess on the battlefield today. Your father was naturally endowed with great prowess, whereas Ravana achieved superior strength because of the benedictions he received from Lord Brahma. You are powerful in both ways. I could have killed you just now, but because you are exhausted from fighting, I will let you return to Lanka to rest. Next time, we can fight under fair conditions."

Kumbha appreciated Sugriva's noble words, but without accepting the offer, he suddenly grabbed the monkey king in his strong arms. As the two squeezed each other in their embrace, the earth began to sink due to the immense pressure exerted upon it. Finally, Sugriva lifted up Kumbha and threw him into the ocean, causing waves as high as mountains to swell in all directions. Kumbha sprang back onto the land, and after rushing impetuously, he threw Sugriva down and began punching him in the chest. Although his armor became smashed and blood gushed from his wounds, Sugriva clenched his powerful fist and pounded it on Kumbha's chest with all his might. As a consequence, the mighty Raksasa fell to the ground, mortally wounded.

After seeing his brother slain, Nikumbha roared so loudly and brandished his club so brazenly that all the monkeys and Raksasas became stunned with fright. When Hanuman came before him, Nikumbha struck him in the chest with his massive club. Still, the only consequence of this mighty blow was that the club shattered into many pieces.
