Hanuman quickly responded by smashing his clenched fist against Nikumbha's chest, making him stagger backwards. After recovering, Nikumbha captured Hanuman in his arms and began to carry him away. Hanuman soon freed himself and then threw Nikumbha violently to the ground. After pouncing upon the Raksasa, Hanuman crushed him with his knees while simultaneously twisting his head off from his trunk. Upon witnessing this ghastly but heroic feat, the elated monkeys shouted with joy.

After receiving the news of Kumbha and Nikumbha's death, Ravana ordered the son of Khara named Maharaksa to go and fight with Lord Rama and Laksmana. Although he boasted excessively about his prowess, as Maharaksa approached the battlefield, inauspicious omens heralded his defeat. The whip slipped out of his driver's hands, and his flagpole fell to the ground. His horses began to falter, as if in a depressed mood, as tears glided down from their eyes. Although a dense dust storm suddenly arose, bringing with it gloomy darkness, Maharaksa ignored all these omens and confidently entered the battlefield.

When the fighting began, many monkeys fell down dead, having been pierced by Maharaksa's arrows. As the Raksasas roared triumphantly and the monkeys began to flee for their lives, Lord Rama picked up His bow. As the Lord sent forth His arrows, the enraged Maharaksa challenged Him with harsh words. Lord Rama simply laughed at the puffed-up Raksasa and exclaimed, "You proud fool! There has never been a war that was won merely by the use of words!"

Maharaksa released a steady stream of arrows, but Lord Rama tore them all to pieces with His own. After coming close, the two began to fight furiously as the demigods assembled overhead to witness the spectacular duel. Although Lord Rama and Maharaksa deeply wounded each other, their strength only seemed to go on increasing. So many arrows were released by them that no one could see the battlefield clearly. Flaring up with rage, Lord Rama split Maharaksa's bow in half, smashed his chariot to pieces, and killed his driver and horses. Jumping from the broken chariot, Maharaksa grabbed a spear that had been presented to him by Rudra and hurled it impetuously at Lord Rama.

Lord Rama was able to cut the spear to pieces with His arrows. Then, as Lord Rama carefully fitted an agni weapon onto His bowstring, Maharaksa madly rushed at Him with his fist upraised. Before Maharaksa could reach him, Lord Rama released His celestial arrow, and when it pierced the Raksasa's heart, he fell to the ground, dead. Seeing that their leader had been slain, the Raksasa army beat a hasty retreat back to Lanka.

Ravana ordered Indrajit to re-enter the battlefield, and so the prince first went to his sacrificial altar. After pouring libations of ghee into the sacrificial fire, Indrajit grabbed a live goat by the neck and threw it in as an offering. As before, Agni personally came to accept the offering, as the sacrificial fire blazed brightly. Indrajit mounted his chariot, after protecting it and all his weapons with the potency of Lord Brahma.

Thereafter, Indrajit showered his arrows upon Lord Rama and Laksmana while remaining invisible in the sky. The sons of Dasaratha tried to counter-attack this assault with celestial weapons, but none of them could even touch the powerful son of Ravana. By using his mystic power, Indrajit created darkness, and when he resumed showering arrows, Lord Rama and Laksmana became pierced all over Their bodies. While aiming in the direction from which the streams of arrows were falling, Lord Rama managed to pierce Indrajit, but Ravana's son remained undaunted.

Finally, Laksmana became so frustrated because of His helplessness that He declared, "I will invoke the brahmastra to destroy all the Raksasas once and for all!"

Lord Rama said, "My dear brother, while engaged in fighting with an enemy, it is not proper to kill others who are not taking part in the battle. I am also very eager to put and end to this powerful Raksasa. Be patient. Let Us discharge more of Our celestial weapons at him."

Indrajit could understand that Lord Rama intended to finish him off quickly and so he hastily returned to Lanka. After a little while, he once again came out of the city, riding upon his chariot, and this time he remained visible and was accompanied by an illusory Sitadevi, sitting by his side. Seeing Indrajit coming, all the monkeys, headed by Hanuman, rushed to attack him, but when they saw that Sitadevi was seated on his chariot, they became exceedingly depressed.

While the monkeys looked on helplessly, Indrajit drew his sword and grabbed Sitadevi by the hair. The illusory Sitadevi cried out, "O my Lord!" and Indrajit struck her violently with his fist. Feeling unbearable agony, Hanuman shouted, "You are the most abominable and evil-minded of all the Raksasas. Rest assured that very soon you will be punished in the hell reserved for the killers of women!"

After saying this, Hanuman rushed at Indrajit, followed by many other monkeys, but the Raksasa repelled them with his arrows. Indrajit declared, "Watch me now as I kill Sita. That which gives pain to the enemy must be done by one endeavoring to win a war!"

Having said this, Indrajit took his sword and violently slashed the illusory Sitadevi diagonally so that she fell to the ground in a pool of blood. Indrajit taunted Hanuman by saying, "Sita is dead, and so all your endeavors for retrieving her have proved to be a waste of time and energy."

For some time, Hanuman attempted to retaliate. Then, he thought, "Since mother Sitadevi has been killed, it would be best for the monkeys to retreat for the time being so that I can report to Lord Rama and receive His instructions."

When Hanuman left the scene of battle, Indrajit returned to his sanctuary, Nikumbhila, to make offerings in the sacrificial fire for the benefit of the Raksasas. When Hanuman met Lord Rama, he informed Him of how Sitadevi had been mercilessly slain by Indrajit. As soon as He heard this, Lord Rama fainted onto the ground, and so the monkeys hurriedly came and sprinkled water over Him.

Laksmana picked up Lord Rama in His arms and lamented, "What terrible misfortune! It appears that good and evil have changed roles! Or, maybe it is that virtue is not strong enough to give a good result without the help of prowess. I think that it is best to depend upon one's strength and forget all the so-called considerations of morality. I hereby vow to destroy all of Lanka this very day, including Indrajit and Ravana! Î Lord Rama, please remember Your divine position as the incarnation of Lord Visnu and thus give up Your excessive sorrow."

At this time Vibhisana came there. When he saw grief-stricken Laksmana with Lord Rama lying unconscious upon his lap, Vibhisana anxiously inquired, “What has happened?"

As Laksmana proceeded to explain about Sitadevi's death, Vibhisana interrupted, saying, "It is not possible that Sitadevi has been killed by Indrajit, for Ravana would never voluntarily part with her. The Sitadevi you saw was an illusory creation. Î Laksmana, You should immediately go to Nikumbhila and kill Indrajit while he is making sacrificial offerings. If you do not attack him now, he will once again make himself invisible after finishing his rituals, and thus he will be invulnerable."

Lord Rama was too aggrieved to clearly understand what Vibhisana was saying and so He asked him to repeat himself. Vibhisana elaborated, "Long ago, Indrajit performed severe austerities by which he satisfied Lord Brahma. When Brahma awarded Indrajit the brahmastra and flying horses, he predicted, 'You will be killed by an enemy as you engage in performing sacrifices at Nikumbhila.' Now, if Laksmana does not go and kill Indrajit, he will become so powerful that surely all the monkeys will be slain."

Lord Rama ordered, "O Laksmana, go and attack Indrajit. Take with you Hanuman, Vibhisana, and the rest of the army."

Laksmana touched Lord Rama's lotus feet, and after once again vowing to kill Indrajit, He departed. As He approached Nikumbhila, Laksmana saw innumerable Raksasas guarding it, and so Vibhisana advised, "Let their army be attacked first. When the Raksasas are hard-pressed, Indrajit will appear."

Thus it so happened that when Indrajit saw how his army was being attacked, he mounted his chariot, even though his sacrifices remained incomplete. As Indrajit engaged in battle with Hanuman, Vibhisana took Laksmana to the spot underneath a banyan tree where Indrajit would offer oblations into the sacrificial fire before invisibly entering the battlefield. Vibhisana knew that Indrajit would soon come to complete his rituals and so he urged Laksmana to wait there and kill him upon his return.

Laksmana did as advised, and sure enough, Indrajit soon returned. When Laksmana challenged him to fight, Indrajit saw Vibhisana and angrily said, "You, my uncle, have come here to do me harm! I am the favorite son of your elder brother. Don't you have any feelings for family members? You are also born in the dynasty of Raksasas. Have you no pride of birth? Wicked fool! Can't you see how those who actually follow religious principles must despise you for betraying your own kinsmen by joining the side of the enemy? Is there no difference between living with one's family and licking the feet of strangers? A person's worst relative is always better than an outsider. Only you could act so treacherously!"

Vibhisana replied, "My dear nephew, if you are so concerned with dharma, then why do you now reproach me, your elder? It is true that I have been born in the family of Raksasas, but my nature is quite different. I hate impudence and cruelty. It is your father and not I who has caused this breach in our family. According to religious injunctions, it is one's duty to renounce all connections with sinful persons, even if they happen to be family relations."

"One who kidnaps another's wife is most abominable, and should be cast off as much as a snake that clings to one's body. To mistrust friends, to have sexual relations with the wife of another, and to steal another's property—these three faults always lead to destruction. Therefore, Ravana is doomed, and along with him, you and all of Lanka will be destroyed. Î Indrajit, you are just a proud, foolish and ill-mannered boy. Say whatever you like, but soon you will lay down your life, being pierced by Laksmana's arrows."

Laksmana was mounted upon Hanuman, and after an exchange of challenges, a fierce duel began. After some time, Indrajit appeared to become pale and weak, because of Laksmana's onslaught of arrows. Vibhisana then urged Laksmana to make short work of His adversary. When Laksmana released some more powerful arrows, the son of Ravana became momentarily dazed.

After quickly recovering, Indrajit chided, "Remember how you were defeated by me before!" He then pierced Laksmana with seven arrows, Hanuman with ten, and Vibhisana with a hundred.

Laksmana simply laughed and proceeded to cover Indrajit with so many arrows that his golden armor was shattered to pieces. The Raksasa retaliated with 1000 arrows, so that Laksmana's armor also became somewhat damaged. Thus, neither could gain the upper hand as the fighting continued for a long time, both being determined to gain victory. Innumerable arrows pierced right through their bodies and entered the earth, so that both warriors became soaked with blood. Besides these, there were countless arrows that had been counteracted, and they were seen spread over the ground like so much kusa grass.

Vibhisana did not want to fight with his nephew, and so he attacked other Raksasas. Jambavan went on a rampage as the fighting between the two armies became very heated. Hanuman put down Laksmana so that he could join the melee. As the sun sank below the horizon, he continued to exterminate the Raksasas by the thousands. Darkness came early because the sky was covered by the incessant flow of arrows. Indeed, Laksmana and Indrajit worked so quickly that the onlookers could not detect the movement of their arms.

Laksmana beheaded Indrajit's driver, and so the Raksasa had to control the reins while simultaneously discharging his arrows. This gave Laksmana an advantage, and so gradually, Indrajit began to lose heart. Four monkey warriors then jumped on Indrajit's four horses and tore them to shreds with their sharp teeth and claws. These monkeys proceeded to smash the chariot and so Indrajit was forced to dismount.

In the dense darkness, Laksmana began to close in, and so Indrajit ordered his army to keep the monkeys busy while he reentered Lanka to get a new chariot.

After some time, when Indrajit returned to the battlefield with renewed enthusiasm, he began striking down the monkeys by the thousands, causing the survivors to take shelter of Laksmana.

Laksmana broke Indrajit's bow in half and pierced his chest with five arrows. In the fierce fighting that followed, Laksmana flared up in anger and beheaded Indrajit's driver, throwing the horses into confusion. Although he tried to retaliate, Indrajit found that all his arrows simply bounced off Laksmana. Considering Laksmana's armor to be impenetrable, Indrajit concentrated on striking Laksmana in the forehead. Laksmana quickly responded by hitting Indrajit in the face with five arrows.

Indrajit pierced Vibhisana's face with three arrows. In a fit of rage, Vibhisana rushed at his nephew. With his club, Vibhisana pounded Indrajit's horses to death. After jumping from his disabled chariot, Indrajit hurled a spear at his uncle. At this point, Laksmana intervened by cutting the spear to pieces with his arrows. When Vibhisana pierced his chest with five arrows, Indrajit placed a terrifying yama weapon on his bowstring. Upon seeing this grave danger, Laksmana invoked a wonderful arrow that had been awarded to Him by Kuvera in a dream. When the two weapons were released, they collided in mid-air, lighting up the sky very brightly. There was an earth-shaking explosion as the weapons shattered into hundreds of pieces amidst crackling sparks and billows of smoke.

Next, Laksmana invoked a Varuna weapon that Indrajit neutralized with a rudra weapon. Taking the offensive, Indrajit discharged an agni weapon, but Laksmana successfully counteracted it with a surya missile. Indrajit put an asura weapon on his bowstring and immediately numerous swords, lances, clubs, cakras and axes issued forth, but by employing a rudra weapon, Laksmana was able to subdue the attack. At this point, Indrajit began to lose heart. Sensing this, Laksmana carefully picked up a very powerful weapon belonging to King Indra.

As Laksmana placed this invincible arrow on His bowstring and drew it back to His ear, He prayed, "O chief of the celestials, if Lord Rama is truly the unrivaled Lord and the shelter of truth and virtue, let this weapon kill Ravana's son."

After being released by Laksmana, that effulgent arrow soared swiftly through the sky and cut off Indrajit's head. This incomparable son of Ravana had been the greatest impediment for Lord Rama's army. The monkeys shouted with joy and jumped up and down, while the panic-stricken Raksasas fled in all directions.

In the sky, the demigods and great rishis ecstatically praised Laksmana for his victory. As flowers showered from heaven upon Laksmana; Vibhisana, Hanuman and Jamba van came to congratulate him. While lashing their tails about and dancing with great joy, the monkeys shouted again and again, "Java Laksmana! Java Sri Lord Rama!"

Laksmana was exhausted and wounded, and so He was leaning on Vibhisana and Hanuman when He came before Lord Rama and circumambulated Him. Vibhisana narrated the account of Indrajit's death, and while listening to it, Lord Rama experienced great transcendental pleasure.

After smelling Laksmana's head and congratulating Him with great affection, Lord Rama said, "Indrajit was just like Ravana's right arm. Now that he has been killed after three days and nights of battle, the Raksasa king will surely come out to fight."

Laksmana was tormented with pain due to his numerous wounds, and so Lord Rama ordered Susena to treat him, along with the multitudes of other monkey soldiers. When Susena made Him inhale the aroma of a certain herb, Laksmana was delighted to find that all His pain vanished and His wounds immediately healed.

Meanwhile, Ravana's ministers came and informed him of how Indrajit had been killed by Laksmana, with the assistance of Vibhisana. Ravana fainted as soon as he heard the news, and it was only after a long time that he regained consciousness. The king lamented very bitterly over the death of his beloved son, who was to be his successor, and while doing so, he became possessed of a violent anger.

In this state of mad rage, Ravana decided to take revenge by killing Sitadevi. At that time his appearance was so ferocious that the other Raksasas hid out of fear. Being eager for battle, Ravana called for his impenetrable armor and the bow and arrows that had been given to him by Lord Brahma at the completion of his severe austerities.

Ravana declared, "What Indrajit did as a conjuring trick, I will now do in fact, just to torment the enemy!"

Saying this, Ravana violently unsheathed his sword and rushed toward the asoka grove, followed by Mandodari and several ministers. Although these well-wishers tried their best to restrain him, Ravana soon came before terrified Sitadevi while waving his sword about menacingly.

Sitadevi could understand that Ravana intended to kill her and so she wondered, "What has happened to make the wicked Raksasa king come here like this, before my allotted twelve months has elapsed? Has he become too frustrated by my constant refusals, or is he going to kill me out of desperation, being unable to defeat my Lord and Laksmana? I should have taken Hanuman's advice and let him carry me back to my Lord on his back. Then I never would have been put into this horrible situation!"

Feeling compassion for grief-stricken Sitadevi, a pious minister named Suparsva said to Ravana, "O lord, you have studied the Vedas and observed the strictest vows. How can you even think of harming a woman? Let Sita be, and instead, vent your terrible wrath upon your real enemy. Today is the fourteenth day of the dark lunar fortnight. Tomorrow, as the new moon emerges, march against Rama and His monkey hordes, to obtain victory. I am sure that after killing Rama, you will be able to enjoy Sita to your heart's content. Why should you prematurely frustrate your ardent desire in this way?"

Ravana accepted this flattering advice. After giving up the idea of killing Sitadevi, he returned to his palace. Later on, as he sat morosely upon his throne, being still highly aggrieved because of the loss of his dear son, Ravana gave the following order, "I want all the remaining Raksasas to go at once and attack Rama. If they are unable to kill Him, tomorrow I will personally go and fight."

It was just before dawn when all the Raksasas rushed out of the city to fight with the monkeys. By sunrise, a furious battle raged on all sides. When the Raksasas succeeded in striking down multitudes of enemy soldiers, the surviving monkeys ran to the shelter of Lord Rama's lotus feet. Lord Rama then employed a spectacular gandharva weapon that issued forth streams of arrows. The showers of arrows became so dense that the Raksasas could no longer see Lord Rama. They could only see the results of His onslaught—the destruction of innumerable Raksasa soldiers.

By the influence of the gandharva weapon, Lord Rama sometimes became invisible. At other times, the Raksasas saw 1000 Lord Ramas, standing before them in all directions. Within the span of just over an hour, 200,000 foot soldiers, 18,000 elephant warriors, 14,000 cavalry, and numerous chariot fighters in the Raksasa army were slain. At last, the survivors fled to Lanka while the demigods in the sky ecstatically praised Lord Rama for His victory.

Lord Rama told the monkey leaders, "You should know that such ability to employ celestial weapons is only within the power of Lord Siva and Me."

Meanwhile, in Lanka, the widows of the slain Raksasas grouped together and piteously lamented the deaths of their husbands. While wailing aloud, they cried out, "We curse Surpanakha, for she is the root cause of the enmity between Lord Rama and Ravana that has destroyed our race. So many times in the past, Lord Rama defeated the Raksasas, and so Ravana should have been aware of His invincibility. Why did not our foolish king accept Vibhisana's good advice and thus avoid this wholesale destruction?"

As he listened to the lamentations of the Raksasa women, Ravana became mad with anger. While hissing like a trampled serpent, he ordered his ministers, "Prepare my army for battle at once! Today I will kill Rama, Laksmana and all the monkeys with my torrents of arrows. Bring my chariot and make sure that every surviving able-bodied Raksasa soldier follows me."

At Ravana's command, 100,000 chariots warriors, 300,000 elephant warriors, 60 million horse soldiers, and uncountable foot soldiers assembled. Ravana mounted his celestial chariot, which was drawn by eight horses and was equipped with numerous weapons belonging to the principal demigods. He was heralded by a tumultuous fanfare of blowing conch shells and trumpets, as well as shouts of encouragement from the citizens.

When the monkeys heard the uproar, a great fear entered their hearts. As Ravana left Lanka through the northern gate, a gloomy atmosphere prevailed, for the sun did not shine brightly. Dark clouds appeared overhead, raining blood. Suddenly, Ravana's horses stumbled. The king's left eye twitched, his left arm throbbed, his face grew pale, and his voice became hoarse. Meteors streaked across the sky, and a vulture came and perched upon Ravana's flagpole, and yet, without minding these evil omens in the least, the king of the Raksasas continued to advance toward his doom.

When the fighting began, Ravana created havoc, for none of the monkeys could stand before his onslaught of arrows. Sugriva rallied the monkeys, and while Ravana turned his attention to Lord Rama, Virupaksa rushed at the monkey king, riding upon an elephant. Sugriva tore up a giant tree and violently smashed the elephant, throwing it backwards. Virupaksa jumped from the wounded elephant, picked up his sword and shield and angrily rushed at Sugriva. Sugriva hurled a boulder at the on-rushing foe, but Virupaksa dodged it and slashed him with his sword.

Being momentarily dazed, Sugriva fell to the ground, but then he sprang up and punched Virupaksa in the chest. This only served to enrage the Raksasa, who then cut off Sugriva's armor and gave him a kick that made him fall over backwards.

Once again, Sugriva sprang to his feet, but when he tried to give Virupaksa a slap, the Raksasa artfully dodged it and struck him on the chest with his fist. Flaring up with rage, Sugriva looked for an opportunity to strike. Then, suddenly, he smashed the palm of his hand on Virupaksa's forehead with all his might. That powerful blow made blood pour profusely from all nine holes of Virupaksa’s body as he slumped dead onto the ground.

Meanwhile, as Ravana advanced toward Lord Rama, he invoked a rahu weapon that created a great destruction of monkeys. Laksmana, who was fighting by Lord Rama's side, rushed forward to intercept Ravana with His torrents of arrows'. Ravana easily counteracted these arrows, and leaving Laksmana aside, he approached Lord Rama.

A fierce duel followed between Lord Rama and His archenemy, their volleys of arrows covering the entire sky. The monkeys and Raksasas suspended their fighting for the time being, just to witness the spectacular contest.

Ravana struck Lord Rama in the forehead with an arrow. Remaining undaunted, Lord Rama retaliated by discharging a rudra weapon, and yet, even though innumerable arrows issued forth, they simply bounced off Ravana's impenetrable armor without causing him the slightest harm.

Ravana next invoked a mystic asura weapon so that countless arrows having the heads of lions, tigers, vultures, serpents and alligators rushed toward Lord Rama with their gaping mouths wide open. Even though Lord Rama employed an agni weapon that melted Ravana's arrows in mid-air, thousands of monkey soldiers were slain. Still, Lord Rama and the monkey chiefs were pleased, because that weapon was well known as being one of Ravana's most formidable.

Next, Ravana invoked a rudra weapon that had been created by Maya Danava, and as a result, numerous maces, lances, thunderbolts and nooses issued forth in a steady stream. Lord Rama quickly foiled the rudra weapon with His gandharva weapon, but Ravana discharged a brilliant surya weapon. As a result, enormous, effulgent cakras shot forth in all directions.

With a display of inconceivable skill, Lord Rama shattered all these cakras with His arrows, but as He did so, Ravana pierced Him in the chest with ten powerful arrows. Lord Rama did not flinch, however, and He retaliated by piercing Ravana with numerous arrows.

Desiring to enter the fray, Laksmana shot seven arrows that cut down Ravana's flag. With another arrow, Laksmana severed the head of Ravana's driver, and with five more he shattered his bow.

At this time Vibhisana rushed forward and killed Ravana's horses with his mace, thus forcing his enraged brother to jump from his chariot. When Ravana angrily hurled a lance at Vibhisana, Laksmana cut it to pieces with three arrows as it soared through the air. Next, Ravana picked up a magical spear that had been made by Maya Danava. Seeing how Vibhisana's life was in danger, Laksmana quickly showered so many arrows upon Ravana that he became stunned while standing with the spear in his hand.

Ravana told Laksmana, "Because You dared to rescue Vibhisana, the spear meant for him will now take away Your life."

After saying this, Ravana roared like a lion and hurled Maya Danava's mystical spear. As it unerringly soared through the air toward Laksmana, Lord Rama requested the spear to become ineffectual. Nonetheless, the spear pierced through Laksmana's chest and stuck into the ground, making the son of Sumitra fall to the ground seriously wounded.

When Lord Rama saw Laksmana's piteous condition, He became exceedingly despondent and His eyes filled with tears. While suppressing His intense grief, Lord Rama went and extracted the spear from Laksmana’s body, even as Ravana showered arrows upon Him. After breaking the spear in half, Lord Rama picked up Laksmana and tearfully embraced Him.

Having ordered Hanuman and Sugriva to guard Laksmana, Lord Rama angrily declared, "I will now exhibit My full prowess against the wicked Ravana, so that he will soon lay down dead upon the battlefield. All the monkeys can go and sit at their ease upon the mountaintops. Let them become spectators, along with the demigods, as I perform a wonderful feat that will be glorified until the dissolution of the material world!"

Thereafter, Lord Rama attacked Ravana with a vengeance. As they proceeded to cover each other with torrents of arrows, the sounds produced by the twanging of their bows were astonishing to hear. The infuriated Lord Rama soon overpowered Ravana, though, and so the Raksasa king left the battlefield out of fear for his life.

Lord Rama returned to where Laksmana was lying. With great anguish He told Susena, "Just by looking at My wounded brother, My strength withers. Without Laksmana, victory will have no meaning for Me. If He dies, then I will follow Him, just as He followed Me when I was exiled to the forest."

In a reassuring tone of voice, Susena said, "My dear Lord, Laksmana is not dying. Just see how His facial luster has not faded, and His eyes still sparkle brightly. Hanuman must immediately be dispatched to the Mahodaya Mountain, so that he can bring the herbs called visalya-karani, savarna-karani, sanjiva-karani and sandhana-karani. By this medicinal treatment, Laksmana can immediately be restored to good health."

Once again, Hanuman made a quick leap to the Himalayas, but when he arrived at the Mahodaya Mountain he could not recognize the required herbs. Finally, out of frustration, he tore off the entire mountain peak. While holding it in both hands, Hanuman bounded into the air and returned to Lanka. After placing the mountain peak near Susena, Hanuman lay down to rest for a moment and said, "I could not recognize the proper herbs and so I decided to bring the whole mountain."

Susena praised Hanuman very highly and then went to look for the required herbs. After finding them, he crushed the herbs into powder. When Laksmana was made to smell them, He instantly became cured of all wounds. As Laksmana stood up, Lord Rama firmly embraced Him and said, "It is My good fortune that You are well. My dear Laksmana, without You, there would be no use in recovering Sita, or even maintaining My life any longer."

Laksmana said, "My dear brother, please do not indulge in such grief any longer. Quickly make good Your vow to kill Ravana and then install Vibhisana as the king of Lanka."

Meanwhile, Ravana mounted another chariot and rushed out from the city, being eager to fight with Lord Rama. Lord Rama picked up His bow and began to shower arrows upon Ravana, and the fighting became very aggressive. From the sky, the demigods exclaimed, "This battle is not being fairly fought, because Lord Rama is standing on the ground while Ravana rides upon his chariot!"

Taking this cue, Indra called for Matali and ordered him to take his chariot to where Lord Rama was stationed upon the battlefield. Matali came before Lord Rama, driving Indra’s golden chariot drawn by 1000 horses having a greenish complexion.

The celestial charioteer announced, "My dear Lord, King Indra requests You to kindly accept this chariot. Inside, You will find Indra's bow and armor, as well as an incomparable spear and various celestial arrows. My dear Lord Rama, please mount this chariot at once, for the demigods are very aggrieved to see how You have to fight while standing upon the ground."

After circumambulating Indra's chariot, Lord Rama mounted it. Thereafter, a thrilling duel took place between Lord Rama and Ravana. When Ravana released a gandharva weapon, Lord Rama quickly neutralized it with another gandharva weapon. Ravana next discharged a raksasa weapon that took the form of innumerable serpents having blazing, wide-open mouths. In response, Lord Rama released a garuda weapon that transformed into countless golden eagles that quickly ate all of Ravana's snake-arrows.

This inflamed Ravana's anger. He rapidly retaliated by discharging 1000 arrows at Lord Rama, numerous more that pierced Matali, a single shaft that knocked down Indra's flag, and many more that afflicted the horses. It appeared as if Lord Rama was being put into great difficulty and so the demigods and monkey warriors became very anxious.

Lord Rama then assumed a very ferocious form of anger, and because of this, the earth began to quake. Many evil omens became visible, so that all beings, including Ravana, became afraid. At this time, numerous demons appeared in the sky to encourage Ravana, just as the demigods were rooting for Lord Rama. As if in response, Ravana picked up a dreadful dart and roared so ferociously that heaven and earth began to tremble. He shouted," Rama, prepare Yourself, for now You are going to die!"

After saying this, Ravana hurled that terrible weapon. As it soared through the sky, it made a loud roaring noise and was encircled by lightning. Lord Rama released innumerable arrows in an attempt to intercept that awesome missile, just as Indra tries to stop the fire of devastation that occurs at the end of the kalpa by pouring torrential rain. Lord Rama could see that the onrushing dart was consuming His arrows, and so He picked up the celebrated spear of Indra and hurled it. While soaring through the air, that spear illuminated all directions and collided with Ravana's dart, shattering it to pieces that fell harmlessly to the ground.

Lord Rama and Ravana continued to assail one another with showers of arrows, so that blood flowed from the wounds that covered their bodies. Still, being undaunted, Lord Rama laughed disdainfully and rebuked Ravana by saying, "O kidnapper of Sita, you are a first-class fool for considering yourself to be a great hero. Factually, you are a great coward, for you only dared to take away My wife after luring Me far away. How can you be proud of overpowering a poor, defenseless woman in the absence of her husband? O Ravana, today you will meet your deserved end as the body you are so attached to will become food for vultures and jackals.”

After saying this, Lord Rama attacked Ravana with redoubled energy and greater dexterity. This was combined with the volleys of stones hurled by the monkeys. Within a short time, Ravana became dazed and bewildered at heart, so that he could no longer properly take up and discharge his weapons. Seeing this, Ravana's charioteer drove him swiftly away from the battlefield, beyond the reach of Lord Rama's arrows.

When Ravana came to his senses, he chastised his driver, saying, "By your shameless action I can understand that you consider me to be impotent, and a coward! By acting independently, you have spoiled my reputation. Take me back to the battlefield at once!"

The chariot driver replied, "O King, I took you away from the fighting for your welfare. You had lost all your strength, and the horses had also become exhausted. Many inauspicious omens were visible as well, and so I did what I considered to be my foremost duty."

Ravana became pacified by his charioteer's words. He ordered, "Go quickly to where Rama is staying! Once Ravana has made up his mind, he does not turn back until he has completely destroyed his enemies!"

Meanwhile, in Ravana's absence, Agastya Rsi came to see Lord Rama, knowing that He had become fatigued from fighting. After being properly honored and welcomed, Agastya Rsi said, "My dear Rama, kindly receive from me the Aditya-hrdaya prayer that is meant for satisfying the sun-god. This mantra bestows great blessings, and cleanses one of all sins. One who chants this hymn prolongs his life and remains fixed on the eternal path of religion, 'O deity of the sun, I offer my obeisances unto you. You are the chief of the demigods, because of your unlimited effulgence that maintains the entire universe. You are worshiped by both demigods and asuras, for obtaining ultimate welfare. You are the reservoir of universal energy and the source of life for all beings. As such, you perfectly represent the Supreme Lord, Visnu, as His empowered expansion. Men who know the Vedas worship you as Lord Narayana, situated within the sun, by chanting suitable prayers three times daily. It is you alone who destroys the dense darkness of this universe, and thus I bow down to you, O splendorous one! Again and again I offer my obeisances unto you, the eye of the Supreme Lord and witness of the world's activities.'"

"My dear Rama, if you recite this mantra while worshiping the sun-god as the all-in-all, You will certainly be able to conquer your enemy. Anyone in difficulty who worships Surya with this prayer never comes to grief."

After the departure of Agastya Muni, Lord Rama felt rejuvenated. He sipped water three times, while uttering the holy name of the Lord. Then, facing the sun, Lord Rama recited the Aditya-hrdaya prayer, and as He did, He felt great transcendental bliss. Afterwards, Lord Rama picked up His bow and advanced toward Ravana, determined to kill him in an all-out effort. At this time Surya spoke to Lord Rama from the sky, urging Him, "Do not delay! Go quickly!"

Lord Rama ordered, "O Matali, drive quickly to where Ravana is staying, but at the same time be very cautious."

Then, remembering that He was speaking to Indra's charioteer, Lord Rama felt embarrassed and so He apologized, "I am very sorry to have instructed you as if I were your master. It is just that I am eager to kill Ravana, so please excuse My offense."

Matali was very touched by Lord Rama's wonderful display of humility. As he maneuvered Indra's chariot close by Ravana's side, Lord Rama and his adversary began to exchange arrows. Soon, the fighting became very intense. Clouds rained blood upon Ravana's chariot, and a flock of vultures followed it. A huge meteor fell nearby and so all the Raksasas became exceedingly despondent, while Ravana became convinced that he would soon die.

On the other hand, very pleasing signs appeared before Lord Rama, and so He became convinced that victory would soon be His. In the duel that followed, Lord Rama and Ravana gradually exhibited the entire wealth of their respective prowess. The competition was so intense that both armies became stunned with amazement. Indeed, all the soldiers stood motionless, just like paintings, and because they were so absorbed in watching the fight, they did not even think of attacking one another.

When Ravana tried to knock down Indra's flag, Lord Rama deflected his arrows with His own. Then, because He was determined to match Ravana, blow for blow, Lord Rama knocked down the Raksasa king's flag. Ravana pierced Indra's horses, but when the celestial steeds did not even stagger, he became angry and frustrated.

At last, Ravana resorted to the Raksasa power of illusion to send forth clubs, discs, trees, and mountain peaks. Lord Rama was able to counteract all these before they reached His chariot, but they fell upon the army of monkeys. Lord Rama and Ravana continued to dispatch thousands of weapons at each other. As they collided in the air, these weapons fell onto the battlefield.

In this way the fighting continued for about an hour. Lord Rama matched Ravana, blow for blow, while all created beings looked on, their minds astonished with wonder. Both drivers also displayed great skill. When the chariots came side by side, Lord Rama forced Ravana's four horses to turn away by piercing them with four arrows. This incited Ravana's anger and so he repeatedly pierced Lord Rama in retaliation. Still, Lord Rama remained undisturbed.

Thereafter, the exchange of all varieties of weapons became so feverish that the fighting was unparalleled in the history of warfare. Sometimes Ravana fought in his ten-headed feature, and at other times he fought in his normal form, having one head. On one occasion, Lord Rama managed to sever Ravana's head with an arrow, but as that head fell to the ground, a duplicate one miraculously cropped up in its place. Lord Rama severed that head, and yet another one immediately manifested itself as a replacement. Again and again Lord Rama cut off Ravana's head, until altogether one hundred such heads lay on the battlefield.

Because each time a new head appeared to replace the one He severed, Lord Rama began to wonder, "With these arrows I formerly killed Marica, Khara and Viradha. I pierced seven sala trees and killed invincible Vali. These arrows had humbled great mountains and agitated the fathomless sea. How is it that they are ineffectual against Ravana?"

The duel continued at a furious pace. Both combatants were obsessed with the desire for killing the other. In fact, several days and nights passed without any break in the fighting.

At last, when Matali saw that Lord Rama was not gaining His desired victory, he inquired, "Why are you ’simply fighting defensively? My Lord, are You not aware of Your limitless potencies? The hour of doom has now arrived for the king of the Raksasas. Why don't You employ the divine brahmastra?"

Being thus reminded of this ultimate weapon, Lord Rama picked up the arrow that Agastya Rsi had formerly given Him at the time of their meeting in the Dandaka forest. That arrow had been constructed personally by Lord Brahma for Indra's use, and later on it was presented to Agastya. Garuda supplied the feathers for that wonderful arrow, and the sharp head combined the energy of the fire-god, Agni, and the sun-god. Mount Meru and Mount Mandara contributed their gravity to the arrow's weight, and its shaft was made from the subtle ethereal element.

This brahmastra weapon was omnipotent and infallible, and its dazzling effulgence made it rival the splendor of the sun. After empowering the brahmastra with the required mantras, Lord Rama placed it upon His bowstring. As the monkeys gazed upon that flaming arrow, their hearts became filled with delight, while a dreadful fear penetrated the cores of the hearts of all the Raksasas.

As Lord Rama pulled the bowstring back to His ear, the earth trembled and the heavens also appeared to become disturbed. When Lord Rama released the brahmastra, it sped through the air like death itself and violently fell upon the chest of wicked Ravana. After piercing right through the king of the Raksasa's heart, that effulgent arrow entered deep into the earth, taking his sinful life along with it. As that awesome brahmastra came and re-entered Lord Rama's quiver, Ravana dropped the bow from his hand and fell down dead from his chariot.

With great, transcendental ecstasy, the monkey warriors loudly proclaimed Lord Rama's victory as they attacked the fleeing Raksasa army. From the sky, the demigods shouted, "Sadhu! Sadhu!" ("Well done! Excellent!"), as they completely covered Lord Rama's chariot with showers of flowers and beat upon their celestial drums.

Now that Ravana was dead at last, the demigods and great rishis felt blessed relief and a peace of mind they had not enjoyed for a long time. A cool and gentle fragrant breeze began to blow, and the sun spread its rays very serenely, so that happiness seemed to pervade all directions.

Sugriva, Angada, Vibhisana and Laksmana were the first to come and pay their homage unto Lord Rama. However, when Vibhisana saw his elder brother lying dead upon the ground, he broke down and cried in an outburst of intense grief.

Meanwhile, news of Ravana's death spread throughout the inner apartments of the royal palace. Ravana's wives came out of the city and entered the battlefield, their hair disheveled, and dress and ornaments in disarray. Overcome by unbearable grief and wailing aloud, some of the women rolled in the dust like madwomen, while others went and embraced different parts of Ravana's dead body. Crying out, "O my lord! O my husband!" one of the ladies hung around Ravana’s neck, while others clutched his feet, rubbed his wounded chest, threw up their arms in despair, or fainted, being unable to bear the grief.

Amidst the sounds of loud wailing, these lamentations were heard, "Oh, dear husband, by ignoring our good advice, as well as that given by Vibhisana, you have brought about your destruction. Now that you are dead, our lives are also finished, for the wife has no other support than her husband. This is the inevitable end for such a cruel and hardhearted person as you. Who else would have dared to kidnap Sitadevi and keep her by force, against her will?"

Ravana's favorite queen, Mandodari, lamented, "My dear husband, even though you were so powerful, you could not stand before Lord Rama. You were too proud because of your acquired prowess and so you became a great burden for the earth. You foolishly could not understand that it was Lord Visnu who had descended upon the earth as Lord Rama, to relieve her of that burden."

"O Ravana, your sinful passion for Sitadevi has turned out to be the cause for all the Raksasas' destruction. You always masqueraded as a great hero, but you were proven to be a coward when you deceitfully kidnapped Sitadevi. Still, despite your abominable character, I do not see how I shall be able to go on living in your absence."

Finally, Mandodari fainted with her head upon Ravana's chest. Her co-wives lifted her up and revived her. At that time Lord Rama ordered Vibhisana, "You should begin the funeral rites for your elder brother without further delay. Only after the cremation of Ravana's body will it be possible to comfort his widows."

Vibhisana replied, "I do not want to perform the funeral ceremonies for a man who kidnapped the wives of others, who was merciless and tyrannical, and who was inclined toward irreligion. Of course, Ravana was my elder brother and so it is my duty to respect him, but on the other hand, because his actions were like those of an enemy, I feel that he does not deserve my worship."

Lord Rama said, "My dear Vibhisana, I approve of your words, because they uphold the cause of virtue. Still, I would like you to cremate your brother's body. After all, despite his faults, Ravana was a great hero, and it is a fact that with the death of his body, all hostilities have ended."

Vibhisana went inside the city to make arrangements for Ravana's funeral. After bringing his maternal grandfather, Malyavan, Vibhisana placed Ravana's body on the funeral carrier and then departed, along with other Raksasas who carried the firewood. Going toward the south, the party arrived at a consecrated place where they cremated Ravana's body according to the Vedic injunctions. Thereafter, Ravana's wives were consoled, and then everyone returned to Lanka.

Having given up His transcendental anger, Lord Rama now assumed a gentle appearance and laid aside his bow, arrows and armor. The demigods departed from their positions in the sky and returned to their abodes, and while going, they chanted the glories of Lord Rama with great satisfaction. After receiving due honor from Lord Rama, and permission to depart, Matali ascended into the sky upon Indra's chariot and returned to the heavenly kingdom.

After coming to Their camp, Lord Rama ordered Laksmana to perform Vibhisana's installation ceremony. In turn, Laksmana gave golden vessels to the chief monkeys and ordered them to go quickly and fetch water from the four seas. Soon thereafter, Laksmana performed the installation ceremony strictly according to the Vedic injunctions. All the citizens of Lanka came to the sacrificial arena with presentations of auspicious articles. After receiving these gifts, Vibhisana offered them to Lord Rama.

Lord Rama told Hanuman, who was standing nearby with folded hands, "Please go and find out how Sita is, and inform her that I have killed Ravana. After doing so, return here with any message she may give you."

After taking permission from King Vibhisana, Hanuman went to the asoka grove. There, he found grief-stricken Sitadevi, surrounded by hideous Raksasis.

Standing meekly in front of Sitadevi, Hanuman said, "Your husband has sent me here to give you this message, 'After many sleepless months, I have finally been able to accomplish My vow to rescue you. Now that your oppressor, the king of the Raksasas, is dead, you can give up all your anxiety.'"

Upon hearing this, Sitadevi became so happy that she could not reply for some time. When Hanuman asked why she remained silent, Sitadevi said, "I can hardly speak, because I am so elated. Î Hanuman, what you have told me is unlimitedly more valuable than any amount of gold or jewels.”

Standing with folded hands, Hanuman suggested, "If you so desire, I can kill all these hideous Raksasa women who have tormented you for so long. In fact, I would take great pleasure in avenging all the suffering you had to undergo. I simply await your permission."

By nature, Sitadevi was very kind to the downtrodden. She replied, "They are only foolish maidservants who had to carry out the orders of the king. Whatever I had suffered was the result of my own misdeeds, and these Raksasis only acted as instruments in the hands of destiny."

"My dear Hanuman, perhaps you have heard this old adage that was once spoken by a bear, 'A great man never takes into account the offenses committed against him. Indeed, he vows that at all costs he will not return evil with evil.' The story goes like this, There was a hunter being chased by a tiger and so he climbed into a big tree. It so happened that there was a bear perched upon one of the branches. Seeing this, the tiger said, 'This hunter is our common enemy. Therefore you should push him out of the tree so that I can eat him.'"

The bear replied, "This hunter has taken shelter of my home and so I will not do anything to harm him. To act in such a way would be most unrighteous."

After saying this, the bear went to sleep.

The tiger then told the hunter, "If you push the bear out of the tree so that I can eat him, I promise that I will not harm you."

Being swayed by the tiger's words, the hunter pushed the sleeping bear, but as he was falling, the bear managed to grab onto a branch and save himself. The tiger then said to the bear, "Because this hunter tried to kill you, you should retaliate by pushing him out of the tree."

And yet, even though the tiger appealed to the bear in this manner, again and again, he refused, saying, "A great person never takes into account the sins of one who has offended him. Instead, at all costs, he keeps his vow to never return evil for evil, because he knows that good conduct is the ornament of virtuous persons.”

Before departing, Hanuman asked Sitadevi if she had a message for Lord Rama. Sitadevi replied, "My only words are this, 'I long to see my dear husband, who is known to be always very affectionate toward His unalloyed devotees.'"

Hanuman said, "Rest assured that you will see Lord Rama and Laksmana this very day. Now, please grant me your permission so that I can return to Lord Rama without further delay.”

Hanuman went and related Sitadevi's message. After doing so, he urged Lord Rama to go and meet Sitadevi at once. "Because she has suffered so much and longs to see You, You should go to the asoka grove immediately," Hanuman pleaded.

Upon hearing this appeal, tears came to Lord Rama's eyes. With His eyes cast downward, Lord Rama ordered Vibhisana, "Have Sitadevi brought to Me, after having bathed, dressed, and decorated herself with celestial ornaments."

Vibhisana went to the asoka grove, and through the Raksasa women he made his presence known to Sitadevi. After very submissively approaching her, Vibhisana said, "Lord Rama would like to see you. First, please bathe and dress yourself in these celestial garments and ornaments. Then, mount upon the palanquin I have brought, for that is Lord Rama's desire."

Sitadevi said, "I want to see Lord Rama immediately. I do not want to bathe first.”

However, Vibhisana advised, "You had better do as Your husband has ordered, for that will bring you all auspiciousness."

Sitadevi went to bathe, and after dressing herself very nicely, she was placed upon the palanquin and brought before her husband. When Vibhisana came before Lord Rama, he saw that the Lord's head was bowed, as if He were absorbed in deep thought.

Vibhisana announced Sitadevi's arrival, and in response, Lord Rama asked that she be brought to Him at once. Hordes of monkeys had come there out of curiosity, just to get a glimpse of Sitadevi. Vibhisana and his four assistants began pushing them back, so that Sitadevi could approach Lord Rama privately. Because of this, there was a great commotion.

Due to His strong affection for His faithful servants, Lord Rama became annoyed to see this, however, and so He told Vibhisana, "Do not harass these monkeys. There is nothing wrong if a chaste woman is seen in public during a time of adversity or war, a svayamvara, a sacrifice, or a wedding. Please allow the monkeys to see Sita if they so desire."

Lord Rama ordered, "Let Sita get down from the palanquin and come to Me on foot."

As Vibhisana escorted Sitadevi, all the monkey chiefs could understand that Lord Rama was in a very stern and grave mood. They were very surprised that not only was Lord Rama making Sitadevi walk within public view, but that His demeanor was so severe. Sitadevi innocently approached Lord Rama with great shyness, as if she were shrinking into herself. Then, when Sitadevi saw the handsome face of her beloved husband, her miseries immediately vanished so that her face shone brightly like the full moon.

As Sitadevi continued to gaze upon Him with great love and affection, Lord Rama began to give vent to His pent-up inner feelings. Lord Rama's heart was tormented by fear of stain to the impeccable reputation of His dynasty. Because of this, He addressed Sitadevi in an angry tone of voice.

Lord Rama said, "I have fulfilled My vow to win you back and thus avenge Ravana's insult to My honor, but you must understand that My great endeavor to kill the king of the Raksasas was not actually for your sake. I did this just to vindicate My good name, and that of the Iksvaku dynasty."

"Truthfully, your appearance before Me is not at all pleasing. You are free to go wherever you like. No cultured man will accept a wife who has been embraced by another man, or who has lived in someone else's house. I am sure that no woman could have remained with Ravana for so long without having been enjoyed by Him. Ravana was obsessed by lust for you. How could he have controlled himself and refrained from enjoying you by force? By killing Ravana, I have regained My honor, But there is no need for Me to have any more attachment for you. You are now free to do as you like. Fix your mind upon Laksmana, Bharata, or anyone else whom you may choose."

While listening to this speech, Sitadevi bent her head low with shame. Having formerly heard only loving words from Lord Rama, His talk seemed like arrows piercing her heart, and thus she began to bitterly weep. Being in the presence of so many spectators, it was very difficult for Sitadevi to endure her husband's reproaches.

Finally, after wiping the tears form her eyes, Sitadevi replied in a faltering voice, "How can You dare speak about me in such an irresponsible manner? Never for a moment did I give up my chastity by body, mind, or words. My character is pure, and so You should not judge me as if I were an ordinary woman. Although I am called Janaki, the daughter of King Janaka, my birth was transcendental, for I appeared from within the earth."

"O Lord, if all along You had cruelly planned to reject me in this way, why didn't you inform me when Hanuman came here as a messenger? If I had known that You did not intend to take me back, I would have immediately given up my life, and thus avoided many months of unbearable suffering. You could have avoided this ghastly war that has taken the lives of countless Raksasas and vanaras. What was the need of demanding so much service from your allies? Î Lord, why are You acting like this? Does my pure devotion for You mean nothing?"

Sitadevi turned to Laksmana and said, "Please build a large fire for me to enter, for this is the only path that remains for one who has been rejected by her husband in public."

While suppressing His agitation, Laksmana looked at Lord Rama. When He saw that His elder brother approved of Sitadevi's words, He went to prepare the fire. In fact, Lord Rama looked so stern and intense that no one dared to even talk to Him, and so what to speak of try and pacify Him.

Thereafter, when the fire blazed brightly, Sitadevi first circumambulated Lord Rama. Then, after coming before the fire with folded hands, Sitadevi bowed down to the brahmanas and demigods.

She then offered this prayer to Agni, "O god of fire, because my heart has never turned away from my husband, please protect me. Although I have never been unfaithful to Him in thought, word or deed, He accuses me of being polluted. Therefore, O lord of fire, seer of all within the three worlds, I request you to become the witness of my purity."

After saying this, Sitadevi circumambulated the fire. Then, as a huge crowd looked on with wonder, she entered the flames with a fearless mind. Within the blazing fire, Sitadevi, who was adorned with dazzling gold ornaments, shone with a golden radiance. As soon as Sitadevi was within the flames, all the women present screamed with horror, and a loud cry of anguish arose from the assembled monkeys and Raksasas. Amidst all these sounds, Lord Rama appeared to become very thoughtful. At that time all the principal demigods hurriedly appeared before Him, riding upon their celestial vehicles.

As Lord Rama stood before them with folded hands, the demigods, headed by Lord Brahma and Lord Siva, said, "O Supreme Personality of Godhead, Lord Ramacandra, we are very pained to see how You are neglecting Your eternal consort, mother Sitadevi. You are the creator of the universe and the Lord of all the demigods. Why don't You recognize Your divinity, instead of rejecting Sitadevi, as if You were a common man?"

Lord Rama replied, "I consider myself to be an ordinary human being, the son of Maharaja Dasaratha, but if there is something more to be said, perhaps you, Lord Brahma, can disclose it.”

Lord Brahma said, "My dear Lord Rama, I will now reveal Your true identity. You are directly Lord Narayana, and thus You are identical with all the forms of visnu-tattva. You are a plenary expansion of Lord Sri Krsna, the original Supreme Personality of Godhead, and thus You are the cause of all causes. You are the universal form, the support of the cosmic manifestation, and all of the demigods are Your parts and parcels, or in other words, Your eternal servants. Sitadevi is none other than Laksmi, the supreme goddess of fortune. Both of you have appeared on the earth for the purpose of destroying Ravana. Now that this mission has been accomplished, You may return to Your transcendental abode in the spiritual sky after ruling the earth for as long as You desire."

As soon as Lord Brahma finished speaking, the fire-god, Agni, emerged from the flames, carrying Sitadevi in his arms. As Agni placed Sitadevi before Lord Rama, everyone was amazed to see how her body, bright red dress, ornaments, and hair showed absolutely no sign of being even slightly burnt.

In his capacity as one of the universal witnesses, Agni announced, "O Lord Rama, here is Your dear wife, Sitadevi. She is completely pure and devoid of even the least tinge of sin. Sitadevi was never the slightest bit unfaithful to You by word, thought or glance, and so what to speak of action."

"My dear Lord Rama, You should accept Sitadevi without reservation and give up Your harsh speech and behavior."

Lord Rama was very pleased to hear this testimony. As tears of joy fell from His eyes, He replied, "O Agni, it was necessary for Sitadevi to undergo this trial by fire, to convince the masses of people of her purity. If I had prevented Sitadevi from entering the fire, people would have criticized Me for accepting her without first proving her chastity. They would have concluded that I had taken her back because of being under the influence of lust to enjoy her."

"Actually, I knew about Sitadevi's purity, and I knew that Ravana could never have polluted her, for she is fully protected by the prowess of her righteousness. It was only to prove Sitadevi's chastity to the world that I appeared to neglect her. Factually, Sitadevi is not different from Me, for she is directly My internal potency, the hladini-sakti. Just as sunlight, being non-different from the sun, is therefore inseparable from it, so there is no possibility of My rejecting Sitadevi."

Actually, Lord Rama felt great transcendental bliss while being reunited with Sitadevi, for His pastimes were all manifestations of His internal potency, and had been enacted for the purpose of relishing spiritual relationships.

Lord Siva then addressed Lord Rama, saying, "My dear Lord, by slaying the incomparably powerful Ravana, You have performed a wonderful feat that will be glorified throughout the three worlds until the time of dissolution."

While pointing toward the sky, Lord Siva said, "My dear Lord Rama, look up and see how Your father is waiting, seated upon his celestial chariot. After having been delivered by Your mercy, he now resides in the planet of Indra, the king of heaven. Go quickly, along with Laksmana, and be reunited with Maharaja Dasaratha, for he has come here just to see You."

Lord Rama and Laksmana went and bowed down before Their father. Feeling extremely delighted, Maharaja Dasaratha took Lord Rama on his lap and said, "My residence in heaven does not give me any real pleasure. My dear Lord Rama, only now that I am able to see You do I feel happy. Kaikeyi's words, demanding Your exile, have always remained imprinted on my heart. Only now that Your period of exile has ended do I feel somewhat relieved. I yearn to see You return to Ayodhya, and witness Your installation as Emperor, after being reunited with Bharata. I can now understand that You are the Supreme Lord, Visnu, and that You had descended upon the earth for the purpose of vanquishing Ravana."

Lord Rama said, "My dear father, I also feel greatly relieved now that My period of exile is over and My mission has been accomplished, but still, there is one thing I wish you would grant Me. May you now withdraw the harsh words you had spoken at the time of My banishment, disowning mother Kaikeyi and Bharata."

Maharaja Dasaratha readily consented, saying, "Let it be so." He fondly embraced Laksmana and declared, "My dear son, because of the dedicated service You have rendered to Rama, I feel eternally indebted to You. You should know that Your elder brother is directly the Supreme Personality of Godhead, appearing in human form, for the welfare of the world. He is worshipful even by the greatest demigods, and so what to speak of ourselves."

Maharaja Dasaratha told Sitadevi, "Please do not bear any grudge against Rama for His testing your purity. You can rest assured that your spotless character and behavior will earn you a place in history as the most glorious woman the world has ever seen."

Having thus spoken, Maharaja Dasaratha remounted his celestial chariot and ascended to heaven. As Lord Rama stood before him with folded hands, Indra said, "My audience can never go in vain and so I would like You to accept a benediction from me."

Lord Rama was pleased to hear this. He requested, "O King of the celestials, kindly bring back to life all the monkey warriors who died in My service. In addition, let all the trees in the places where these great heroes dwell become filled with fruit, even when out of season."

Indra replied, "Although this boon is very difficult for even me to grant, I shall happily do so."

Immediately, all the monkeys that had died in the battle began to rise up from the ground. Because all their wounds were completely healed, it appeared to the vanaras as if they were awakening from a deep sleep. However, when they saw Lord Rama and all the demigods before them, the monkeys could understand that they had gotten back their lost lives, and so they felt supremely delighted.

Indra then ascended to heaven, followed by all the demigods. Lord Rama and the monkeys passed the night at that place. The next morning, Vibhisana came to see Lord Rama, along with numerous maidservants carrying all kinds of paraphernalia for His bath.

However, Lord Rama ordered, "My dear Vibhisana, summon all the monkeys, headed by Sugriva, and let them utilize this royal luxury. As long as I am separated from Bharata, who is practicing severe austerities on My behalf, such opulence does not appeal to Me. My only request is that you arrange for My speedy passage back to Ayodhya, for to travel there by foot would be an arduous journey."

Vibhisana replied, "I can enable You to reach Ayodhya this very day by making use of the Puspaka chariot, but I request that You, Sitadevi and Laksmana remain here for some time, along with the army of monkeys, so that I can royally entertain all of you before Your departure."

Lord Rama replied, "I certainly cannot refuse your hospitality, and yet, because My anxiety to meet Bharata, My mother and My step-mothers is so great, I beg that you allow Me to depart without delay."

Vibhisana quickly went and brought the Puspaka chariot. This wonderful vehicle had originally belonged to Kuvera, before it had been forcibly taken away by Ravana. The Puspaka chariot was built by Visvakarma, it was made mostly of gold, and it had seats made of vaidurya gems. This aerial chariot could travel anywhere, following the mental indications of its driver.

When Lord Rama and Laksmana saw the chariot awaiting Their commands, They were astonished. Before departing, Lord Rama requested Vibhisana to present gifts of gold and jewels to all the monkey soldiers.

After mounting the Puspaka chariot, along with Laksmana and Sitadevi, Lord Rama addressed those who surrounded Him, "There is no way I can repay all you monkey warriors for your heroic fighting on My behalf. Your unflinching devotional service will always serve as an inspiration for future devotees. Your glories will forever shine brightly. Now please return to Kiskindha and live there happily under Sugriva's leadership. My dear Vibhisana, you should accept the responsibility for ruling Lanka at once, because the citizens have become bereft of their king."

While standing before Lord Rama with folded hands, Sugriva and Vibhisana pleaded, "O Lord, please allow us to accompany You to Ayodhya. After seeing the coronation ceremonies, we will return home.”

Lord Rama replied, "There is nothing that would please Me more than to return to Ayodhya with all of My dear friends. Both of you can get in the Puspaka chariot, and let all the other monkey heroes and Raksasas come as well."

Finally, after all were comfortably seated, the Puspaka chariot rose majestically into the air. While the monkeys, bears and Raksasas were enjoying the flight, Lord Rama pointed out all the sights to Sitadevi.

Lord Rama said, "Just see the great battlefield where all the heroic Raksasas lay dead, having been killed just for your sake. There is Ravana, there is Kumbhakarna, there is Indrajit, and there is Prahasta. Over there is the bridge called Nalasetu, over which we crossed the ocean to Lanka. There, on the far shore, is Setubandha, where Lord Siva had appeared to Me, and where the construction of the bridge had begun. From this time on, Setubandha will be a very sacred place, capable of washing away all one's accumulated sinful reactions."

When Lord Rama pointed out Kiskindha, Sitadevi said, "I would be pleased if I could return to Ayodhya in the company of all the wives of the monkey chiefs."

Lord Rama granted Sitadevi's wish, and after halting the chariot, He instructed Sugriva and others to quickly go and bring their wives. When everyone was once again seated, the journey continued.

Lord Rama pointed out, "There is Mount Rsyasrnga, where I met Sugriva, and nearby, you can see the heavenly Lake Pampa, which is full of bluish lotus flowers. Further on, you can see the River Godavari, and on its shore, Agastya Rsi's asrama. My dear Sita, there is the spot where Ravana kidnapped you! There is Citrakuta, where Bharata came to meet Me. There is the River Yamuna, and there is the mighty Ganga, where King Guha's capital, Srngaverapura, can be seen."

In this way Sitadevi, Lord Rama and Laksmana remembered Their entire forest life in reverse order as They retraced their way back home to Ayodhya. Finally, the River Sarayu came into view, and then at last, the outskirts of Ayodhya.

Before entering Ayodhya, Lord Rama stopped at Bharadvaja Rsi's asrama, so that He could inquire about the welfare of His relatives before meeting them.

After heartily welcoming Lord Rama and receiving His obeisances in return, Bharadvaja said, "In Your absence Bharata has been living a life of severe austerities, wearing deerskin and tree bark and keeping matted hair. He has been ruling the kingdom as Your subordinate by keeping Your shoes upon the royal throne. Î Lord Rama, by my mystic power I know everything that has happened during Your exile. I am very pleased that You have removed the burden of the earth, and so I would like to award You any benediction You may desire."

Lord Rama happily replied, "Let all of the trees along the way to Ayodhya become full of fruit and flowers. Let streams of honey flow from these trees, exuding the fragrance of nectar."

As soon as these words were spoken, all the trees along the road to Ayodhya immediately became filled with sumptuous fruit. When they saw this miraculous transformation, thousands of monkeys quickly jumped from the Puspaka chariot and began feasting to their full satisfaction. Lord Rama was always thinking of how He could reward the monkeys for the selfless service they had rendered, and so He felt very happy to have received this opportunity to please them.

Turning to Hanuman, Lord Rama said, "I would like you to go and inform Guha of My arrival. After that, go to Nandigrama. I want you to describe to Bharata all the events relating to Sita's abduction and her subsequent recovery. Watch the expression on Bharata's face very carefully as He hears about My arrival. Then, report back to Me before we leave this place. If Bharata wants to rule the kingdom, whether it be due to attachment for position and its resultant power, or attachment to royal luxuries, or even because of Kaikeyi's urging, I am happy to allow Him to do so."

Hanuman took a human form and departed, traveling through the air. First he went and informed Guha that Lord Rama would come to meet him after spending the night at Bharadvaja Rsi's asrama. Then, upon his arrival at Nandigrama, Hanuman saw Bharata dressed in tree bark and having matted hair. Bharata had been living in a small cottage, subsisting only upon fruit and roots, and he appeared to be very miserable and emaciated.

Hanuman approached Bharata and announced, "I have come here as a messenger from Lord Rama. He inquires about your welfare, and He wants to inform you that He will return to Ayodhya very soon."

When Bharata heard these nectarean words, his face lit up with great delight. Having become exhilarated with transcendental emotion, he suddenly fainted to the ground. After coming to his senses, Bharata stood up and embraced Hanuman with great satisfaction. While bathing Hanuman with torrents of tears, Bharata said, "Because you have brought me this wonderful news, I will immediately reward you with 100,000 cows, a hundred villages and sixteen virgin girls to marry. Please sit down and tell me everything that happened during Lord Rama's exile."

Hanuman narrated everything, and while doing so, he revealed his identity. When he heard about Lord Rama's immanent return, Bharata exclaimed, "My long-cherished desire is finally going to be fulfilled!"

Bharata ordered Satrughna to make all the arrangements for Lord Rama's reception. Sumantra and the other ministers soon arrived at Nandigrama, riding upon elephants. Mother Kausalya, Sumitradevi and Kaikeyidevi came riding in palanquins. Engineers and work crews also arrived, to begin constructing a new road connecting Nandigrama with Ayodhya.

After all the arrangements were made, Bharata picked up Lord Rama's sandals, a white royal umbrella, and camaras. Accompanied by many brahmanas, He left His cottage amidst the blowing of conch shells and beating of drums, to wait for Lord Rama's arrival.

Meanwhile, because the news had spread like wildfire, practically the entire population of Ayodhya came to Nandigrama in the expectation of seeing Lord Rama. After some time, when there was still no sign of His arrival, Bharata told Hanuman, "I hope that you are not exhibiting your frivolous monkey nature by joking with me."

In response, Hanuman pointed out the clouds of dust in the distance that were being raised by the approaching monkeys. Just then, tumultuous roaring sounds became distinctly audible as well. When Hanuman sighted the Puspaka chariot in the distance, he shouted, "Here comes Sri Lord Rama!" A loud clamor arose as the restless crowd sighted the Puspaka chariot, appearing like the full moon in the sky.

As everyone got down from their horses, elephants and chariots, out of respect, Bharata began to worship Lord Rama from a distance. With folded hands, He recited many prayers to the Lord, and then He offered various articles. Finally, when Bharata could distinctly see Lord Rama, who was glowing magnificently while seated at the front of the Puspaka chariot, He bowed down with great reverence.

When the celestial airship landed, Bharata ran and climbed aboard to greet His elder brother. Lord Rama immediately got up from His seat, and after embracing Bharata with great affection, took Him upon His lap.

Afterwards, Bharata greeted Laksmana and Sitadevi. While embracing Sugriva, he said, "Although We are four, you are now just like Our fifth brother."

At this time, Lord Rama approached His mother, mother Kausalya, and lovingly clasped her feet. Then, one after another, He greeted mother Sumitra, Kaikeyi and muni Vasistha, as all the citizens came to welcome Him with folded hands.

Bharata approached Lord Rama, carrying His wooden shoes in His hands. As He carefully placed them on Lord Rama's lotus feet, Bharata said, "Here is the kingdom that I was overseeing in Your absence. By Your mercy, Ayodhya is flourishing and the treasury, storehouses and army have increased tenfold. My duty is now over and so I hereby relinquish everything to You."

After this, Lord Rama ordered the Puspaka chariot to return to its original owner, the god of wealth, Kuvera. That celestial vehicle ascended into the sky, heading toward the North.

When Lord Rama sat down at the lotus feet of His spiritual master, Vasistha, Bharata came and requested, "My dear elder brother, please install Yourself on the royal throne without further delay and resume a life of royal luxury."

Lord Rama gave His consent and so barbers were immediately summoned and His matted hair was shaved off. After bathing, Lord Rama dressed in a royal style, while the three mothers similarly dressed Sitadevi and the monkeys' wives. At Satrughna's command, Sumantra came to Lord Rama with a lavishly decorated chariot. Lord Rama graciously mounted it, Bharata picked up the reins, and Satrughna held the royal white umbrella. On either side of Lord Rama stood Laksmana and Vibhisana, waving a fan and a camara. From the sky, the demigods and celestial rishis glorified Him with carefully chosen words.

As Lord Rama proceeded toward Ayodhya, a huge procession followed Him. All the monkeys, appearing in human form, rode upon elephants. When Lord Rama entered His capital, He saw how all the citizens had come out of their houses and lined the streets to welcome Him. Men and women, the elderly and children, gazed upon Lord Rama as if they were getting back their long-lost lives.

While waving their cloths and jumping with excitement, the people shouted, "Our beloved prince has returned! All glories to Lord Rama, the maintainer of His devotees!”

Amidst the playing of musicians and the chanting of Vedic mantras by the brahmanas, Lord Rama reciprocated by glancing lovingly over His subjects. While approaching his father's palace, Lord Rama greeted His ministers and described to them the political alliances He had made with the monkeys and Vibhisana.

Lord Rama ordered that His palace be given for Sugriva's use, and so Bharata took the king of the monkeys by the hand and led him there. At Bharata's request, Jambavan, Hanuman, Gavaya and Rsabha brought water from the four seas, while five hundred other powerful monkeys brought water from five hundred sacred rivers. These vessels of water were placed before Vasistha.

Soon thereafter, the sage had Lord Rama seated, along with Sitadevi, upon a royal throne. With the assistance of Vamadeva, Jabali, Kasyapa, Katyayana, Suyajha, Gautama and Vijaya Rsis, Vasistha performed the abhiseka, and the first bathing was done by the brahmanas. Next, unmarried virgin girls got the chance to bathe Lord Rama, and then the ministers, leading warriors and vaisyas, one after another. After the final bathing, Vasistha had Lord Rama sprinkled with herbs by the four lokapalas and other chief demigods, who were witnessing the coronation from the sky.

When Lord Rama sat on the golden throne bedecked with valuable jewels, Vasistha came and placed the royal crown on His head and decorated His body with gold ornaments. This crown had been worn by all the kings of the Iksvaku dynasty and was made by Lord Brahma especially for the coronation of Vaivasvata Manu.

At Indra’s prompting, Vayu came and placed a garland made from one hundred golden lotus flowers around Lord Rama's neck. The god of air also presented a celestial necklace made of pearls and jewels. Satrughna held the umbrella over Lord Rama's head, while Sugriva and Vibhisana fanned Him from both sides.

At the completion of the ceremony, as the Gandharvas sang and the Apsaras danced in ecstasy, Lord Rama gave 100,000 cows in charity to the brahmanas, as well as 300 million gold coins and all varieties of precious jewels. Lord Rama gave a celestial necklace of gold and jewels to Sugriva, and He gave Angada a pair of bracelets that were adorned with diamonds and vaidurya stones. To Sitadevi, Lord Rama presented the necklace that had been given to Him by Vayu, as well as many lavishly decorated dresses.

Sitadevi had a very strong wish to give something to Hanuman as a token of her appreciation for all he had done for her. With this in mind, she unclasped the necklace Lord Rama had given her and looked at the Lord questioningly. Understanding her intention, Lord Rama requested Sitadevi to give the necklace to Hanuman and so she happily went and placed it around his neck.

All the monkey chiefs were given valuable clothing and ornaments, and in this way the coronation of Lord Rama came to a successful conclusion. Being greatly satisfied at heart, all the monkeys returned to their respective kingdoms, and Vibhisana departed for Lanka.

After everyone had left, Lord Rama said to Laksmana, "My dear brother, now that I have been installed upon the royal throne, I wish to install You as My successor."

And yet, despite being repeatedly addressed by Lord Rama in this way, Laksmana remained silent, because He refused to accept the proposal. Lord Rama could very well understand Laksmana's mind, and so at last He conferred the title upon Bharata.

Thereafter, Lord Rama ruled the earth from His capital, Ayodhya, for 11,000 years. During that period, Lord Rama performed numerous sacrifices, including one hundred asvamedha-yajnas. During the reign of Lord Rama, there were no widows to lament the loss of their husbands, there were no diseases, and there were no thieves. In fact, even wild animals gave up their natural enmity and did not kill one another. All the citizens were fully righteous. They considered Lord Rama their Lord and master and indeed, their very life and soul. Everyone lived for thousands of years and had many sons. All talk was centered around Lord Rama alone. Thus, the entire earth appeared as if it had been transformed into the kingdom of God, Vaikunthaloka.

Lava and Kusa concluded their narration by saying, "Anyone who daily listens to this transcendental history, known as Ramayana, will become completely freed from all sinful reactions. This sacred narration grants prowess, longevity and victory to those who subdue their passions and hear with faith."

"The reader of Ramayana, if a woman, will receive sons; if a king, will conquer the earth; if a traveler, will reach his destination; and if a sinful person, will be cleansed.
